Exact DG-categories and fully faithful triangulated inclusion functors
Leonid Positselski

Abstract
. We construct an “almost involution” assigning a new DG-category to a given one, and use this construction to recover, say, the abelian category of graded modules over the graded ring R ∗ R^{*} from the DG-category of DG-modules over a DG-ring ( R ∗ , d ) (R^{*},d) . This provides an appropriate technical background for the definition and discussion of abelian and exact DG-categories. In the setting of exact DG-categories, derived categories of the second kind are defined in the maximal natural generality. We develop the related abstract category-theoretic language and use it to formulate and prove several full-and-faithfulness theorems for triangulated functors induced by the inclusions of fully exact DG-subcategories. Such functors are fully faithful for derived categories of the second kind more often than for the conventional derived categories. Examples and applications range from the categories of complexes in abelian/exact categories to matrix factorization categories, and from curved DG-modules over curved DG-rings to quasi-coherent CDG-modules over quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebras over schemes.



Introduction

Abelian and exact categories (in the sense of Quillen) are popular
subjects of contemporary research, and indeed they are crucially
important concepts in homological algebra.
The same applies to differential graded categories.
Still it often goes unmentioned that, for many examples of
DG-categories appearing “in nature”, there is an abelian or exact
category structure lurking nearby.

In fact, there are usually
two
such exact/abelian categories
related to a DG-category.
For example, if
\mathsf{A}
is an abelian category, then the DG-category
of complexes in
\mathsf{A}
has two underlying abelian categories:
the abelian category of graded objects in
\mathsf{A}
(with homogeneous
maps of degree
0
as morphisms) and the abelian category of
complexes in
\mathsf{A}
(with closed morphisms of degree
0
between
the complexes playing the role of morphisms in the abelian category).
Defining an
abelian
or
exact DG-category
as
an abstract category-theoretic concept involves elaborating on
the connection between the two underlying abelian/exact categories.

The main construction of this paper assigns to a DG-category
\mathbf{A}

another DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
Iterating this procedure twice, for a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts
and cones, leads to a DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
and a fully faithful
DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
, which is
an equivalence whenever the original DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is
idempotent-complete and all twists of its objects by Maurer–Cartan
cochains in their complexes of endomorphisms exist in
\mathbf{A}
.
Otherwise, generally speaking, the passage from
\mathbf{A}
to

\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
adds all twists and some of their direct summands
(see Propositions
3.5

and
3.14
).

We use this construction for our purposes as a way to produce
a preadditive category that can serve the role of “the underlying
category of graded objects” of a DG-category.
The point is that, given a DG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*},d)
,
not every
graded
R^{*}
-module admits a differential making it a DG-module
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, not even at the cost of adding an extra direct summand
(Examples
3.2
–
3.3
).
Given the DG-category
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
, however, one can recover
the whole category of graded
R^{*}
-modules by attaching to every graded

R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
a contractible DG-module
G^{+}(M^{*})
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

freely generated by
M^{*}
, endowed with its canonical contracting
homotopy with zero square.
In our notation, this means that the abelian category
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}

of graded
R^{*}
-modules is equivalent to the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

of closed morphisms of degree
0
in the DG-category

\mathbf{A}^{\natural}=(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural}

(see Example
3.17
).

Given a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, there is a triple
(in fact, a doubly infinite shift-periodic sequence) of faithful,
conservative adjoint functors between the additive categories

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
of closed morphisms of degree
0

in
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
These functors are interpreted as “forgetting the differential”
(say, in a DG-module) and “freely generating a DG-module by
a graded module”.

A DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is said to be
abelian
if both
the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are abelian.
The natural functors mentioned above then preserve and reflect
short exact sequences.
An
exact structure
on a DG-category
\mathbf{E}
is the datum of
exact category structures on both the additive categories

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
such that the same functors
preserve and reflect admissible short exact sequences.
Complexes in abelian/exact categories, DG-modules or curved
DG-modules, and factorization categories provide natural examples
of abelian and exact DG-categories.
While an exact DG-category need not have twists, any abelian
DG-category has them; so for an abelian DG-category, the DG-functor

\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is a DG-equivalence.

The first part of this paper (Sections
1
–
4
) is a greatly expanded version
of
[
35
, Section 3.2 and Remark 3.5]
.
The theory whose key elements were very briefly hinted at
in
[
35
]
is developed with full details here.
After all the preparatory work is done and the theory is ready,
we eventually arrive, under mild assumptions, at a simple and
straightforward equivalent definition of an exact DG-category
(see Remark
4.18
).
But the theory serves to demonstrate that this simple definition is
the right one.

What can one do with an exact DG-category?
Following
[
35
, Remarks 3.5–3.7]
, we argue that
exact DG-categories provide the maximal natural generality setting
for the constructions of derived categories of the second kind,
such as the coderived, contraderived, and absolute derived categories.
The concept of derived categories of the second kind goes back
to
[
34
, Sections 2.1 and 4.1]

and
[
35
, Sections 3.3 and 4.2]
; we refer the reader
to
[
52
, Remark 9.2]
and
[
47
, Section 7]
for a historical
and philosophical discussion.

Let us
warn
the reader that in this paper we generally consider
the complexes of morphisms in DG-categories up to closed isomorphism
of complexes and
not up to quasi-isomorphism
, and accordingly
the DG-categories themselves are considered up to equivalence and

not up to quasi-equivalence
.
So we mostly deal with equivalences rather than quasi-equivalences of
DG-categories; likewise, “fully faithful DG-functors” are presumed
to induce termwise (closed) isomorphisms of the complexes of morphisms
rather than merely quasi-isomorphisms.
This is the natural point of view in the context of derived categories
of the second kind.

Similarly, by
finite direct sums
in a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
we
mean finite direct sums in the preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
of
closed morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
A DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is called
additive
if it has finite
direct sums in this sense (i. e., if the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is
additive).
This is a
stronger
condition than the existence of finite
direct sums in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The existence of finite direct sums in
\mathbf{A}
is also a
stronger

condition than the existence of finite direct sums in the category

\mathbf{A}^{0}
of arbitrary (not necessarily closed) morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
Likewise, by a
complex in

\mathbf{A}
we always mean a complex in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathsf{A})
; no notions such a homotopy complex in a DG-category
are considered in this paper.
See Section
1.3
for a discussion.

In the second part of the present paper
(Sections
5
–
9
), we formulate and prove some of the most
important general properties of derived categories of the second kind
using the concepts and tools developed in the first part of the paper.
In that, we follow the approaches of
[
35
, Sections 3.5–3.7
and 3.11]
and
[
18
, Sections 1.4–1.6]
.
The language of abelian and exact DG-categories unites the results
about

(1)

the coderived, contraderived, and absolute derived categories of
(complexes in) abelian and exact categories, and

(2)

the coderived, contraderived, and absolute derived categories of
DG-modules over DG-rings (and curved DG-modules over curved
DG-rings)

in a common framework, which includes also (matrix) factorization
categories, quasi-coherent CDG-modules over quasi-coherent
CDG-quasi-algebras over schemes, etc.
In particular, in Section
5
we reproduce
the results of
[
35
, Sections 3.5–3.7]
about graded-injective
and graded-projective resolutions in the context of exact
DG-categories, as promised in
[
35
, Remarks 3.5–3.7]
.

Replacing an abelian or exact category with its (co)resolving
subcategory with respect to which all the objects of the ambient
category have finite (co)resolution dimension generally leaves
the derived category unchanged.
This applies both to the conventional derived categories (as defined
for exact categories in
[
29
]
), including the conventional
unbounded derived categories, as explained
in
[
57
, Proposition 5.14]
or
[
38
, Proposition A.5.8]
,
and to derived categories of the second kind.
For derived categories of the second kind, we establish this
property in the generality of exact DG-categories (or more
precisely, exact DG-pairs) in Section
6
,
using the technique going back to
[
34
, Section 7.2.2]
,

[
33
, Section 3.2]
, and
[
18
, Section 1.4]
(it is also
sketched in
[
38
, Proposition A.5.8]
).
Let us emphasize that checking the essential surjectivity of such
triangulated functors is relatively easy; it is proving
the full-and-faithfulness that requires the bulk of the work.
See Theorem
6.6
.

The nature of the construction of the triangulated Verdier quotient
category is such that inclusions of full subcategories of abelian
or exact categories need not induce fully faithful functors between
their derived categories, generally speaking.
Proving that, under various specific assumptions, the inclusion of
a full subcategory into an abelian, exact, or homotopy category remains
fully faithful after the passage to the derived categories is
a nontrivial task.
The results of the paper
[
48
]
, where the inclusions of
the full subcategories of Noetherian or coherent objects into
the ambient abelian categories of infinitely generated objects are
considered, illustrate the observation that such functors between
derived categories of the second kind tend to be fully faithful
more often than for the conventional unbounded derived categories.
Another (and more obvious) such illustration is provided by
the inclusions of the full subcategories of projective or injective
objects into an abelian or exact category.

In this paper, we deal with derived categories of the second kind.
So we leave aside the results about fully faithful inclusions of
conventional unbounded derived categories provable by the method
worked out in
[
40
, Theorems 1.3 and 2.9, Corollaries 1.4
and 2.10]
,
[
42
, Theorem 6.4 and Proposition 6.5]
,
and
[
44
, Proposition 6.5]
.
Instead, using the technique of
[
18
, Section 1.5]

and
[
37
, Theorem 4.2.1]
and following the approach
of
[
25
, Section 12]
and
[
38
, Section A.2]
, we consider
what we call
self-resolving
(or
self-coresolving
)
subcategories in exact categories.
We work with exact DG-pairs
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
; here
\mathbf{E}
is an exact
DG-category playing the role of the DG-category of (curved)
DG-modules, while the exact category
\mathsf{K}
plays the role of
the category of graded modules over the underlying graded ring.

In the context of exact DG-categories or exact DG-pairs, we show
that the inclusion of a self-resolving subcategory induces a fully
faithful functor between the absolute derived and the contraderived
categories, while the inclusion of a self-coresolving subcategory
induces fully faithful functors between the absolute derived and
coderived categories.
In the case when the exact subcategory is actually resolving or
coresolving, it follows that the induced triangulated functor is
a triangulated equivalence.
This is the material of our Section
7
.
The particular case of its results described
in
[
38
, Proposition A.3.1(b)]
has already found its uses in
the context of so-called pseudo-coderived and pseudo-contraderived
categories
[
50
, Section 4]
,
[
43
, Section 1]
.
See Theorems
7.9

and
7.11
for the full
generality.

It is a well-known fact that the theory of unbounded derived
categories becomes simpler under the assumption of finite homological
dimension of the abelian/exact category.
The same applies to derived categories of the second kind, but
there is a caveat pertaining to DG-modules.
Let us explain the situation, starting with the simpler case of
complexes in exact categories.

It is obvious from the definitions that any absolutely acyclic complex
in an exact category is acyclic.
If the exact category has exact (co)product functors, then any
co/contraacyclic complex is acyclic as well.
By the definition, any absolutely acyclic complex is both coacyclic
and contraacyclic.
So the coderived and contraderived categories are “sandwiched in
between” the absolute derived and the conventional derived category,
in the sense of the existence of natural triangulated Verder
quotient functors acting from the absolute derived category to
the co/contraderived category and then to the conventional derived
category.
By
[
34
, Remark 2.1]
, for an exact category of finite
homological dimension, all acyclic complexes are absolutely acyclic;
so the absolute derived category coincides with the conventional
derived category.
Hence both of them coincide with the co/contraderived category if
the exact category has exact (co)product functors.

Using
[
35
, Theorems 3.4.1(d) and 3.6]
, one can extend
these results to nonpositively cohomologically graded DG-rings
with the underlying graded ring having finite global dimension;
and
[
35
, Theorems 3.4.2(d) and 3.6]
imply the similar
results for connected, simply connected nonnegatively cohomologically
graded DG-rings with the underlying graded ring of finite global
dimension (see
[
47
, Theorem 7.8(b)]
).
There are other sets of assumptions under which the absolute derived
category of DG-modules over a DG-ring coincides with
the conventional derived category; e.g., this holds for cofibrant
DG-algebras over a field
[
35
, Theorem 9.4]
.
But this is
not
true for nonnegatively cohomologically
graded DG-rings with the underlying graded ring having finite global
dimension in general (see the references in
[
35
, last paragraph
of Section 3.6]
for a detailed discussion).

Once again, in this paper we only deal with derived categories of
the second kind.
For an exact DG-pair
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
such that the exact category
\mathsf{K}

has finite homological dimension, we show that the thick subcategory
of absolutely acyclic objects in the homotopy category of
\mathbf{E}
is

strongly generated
(in the sense of
[
13
]
) by
the totalizations of short exact sequences (see
Proposition
8.8
).
It follows that the absolute derived category of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with
the coderived and/or contraderived category whenever
\mathsf{K}
has finite
homological dimension and the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact
(co)product functors.
These assertions are easy to prove under the assumption of enough
projective or injective objects in
\mathsf{K}
, but the general case is quite
involved.
This is the material of Section
8
, where
we spell out the details of the argument whose ideas go back
to
[
18
, proof of Theorem 1.6]
.
See Theorem
8.9
.

In the last Section
9
, we mostly deal with
abelian DG-categories.
We consider Grothendieck abelian DG-categories, locally finitely
presentable DG-categories, locally Noetherian and locally coherent
DG-categories.
The main results concern compact generators in the coderived categories
of abelian DG-categories from the latter two classes.
For any locally Noetherian DG-category
\mathbf{A}
, we denote by

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}\subset\mathbf{A}
the full abelian DG-subcategory of finitely
generated (Noetherian) objects in
\mathbf{A}
, and show that the absolute
derived category of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}
is a full triangulated subcategory
in the coderived category of
\mathbf{A}
, consisting of compact objects and
generating the whole coderived category.
The proof of this result, going back to D. Arinkin and originally
recorded, with his kind permission, in
[
35
, Theorem 3.11.2]

in the context of CDG-modules over CDG-rings, is reproduced in
the much more general context of locally Noetherian DG-categories
in this paper.
See Theorem
9.23
.

For locally coherent DG-categories, we obtain a similar compact
generation result under additional assumptions of what we call
“finiteness of fp-dimensions” of the two locally coherent
abelian categories associated with a locally coherent DG-category.
The coderived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})
of a locally coherent
(Grothendieck abelian) DG-category
\mathbf{A}
contains the absolute
derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}})
of its full abelian
DG-subcategory of finitely presentable (coherent) objects
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}}

as a full triangulated subcategory consisting of compact objects.
Under the additional assumptions mentioned above, the full subcategory

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}})
generates the coderived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})
.
This is the result of
Theorem
9.39
.

As a corollary, we obtain the following result for CDG-modules
over a CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
.
Assume that the graded ring
R^{*}
is graded left coherent and
there is an integer
n
such that all the homogeneous left ideals
in
R^{*}
have less than
\aleph_{n}
generators.
Then the coderived category of left CDG-modules

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
coincides with the homotopy category of
graded-injective left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(known as
the “coderived category in the sense of Becker”
[
7
,
52
]
),
is compactly generated and, up to direct summands, the absolute
derived category of finitely presentable CDG-modules

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}_{\mathbf{fp}})
is the full subcategory of
compact objects in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
.
This is our
Corollary
9.42
.

Other than proving that, under various restrictive assumptions on
an abelian or exact DG-category, its coderived or contraderived
category in our sense agrees with the one in the sense of Becker, we
do
not
consider specifically co/contraderived categories in
the sense of Becker in this paper.
In particular, no model structures are mentioned in this paper.
This material is relegated to a separate paper
[
53
]
, joint
with J. Št​’ovíček, where we purport to show that the theory
of Becker’s derived categories of the second kind, as developed for
CDG-modules in
[
7
]
and for abelian categories in
[
52
]
,
finds its maximal natural generality in the context of locally
presentable abelian DG-categories.
More precisely, it seems that the natural generality for Becker’s
coderived categories is that of Grothendieck abelian DG-categories,
while for Becker’s contraderived categories the natural context is
that of locally presentable abelian DG-categories with enough
projective objects.
In particular, the finite fp-projective dimension and

\aleph_{n}
-Noetherianity assumptions in the above-mentioned results
in the (locally) coherent case can be dropped if one is willing to
consider Becker’s coderived categories, as we show in
[
53
]
.

1.
Graded Categories and DG-Categories

1.1.
Grading group datum

We will work with complexes (graded abelian groups, etc.) whose
components are indexed by a fixed abelian group
\Gamma
.
A symmetric bilinear form
\sigma\colon\Gamma\times\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2

and an element
\boldsymbol{1}\in\Gamma
are presumed to be chosen,
and the equation
\sigma(\boldsymbol{1},\boldsymbol{1})=1\bmod 2
is
imposed.
The differentials on complexes will raise the degree
by
\boldsymbol{1}
, and the sign
(-1)^{\sigma(a,b)}
will appear
when a factor of degree
a\in\Gamma
and a factor of
degree
b\in\Gamma
are moved around each other in a formula.

The most standard example is
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
,
\boldsymbol{1}=1
, and

\sigma(a,b)=ab\bmod 2
.
Alternatively, taking
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\boldsymbol{1}=1\bmod 2
, and

\sigma(a,b)=ab
means considering
2
-periodic complexes.
When working with complexes of vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
the form
\sigma
is not needed, so one can even put
\Gamma=0

and consider
1
-periodic complexes.

The notation related to the grading group will be generally
suppressed in the sequel: we will write
1
instead of
\boldsymbol{1}
,

n
instead of
n\cdot\boldsymbol{1}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
ab
instead of

\sigma(a,b)
,
a
instead of
\sigma(\boldsymbol{1},a)
, etc.
The meaning will be clear from the context.
The abelian group homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow\Gamma
taking
1
to

\boldsymbol{1}
will be presumed when constructing the total complexes
of bicomplexes graded by
\Gamma
along one coordinate and by
the integers along the other one.
The reader is referred to
[
33
, Section 1.1]
for a more detailed
discussion.

1.2.
Graded categories

A
\Gamma
-graded category
is a category enriched in

\Gamma
-graded abelian groups.
In other words, a
\Gamma
-graded category
\mathcal{A}
assigns to every
pair of objects
X
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}
a
\Gamma
-graded abelian group
of morphisms
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{i\in\Gamma}\Hom^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)
.
To every triple of objects
X
,
Y
,
Z\in\mathcal{A}
, a morphism of

\Gamma
-graded abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(Y,Z)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Z)
, called the composition of
morphisms, is assigned.
The composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
must be associative as usual.
The identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{X}
is an element of the group

\Hom^{0}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,X)
.

A
preadditive category
is a category enriched in abelian
groups.
For any
\Gamma
-graded category
\mathcal{A}
, its underlying preadditive
category
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is defined by the rules that the objects of
\mathcal{A}^{0}

are the same as the objects of
\mathcal{A}
and
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{0}}(X,Y)=\Hom^{0}_{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)
for all objects
X
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}
(with
the composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}^{0}
defined in the obvious way
in terms of the composition of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
).
A preadditive category is said to be
additive
if the coproduct
of any finite collection of objects exists, or equivalently,
the product of any finite collection of objects exists.
A
\Gamma
-graded category is said to be
additive
if its
underlying preadditive category is additive.

A (covariant)
graded functor

F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
between

\Gamma
-graded categories
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is a rule assigning to
every object
X\in\mathcal{A}
an object
F(X)\in\mathcal{B}
and to every pair
of objects
X
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}
a morphism of
\Gamma
-graded abelian
groups
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(F(X),F(Y))
in a way
compatible with the compositions and the identity morphisms.
A graded functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
induces an additive functor
between the underlying preadditive categories
F^{0}\colon\mathcal{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{0}
.

A graded functor
F\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is said to be
fully
faithful
if it induces isomorphisms of graded abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Y)\simeq\Hom_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(F(X),F(Y))
for all objects

X
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}
.
A graded functor
F
is said to be an
equivalence of graded
categories
if
F
is fully faithful and
F^{0}\colon\mathcal{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}^{0}

is an essentially surjective additive functor.

Given two objects
X
and
Y
in a
\Gamma
-graded category
\mathcal{A}

and an element
i\in\Gamma
, one says that
Y
is a
shift
of

X
by
[i]
and writes
Y=X[i]
if morphisms
f\in\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{-i}(X,Y)

and
g\in\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(Y,X)
are given such that
fg=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
and

gf=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
Clearly, the shift
X[i]
of a given object
X\in\mathcal{A}
by a given
degree
i\in\Gamma
is defined uniquely up to a unique isomorphism of
degree
0
.
If all shifts exist in
\mathcal{A}
, then they are well-defined as graded
functors (in fact, autoequivalences)
[i]\colon\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}
.
The induced functor between the underlying preadditive categories
will be denoted also by
[i]\colon\mathcal{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{0}
.

A graded category with shifts is uniquely determined by its
underlying preadditive category with the shift functors acting on it.
The graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Y)
is recovered by the rule

\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(X,Y)=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}(X,Y[i])=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}^{0}}(X,Y[i])
for
all objects
X
,
Y\in\mathcal{A}
and degrees
i\in\Gamma
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a graded category.
An object
X\in\mathcal{A}
is said to be the
coproduct
of a family
of objects
X_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
if a functorial isomorphism of graded
abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X,Z)\simeq\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(X_{\alpha},Z)

is given for all the objects
Z\in\mathcal{A}
(where
\prod
denotes
the product of graded abelian groups).
Similarly, an object
Y\in\mathcal{A}
is said to be the
product
of
a family of objects
Y_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
if a functorial isomorphism of
graded abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(Z,Y)\simeq\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(Z,Y_{\alpha})
is given for all the objects
Z\in\mathcal{A}
.
Here one requires functoriality with respect to all morphisms in
\mathcal{A}

(of all the degrees).
If
\mathcal{A}
is a graded category with shifts, then an object
X

is the coproduct of the objects
X_{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{A}
if and only if it
is their coproduct in the category
\mathcal{A}^{0}
, and similarly an object
Y

is the product of the objects
Y_{\alpha}
in
\mathcal{A}
if and only if it is
their product in
\mathcal{A}^{0}
.

An additive category
\mathsf{A}
is said to be
idempotent-complete

if for every object
X\in\mathsf{A}
and every morphism
e\colon X\longrightarrow X

such that
e^{2}=e
there exist two objects
Y
,
Z\in\mathsf{A}
and
an isomorphism
X\simeq Y\oplus Z
for which the morphism
e
is
equal to the composition of the direct summand projection
X\longrightarrow Y

with the direct summand inclusion
Y\longrightarrow X
.
An additive graded category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be

idempotent-complete
if the additive category
\mathcal{A}^{0}
is
idempotent-complete.

1.3.
DG-categories

This section is a brief reminder of the material
of
[
35
, Section 1.2]
, intended mainly to fix the terminology
and notation.

A
DG-category
is a category enriched in (
\Gamma
-graded)
complexes.
In other words, a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
assigns to every pair of
objects
X
,
Y\in\mathbf{A}
a complex of abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
.
To every triple of objects
X
,
Y
,
Z\in\mathbf{A}
, a morphism of
complexes of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y,Z)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Z)
, called the composition
of morphisms, is assigned.
The composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
must be strictly associative,
i. e., associative on the level of cochains.
The identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{X}
is a cocycle in
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(X,X)
.
Generally, a
closed morphism of degree
i
between objects

X
and
Y\in\mathbf{A}
is a cocycle in
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i}(X,Y)
.

To every DG-category
\mathbf{A}
one can assign a preadditive category

\mathbf{A}^{0}
and a graded category
\mathbf{A}^{*}
.
The objects of
\mathbf{A}^{0}
and
\mathbf{A}^{*}
are the same as the objects of

\mathbf{A}
, and the morphisms are defined by the rules

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{0}}(X,Y)=\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(X,Y)
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{*}}^{i}(X,Y)=\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i}(X,Y)
for all objects
X
,
Y\in\mathsf{A}
.

Furthermore, to any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
one can assign a graded
category
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})
and a preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The objects of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})
are the same as the objects of
\mathbf{A}
,
and the morphisms are defined by the rule that

\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})}^{*}(X,Y)
is the graded group of all cocycles in
the complex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
, i. e., closed morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
The preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is defined as

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})=(\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}))^{0}
, so
\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,Y)
is
the group of cocycles in the term
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(X,Y)
of
the complex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
.

Similarly, to any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
one can assign a graded
category
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})
and a preadditive category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The objects of
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})
are the same as the objects of
\mathbf{A}
,
and the morphisms are defined by the rule that

\Hom_{\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})}^{*}(X,Y)=H^{*}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
is the graded group of
cohomology of the complex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
.
The preadditive category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is defined as

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})=(\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A}))^{0}
, so
\Hom_{\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,Y)=H^{0}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)
.

By a
direct sum
of a finite set of objects in a DG-category

\mathbf{A}
we mean their direct sum in the preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
So an object
Z\in\mathbf{A}
is a direct sum of two objects
X
,
Y\in\mathbf{A}

if closed morphisms of degree zero
\iota_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow Z
,

\pi_{X}\colon Z\longrightarrow X
,
\iota_{Y}\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
, and
\pi_{Y}\colon Z\longrightarrow Y
are given, satisfying the usual equations for morphisms defining
a direct sum (biproduct) of two objects in an additive category.
This is a
stronger
condition than the object
Z
being
a direct sum of
X
and
Y
in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathsf{A})
,
in that the equations need to be satisfied in
\mathbf{A}
and not only
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The object
Z
being a direct sum of
X
and
Y
in
\mathbf{A}
is also
a
stronger
condition than
Z
being a direct sum of
X
and
Y

in
\mathbf{A}^{0}
, in that the structure morphisms
\iota_{X}
,
\pi_{X}
,

\iota_{Y}
, and
\pi_{Y}
need to be closed.

A DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is said to be
additive
if the preadditive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is additive, i. e., if finite direct sums exist
in
\mathbf{A}
.
In this case, the preadditive categories
\mathbf{A}^{0}
and
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

are additive as well.
An additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is said to be
idempotent-complete

if the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is idempotent-complete.

Let
X
be an object of a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
.
An element
a\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X)
is said to be a
Maurer–Cartan
cochain
(or to
satisfy the Maurer–Cartan equation
) if

d(a)+a^{2}=0
in
\Hom^{2}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X)
.
An object
Y\in\mathbf{A}
is called the
twist
of
X
by
a Maurer–Cartan cochain
a\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X)
(denoted
Y=X(a))
if
morphisms
f\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Y)
and
g\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(Y,X)
are given
such that
fg=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
,
gf=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and
d(f)=fa
.
Then one has
d(g)=-ag
, hence
X=Y(-fag)
.

Conversely, let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathbf{A}
, and

f\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Y)
and
g\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(Y,X)
be two morphisms
such that
fg=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
and
gf=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
Put
a=gd(f)\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X)
.
Then
a
is a Maurer–Cartan cochain and
Y=X(a)
.
The twist
X(a)
of a given object
X
by a given Maurer–Cartan
cochain
a
, if it exists, is defined uniquely up to a unique
closed isomorphism of degree
0
.

Given an object
X\in\mathbf{A}
and a degree
i\in\Gamma
, one says that
an object
Y\in\mathbf{A}
is a
shift
of
X
by
[i]
(denoted by

Y=X[i]
) if morphisms
f\in\Hom^{-i}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,Y)
and

g\in\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}}(Y,X)
are given such that
fg=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
,
gf=\mathrm{id}_{X}
,
and
d(f)=0
(equivalently,
d(g)=0
).
Clearly, the shift
X[i]
of a given object
X
by a given degree
i
,
if it exists, is defined uniquely up to a unique closed isomorphism
of degree
0
.
Any shift in a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is also a shift in the graded
categories
\mathbf{A}^{*}
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})
, and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})
.

An object
X\in\mathbf{A}
is said to be the
coproduct
of a family
of objects
X_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{A}
if an isomorphism of complexes of
abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Z)\simeq\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X_{\alpha},Z)
is specified for all the objects
Z\in\mathbf{A}
, in a way
functorial with respect to all morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
Equivalently, one can say that
X=\coprod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
in
\mathbf{A}

if a closed morphism
X_{\alpha}\longrightarrow X
of degree
0
is given for
every index
\alpha
making
X
the coproduct of the objects

X_{\alpha}
in the graded category
\mathbf{A}^{*}
.
The coproduct of a family of objects in
\mathbf{A}
, if it exists, is
defined uniquely up to a unique closed isomorphism of degree
0
.
Any coproduct in a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is also a coproduct in
the graded categories
\mathbf{A}^{*}
,
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})
, and
\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})
.

Dually, an object
Y\in\mathbf{A}
is said to be the
product
of
a family of objects
Y_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{A}
if an isomorphism of complexes
of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,Y)\simeq\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,Y_{\alpha})
is specified for all the objects
Z\in\mathbf{A}
, in a way
functorial with respect to all morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
Equivalently, one can say that
Y=\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
in
\mathbf{A}

if a closed morphism
Y\longrightarrow Y_{\alpha}
of degree
0
is given for
every index
\alpha
making
Y
the product of the objects

Y_{\alpha}
in the graded category
\mathbf{A}^{*}
.

Finite products in
\mathbf{A}
coincide with the finite coproducts,
and with what we defined above as finite direct sums in
\mathbf{A}
;
they exist if and only if the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is additive.
So finite (co)products in
\mathbf{A}
are the same thing as finite
(co)products in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, essentially because finite direct sums
can be described in terms of structure morphisms and equations on
them, as mentioned above.
However, one needs to make additional assumptions about
\mathbf{A}
in order
to prove that any infinite coproduct in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is a coproduct
in
\mathbf{A}
; see Lemma
9.2
below.

An object
C\in\mathbf{A}
is said to be the
cone
of a closed
morphism
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
if an isomorphism
between the complex of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,C)
and
the cone of the morphism of complexes
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,f)\colon\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,X)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,Y)
is specified for all objects

Z\in\mathbf{A}
, in a way functorial with respect to all morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
Equivalently,
C
is the cone of
f
if an isomorphism between
the complex of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(C[-1],Z)
and the cone
of the morphism of complexes
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(f,Z)\colon\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y,Z)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Z)
is specified for all objects
Z\in\mathbf{A}
,
in a way functorial with respect to all morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
The cone of a closed morphism in
\mathbf{A}
, if it exists, is defined
uniquely up to a unique closed isomorphism of degree
0
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a closed morphism of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}

and
C=\cone(f)
be its cone.
Then there are natural closed morphisms
Y\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow X[1]

of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
.
The short sequence
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow X[1]\longrightarrow 0

is split exact in the preadditive category
\mathbf{A}^{0}
.
Conversely, any short sequence
0\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 0
of closed morphisms of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
which is
split exact in
\mathbf{A}^{0}
arises from a closed morphism

f\colon W[-1]\longrightarrow U
of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
in this way.

In an additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts, the cone
C=\cone f

of a closed morphism
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of degree
0
can be constructed
as a twist
(Y\oplus X[1])(a_{f})
of the direct sum
Y\oplus X[1]
by
a suitable Maurer–Cartan cochain
a_{f}
produced from
f
.
The Maurer–Cartan cochain
a_{f}
has an additional property that

d(a_{f})=0=a_{f}^{2}
.
Thus any additive DG-category with shifts and twists has cones.
Conversely, any DG-category with shifts, cones, and a zero object
is additive.

The notion of an additive DG-category with shifts and
twists is closely related to the concepts of a “twisted complex”
and the “convolution of a twisted complex” going back to
the paper
[
12
, Section 1]
, while the notion of an additive
DG-category with shifts and cones corresponds to the discussion of
“one-sided twisted complexes” in
[
12
, Section 4]

(another relevant term is a “pretriangulated DG-category”).

By a
complex
in a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
we will mean a sequence
of objects and morphisms

\dotsb\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX^{-1}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX^{0}\longrightarrow X^{1}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX^{2}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\dotsb

in
\mathbf{A}
, indexed by the integers
n\in\mathbb{Z}
or elements of the grading
group
n\in\Gamma
.
The differentials
X^{n}\longrightarrow X^{n+1}
are presumed to be closed
morphisms of degree
0
, and their compositions
X^{n-1}\longrightarrow X^{n}\longrightarrow X^{n+1}
must vanish as morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
So a complex in
\mathbf{A}
is the same thing as a complex in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

The
coproduct totalization

\Tot^{\sqcup}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of a complex

X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathbf{A}
is an object
Y\in\mathbf{A}
such that an isomorphism between
the complex of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y,Z)
and the total complex
of the bicomplex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},Z)
, constructed by taking
infinite products of abelian groups along the diagonals, is specified
for all objects
Z\in\mathbf{A}
, in a way functorial with respect to all
morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
In a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with twists, shifts, and coproducts,
the coproduct totalization
\Tot^{\sqcup}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
can be constructed as
a twist of the coproduct
\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X^{n}[-n]
or

\coprod_{n\in\Gamma}X^{n}[-n]
by a Maurer–Cardan cochain produced
from the differentials of the complex
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
The coproduct totalization of a complex in
\mathbf{A}
, if it exists, is
defined uniquely up to a unique closed isomorphism of degree
0
.

Dually, the
product totalization

\Tot^{\sqcap}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of
a complex
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathbf{A}
is an object
Y\in\mathbf{A}
such that
an isomorphism between the complex of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,Y)

and the total complex of the bicomplex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Z,X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
,
constructed by taking infinite products of abelian groups along
the diagonals, is specified for all objects
Z\in\mathbf{A}
, in a way
functorial with respect to all morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.
In a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with twists, shifts, and products, the product
totalization
\Tot^{\sqcap}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
can be constructed as a twist of
the product
\prod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}X^{n}[-n]
or
\prod_{n\in\Gamma}X^{n}[-n]

by a Maurer–Cardan cochain produced from the differentials of
the complex
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
For a bounded complex
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathbf{A}
, the product and coproduct
totalizations agree, and can be constructed as an iterated cone
(with a possible shift).
The cone of a closed morphism of degree
0
is the totalization of
the related two-term complex in
\mathbf{A}
.

A (covariant)
DG-functor

F\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}
between two
DG-categories
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
is a rule assigning to every object

X\in\mathbf{A}
an object
F(X)\in\mathbf{B}
and to every pair of objects

X
,
Y\in\mathbf{A}
a morphism of complexes of abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(F(X),F(Y))
in a way compatible
with the compositions and identity morphisms.
Any DG-functor preserves finite direct sums, shifts, twists,
and cones (in other words, one can say that these operations are
examples of “absolute weighted colimits” in DG-categories,
in the sense of
[
32
, Section 5]
).

A DG-functor
F\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}
is said to be
fully faithful

if the morphism of complexes of abelian groups
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(F(X),F(Y))
is a (termwise) isomorphism for all
the objects
X
,
Y\in\mathbf{A}
.
A fully faithful DG-functor is said to be an
equivalence of
DG-categories
if for any object
Z\in\mathbf{B}
there exists an object

X\in\mathbf{A}
together with a closed isomorphism
F(X)\simeq Z
of
degree
0
in
\mathbf{B}
.
A
full DG-subcategory

\mathbf{A}\subset\mathbf{B}
is a subclass of
objects in
\mathbf{B}
endowed with the induced DG-category structure
(so that the inclusion
\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}
is a fully faithful
DG-functor).
A full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{A}\subset\mathbf{B}
is said to be
closed
under direct summands
if the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is
closed under direct summands in the preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
.

Any DG-functor
F\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}
induces graded functors

F^{*}\colon\mathbf{A}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{*}
,
\mathcal{Z}(F)\colon\allowbreak\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{B})
, and
\mathcal{H}(F)\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{B})
.
Hence additive functors
F^{0}\colon\mathbf{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{0}
,
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(F)\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
, and

\mathsf{H}^{0}(F)\colon\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
are also induced.
A DG-functor
F\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}
is said to be
a
quasi-equivalence
if the induced graded functor

\mathcal{H}(F)\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{B})
is an equivalence of graded
categories.

For any additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones,
the degree-zero cohomology category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
with its induced
shift functor
X\longmapsto X[1]
has a natural structure of
a triangulated category.
For additive DG-categories
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
with shifts and cones,
the functor
\mathsf{H}^{0}(F)
induced by any DG-functor
F\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}

is triangulated.

2.
Thematic Examples

2.1.
Complexes in an additive category

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a preadditive category.
The preadditive category
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
of graded objects in
\mathsf{A}
(and
homogeneous morphisms of degree
0
between them) can be simply defined
as the Cartesian product
\mathsf{A}^{\Gamma}
of
\Gamma
copies of
\mathsf{A}
.
Explicitly, an object
X^{*}\in\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
is a collection of objects

X^{*}=(X^{i}\in\mathsf{A})_{i\in\Gamma}
; a morphism
f_{*}\colon X^{*}\longrightarrow Y^{*}
in

\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
is a collection of morphisms

(f_{i}\colon X^{i}\to Y^{i})_{i\in\Gamma}
.
The addition of morphisms, the composition of morphisms, and
the identity morphisms in
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
are defined in the obvious way.

The graded category
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
of graded objects in
\mathsf{A}
(and
homogeneous morphisms of various degrees
n\in\Gamma
between them)
has the same objects as the category
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
.
For any objects
X^{*}
and
Y^{*}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
, the graded abelian group

\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{*}(X^{*},Y^{*})
is defined by the rule

\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{n}(X^{*},Y^{*})=\Hom_{\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},Y^{*}[n])
, where

Y^{*}[n]^{i}=Y^{n+i}
for all
n
,
i\in\Gamma
.
So
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
is a
\Gamma
-graded category with shifts, and

\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})=(\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A}))^{0}
.

The categories
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
are additive whenever
the preadditive category
\mathsf{A}
is.
Assuming
\mathsf{A}
is additive, the categories
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})

are idempotent-complete whenever the category
\mathsf{A}
is.
The categories
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
have infinite coproducts
or products whenever the category
\mathsf{A}
has.

A
complex

X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathsf{A}
is a
\Gamma
-graded object
endowed with a homogeneous differential
d
of degree
1
satisfying
the equation
d^{2}=0
.
This means for every
i\in\Gamma
the component
d_{i}\colon X^{i}\longrightarrow X^{i+1}
of the differential
d
is a morphism in
\mathsf{A}
and

d_{i+1}d_{i}=0
for all
i\in\Gamma
.
For any two complexes
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathsf{A}
, the graded
abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{*}(X^{*},Y^{*})
of morphisms between
the underlying graded objects of
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is endowed with
the differential
d
of degree
1
defined by the formula

d(f)_{i}=d_{Y,n+i}f_{i}-(-1)^{n}f_{i+1}d_{X,i}\colon\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{n}(X^{*},Y^{*})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{n+1}(X^{*},Y^{*})
, where
d_{X,i}\colon X^{i}\longrightarrow X^{i+1}
and
d_{Y,i}\colon Y^{i}\longrightarrow Y^{i+1}
.
The resulting complex of abelian groups is denoted by

\Hom_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
; it is the complex of morphisms
from
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to
Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in the
DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})

of complexes in
\mathsf{A}
.

Notice that
every graded object in
\mathsf{A}
admits a differential
making it a complex
(e. g., the zero differential).
Consequently, the graded category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{*}
is naturally equivalent
to the graded category
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
of graded objects in
\mathsf{A}
, and
the preadditive category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{0}
is naturally equivalent
to
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
.

The preadditive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A}))
of closed morphisms of
degree zero in
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
is called the
category of complexes
in
\mathsf{A}
and denoted by
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
One can also consider the graded category of complexes
\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{A})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A}))
.
The preadditive category
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A}))
of closed
morphisms of degree zero in
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
viewed up to cochain homotopy
is called the
homotopy category of
(
complexes in
)
\mathsf{A}
.
One can also consider the graded homotopy category
\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{A})=\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A}))
.

The DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
always has shifts and twists.
So the graded categories
\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{A})
have shifts, too.
When
\mathsf{A}
is an additive category, the DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
is
additive as well; hence it also has cones.
The categories
\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{A})
, and
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A})

are additive, too, in this case (in fact,
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A})
is
a triangulated category).
When
\mathsf{A}
is an abelian category, so is
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.

When an additive category
\mathsf{A}
is idempotent-complete, so
are the DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
, the graded category
\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
and the additive category
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
The categories
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
\mathcal{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
,
\mathcal{K}(\mathsf{A})
,
and
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A})
have infinite products or coproducts whenever
the category
\mathsf{A}
has.

2.2.
Curved DG-modules

A
graded ring

R^{*}=\bigoplus_{n\in\Gamma}R^{n}
is a graded
abelian group endowed with an associative, unital ring structure
given by a homogeneous multiplication map
R^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}
.
The unit is an element of
R^{0}
.

A
graded left
R^{*}
-module

M^{*}=\bigoplus_{n\in\Gamma}M^{n}

is a graded abelian group endowed with an associative, unital left

R^{*}
-module structure given by a homogeneous action map

R^{*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
.

Graded right
R^{*}
-modules
are defined similarly.

Let
L^{*}
and
M^{*}
be two graded left
R^{*}
-modules.
A
homogeneous
R^{*}
-module map
f\colon L^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
of degree

n\in\Gamma
is a homogeneous map of degree
n
between
underlying graded abelian groups of
L^{*}
and
M^{*}
satisfying
the equation
f(rx)=(-1)^{n|r|}rf(x)
for all
r\in R^{|r|}

and
x\in L^{|x|}
(notice the sign rule!).
In the graded category
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}
of graded left
R^{*}
-modules,
the objects are the graded left
R^{*}
-modules, and the degree
n

component of the graded group of morphisms
\Hom_{R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}}^{*}(L^{*},M^{*})=\Hom^{*}_{R^{*}}(L^{*},M^{*})
is the group of all homogeneous
R^{*}
-module
maps
L^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
of degree
n
.

The graded category
{\operatorname{\mathit{mod--}}}R^{*}
of graded right
R^{*}
-modules is
defined similarly, except that there is no sign involved in
the definition of a homogeneous right
R^{*}
-module map.
In the additive categories
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}=(R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}})^{0}
and

{\operatorname{\mathsf{mod--}}}R^{*}=({\operatorname{\mathit{mod--}}}R^{*})^{0}
, the homogeneous
R^{*}
-module maps of
degree
0
form the groups of morphisms.
The graded categories
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}
and
{\operatorname{\mathit{mod--}}}R^{*}
have shifts.
The categories
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}
,
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
,
{\operatorname{\mathit{mod--}}}R^{*}
, and

{\operatorname{\mathsf{mod--}}}R^{*}
are idempotent-complete, and they have infinite
coproducts and products.
The categories
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
and
{\operatorname{\mathsf{mod--}}}R^{*}
are abelian.

A
DG-ring

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*},d)
is a complex of abelian groups
endowed with an associative, unital graded ring structure given
a multiplication map
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
which is
a morphism of complexes.
This means that
d\colon R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}
is a odd derivation of degree
1

on the graded ring
R^{*}
, i. e., it is a homogenenous endomorphism
of degree
1
of the graded abelian group
R^{*}
satisfying
the
Leibniz rule with signs
and, in addition,
the equation
d^{2}=0
is satisfied.

A
left DG-module

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
a complex of abelian groups endowed with an associative, unital graded
left
R^{*}
-module structure given by a multiplication map
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
which is a morphism of complexes.
This means that
d_{M}\colon M^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
is an odd derivation of
degree
1
on the graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
compatible with the odd
derivation
d
on the graded ring
R^{*}
, i. e.,
d_{M}
is a homogeneous
endomorphism of degree
1
of the graded abelian group
M^{*}

satisfying the Leibniz rule with signs
d_{M}(rx)=d(r)x+(-1)^{|r|}rd_{M}(x)

for all
r\in R^{|r|}
and
x\in M^{|x|}
and, in addition,
the equation
d_{M}^{2}=0
is satisfied.

Right DG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
are defined similarly.

Left DG-modules over a DG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
form a DG-category

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
, and similarly right DG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
form
a DG-category
{\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in a natural way.
The context of DG-rings is
not
the maximal natural
generality for this construction of DG-categories, however,
as the construction can be extended naturally to a wider setting
of
curved
DG-rings (
CDG-rings
) and curved
DG-modules over them.
Let us recall the related definitions.

A
CDG-ring

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
is a graded ring endowed with
an odd derivation
d\colon R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}
of degree
1
(i. e.,

d(rs)=d(r)s+(-1)^{|r|}rd(s)
for all
r\in R^{|r|}
and
s\in R^{|s|}
)
and an element
h\in R^{2}
for which the following two equations
are satisfied:

(i)

d^{2}(r)=[h,r]
(where
[h,r]=hr-rh
is the commutator)
for all
r\in R^{*}
; and

(ii)

d(h)=0
.

The element
h\in R^{2}
is called the
curvature element
.
A pair
(R^{*},d)
is a DG-ring if and only if the triple
(R^{*},d,0)

is a CDG-ring; so the DG-rings are just the CDG-rings with
zero curvature.
We refer to
[
35
, Section 3.1]
or
[
45
, Section 3.2]

for the (nontrivial!) definition of a
morphism of CDG-rings
;
we will recall it below in Section
2.4
.

A
left CDG-module

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is
a graded left
R^{*}
-module endowed with an odd derivation

d_{M}\colon M^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
of degree
1
compatible with the derivation
d

on
R^{*}
(i. e.,
d_{M}(rx)=d(r)x+(-1)^{|r|}rd_{M}(x)
for all

r\in R^{|r|}
and
x\in M^{|x|}
) such that the following
equation holds:

(iii)

d_{M}^{2}(x)=hx
for all
x\in M^{*}
.

Similarly, a
right CDG-module

\boldsymbol{N}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(N^{*},d_{N})

over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is a graded right
R^{*}
-module endowed with an odd
derivation
d_{N}\colon N^{*}\longrightarrow N^{*}
of degree
1
compatible with
the derivation
d
on
R^{*}
(i. e.,
d_{N}(yr)=d_{N}(y)r+(-1)^{|y|}yd(r)

for all
y\in N^{|y|}
and
r\in R^{|r|}
) such that the following
equation holds:

(iv)

d_{N}^{2}(y)=-yh
for all
y\in N^{*}
.

For any two left CDG-modules
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(L^{*},d_{L})
and

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
over a CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
,
the graded abelian group
\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}(L^{*},M^{*})
is endowed with
a natural differential
d
of degree
1
defined by the usual rule

d(f)(x)=d_{M}(f(x))-(-1)^{|f|}f(d_{L}(x))
for all
x\in L^{*}
, where

f\in\Hom_{R^{*}}^{|f|}(L^{*},M^{*})
.
One has
d^{2}(f)=0
, as the two curvature-related terms cancel each
other:

d(d(f))(x)=d_{M}(d(f)(x))-(-1)^{|f|+1}d(f)(d_{L}(x))\\
=d_{M}(d_{M}(f(x))-(-1)^{|f|}d_{M}(f(d_{L}(x)))-(-1)^{|f|+1}d_{M}(f(d_{L}(x)))-f(d_{L}(d_{L}(x)))\\
=hf(x)-f(hx)=0.

Hence we obtain a complex of abelian groups, denoted by

\Hom_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.

In the
DG-category
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
of left CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, the left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
are the objects,
and
\Hom_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=\Hom_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})

is the complex of morphisms from
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
to
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
The
DG-category
{\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
of right CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is defined similarly.

The DG-categories
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
and
{\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
are additive
with shifts and twists, and with infinite coproducts and products.
For example, if
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
is a left CDG-module over

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
a\in\Hom^{1}_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}(\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
is
a Maurer–Cartan cochain, then the rule
d_{M}^{\prime}(x)=d_{M}(x)+ax
for all

x\in M^{*}
defines a left CDG-module
{}^{\prime}\!\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M}^{\prime})

over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
such that
{}^{\prime}\!\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(a)
.

The category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
of closed morphisms of degree
zero in
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
is abelian.
It is called the
abelian category of left CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

A DG-module over a DG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*},d)
is the same thing as
a CDG-module over the CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,0)
.
So the DG-categories of CDG-modules
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
and

{\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
are equivalent (in fact, isomorphic) to
the DG-categories of DG-modules
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
and

{\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, respectively, when
h=0
.

The reader should be warned that the graded category

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{*}
is
not
equivalent to the graded category

R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}
, generally speaking, and the additive category

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
is likewise
not
equivalent to the abelian
category
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
Rather,
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{*}
and
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
are equivalent
to certain (not well-behaved) full subcategories in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}
and

R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, respectively.
This happens because
not every graded
R^{*}
-module admits
a structure of CDG-module over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(not even when
h=0
).
We refer to Section
3.1
below for
a substantial discussion.

2.3.
Quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebras

Let
R
be a commutative ring and
A
be an
R
-
R
-bimodule.
Define an increasing filtration
F_{0}A\subset F_{1}A\subset F_{2}A\subset\dotsb
on
A
by the rule that
F_{-1}A=0
and
an element
a\in A
belongs to
F_{n}A
if and only if

ra-ar\in F_{n-1}A
for all
r\in R
.
Then
F_{n}A
is an
R
-
R
-subbimodule in
A
for every
n\geq 0
.
If
A
is an associative ring with the
R
-
R
-bimodule structure
induced by a ring homomorphism
\rho\colon R\longrightarrow A
, then
F
is
a multiplicative filtration on
A
, i. e.,
1\in\rho(R)\subset F_{0}A

and
F_{n}A\cdot F_{m}A\subset F_{n+m}A
for all
n
,
m\geq 0
.

Let us say that
A
is a
quasi-module
(in a more common
language, a
differential bimodule
) over
R
if the filtration

F
is exhaustive, that is
A=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}F_{n}A
.
Notice that any
R
-module
M
, viewed as an
R
-
R
-bimodule in
which the left and right actions of
R
agree, becomes a quasi-module
over
R
(with
F_{0}M=M
).

An associative ring
A
endowed with a ring homomorphism
R\longrightarrow A

making
A
a quasi-module over
R
is said to be a
quasi-algebra

over
R
.
For example, if
k\subset R
is a subring in a commutative ring
R
,
then the ring
A
of all
k
-linear differential operators

R\longrightarrow R

[
10
,
55
]
is a quasi-algebra over
R
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
A
and
B
be two quasi-modules over
R
.
Then
A\otimes_{R}B
is also a quasi-module over
R
.

Proof.

The image of the map
F_{i}A\otimes_{R}F_{j}B\longrightarrow A\otimes_{R}B
induced
by the inclusions
F_{i}A\longrightarrow A
and
F_{j}B\longrightarrow B
is
contained in the subbimodule
F_{n}(A\otimes_{R}B)\subset A\otimes_{R}B

for
n=i+j
.
Hence the filtration
F
on
A\otimes_{R}B
is exhaustive whenever
the similar filtrations on
A
and
B
are exhaustive.
∎

We denote by
R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
the abelian category of (ungraded, left)
modules over a ring
R
.
A homomorphism of commutative rings
\sigma\colon R\longrightarrow S
is
an epimorphism in the category of commutative (equivalently,
associative) rings if and only if the natural maps

S\rightrightarrows S\otimes_{R}S\longrightarrow S
are isomorphisms
(in this case, the two maps
S\rightrightarrows S\otimes_{R}S

are equal to each other).
A ring homomorphism
\sigma
is an epimorphism if and only if
the related functor of restriction of scalars
\sigma_{*}\colon S{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}\longrightarrow R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
is fully faithful
[
56
, Section XI.1]
.

A commutative ring epimorphism
\sigma\colon R\longrightarrow S
is said
to be
flat
if
S
is a flat
R
-module.
In this case, the full subcategory
\sigma_{*}(S{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
is
closed under extensions in
R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}

[
21
, Theorem 4.4]
.
Since the functor
\sigma_{*}
preserves all limits and colimits,
it is also clear that, for any ring epimorphism
\sigma
,
the full subcategory
\sigma_{*}(S{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
is closed under
limits and colimits in
R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
.

Let
U
be an affine scheme and
V\subset U
be an affine open
subscheme.
Let
R=O(U)
and
S=O(V)
be the rings of functions on
U
and
V
,
and let
\sigma\colon R\longrightarrow S
be the ring homomorphism corresponding
to the open immersion morphism
V\longrightarrow U
.
Then
\sigma
is a flat epimorphism of commutative rings.
This is the main example of a flat epimorphism we are interested in.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\sigma\colon R\longrightarrow S
be a flat epimorphism of commutative rings,
and let
A
be a quasi-module over
R
.
Then

(a)
the map
\sigma
induces isomorphisms of

R
-
R
-bimodules
S\otimes_{R}A\longleftarrow S\otimes_{R}A\otimes_{R}S\longrightarrow A\otimes_{R}S
;

(b)
the
S
-
S
-bimodule
S\otimes_{R}A
is a quasi-module
over
S
.

Proof.

Part (a): it suffices to show that the
R
-
S
-bimodule
A\otimes_{R}S
,
viewed as an
R
-module with the left action of
R
, belongs to
the full subcategory
\sigma_{*}(S{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})\subset R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
.
Indeed, put
G_{n}(A\otimes_{R}S)=F_{n}A\otimes_{R}S\subset A\otimes_{R}S
.
Then
G
is an exhaustive increasing filtration of the bimodule

A\otimes_{R}S
by subbimodules such that the left and right actions of
R

in the successive quotient bimodules
G_{n}(A\otimes_{R}S)/G_{n-1}(A\otimes_{R}S)\simeq(F_{n}A/F_{n-1}A)\otimes_{R}S
agree.
Hence, viewed as
R
-modules with the left action of
R
,
the successive quotients admit an extension of their
R
-module
structure to an
S
-module structure.
It remains to recall that the full subcategory
\sigma_{*}(S{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})\subset R{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
is closed under extensions and direct limits.

Part (b): Following the proof of part (a), the
S
-
S
-bimodule

S\otimes_{R}A\simeq S\otimes_{R}A\otimes_{R}S\simeq A\otimes_{R}S
admits an exhaustive
increasing filtration by subbimodules such that the left and right
actions of
S
in the successive quotient bimodules agree.
Hence
S\otimes_{R}A
is a quasi-module over
S
.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

Let
\sigma\colon R\longrightarrow S
be a flat epimorphism of commutative rings.
Let
A
be a quasi-module over
R
and
B
be an
S
-
S
-bimodule.
Let
A\longrightarrow B
be a morphism of
R
-
R
-bimodules.
Then the induced morphism of
S
-
R
-bimodules
S\otimes_{R}A\longrightarrow B
is
an isomorphism if and only if the induced morphism of

R
-
S
-bimodules
A\otimes_{R}S\longrightarrow B
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
2.2
(a).
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
A
be a sheaf of bimodules over the structure
sheaf
O_{X}
of
X
.
Assume that, for every affine open subscheme
U\subset X
,
the
O(U)
-
O(U)
-bimodule
A(U)
is a quasi-module.
Then the sheaf of
O_{X}
-modules
A
with the left
O_{X}
-module
structure is quasi-coherent if and only if the sheaf of
O_{X}
-modules

A
with the right
O_{X}
-module structure is quasi-coherent.

Proof.

A sheaf of
O_{X}
-modules
M
on a scheme
X
is quasi-coherent if and
only if, for every pair of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
,
the map of
O(V)
-modules
O(V)\otimes_{O(U)}M(U)\longrightarrow M(V)
induced
by the restriction map
M(U)\longrightarrow M(V)
is an isomorphism.
Hence the assertion of the proposition follows from
Corollary
2.3
.
∎

Let
X
be a scheme.
A
quasi-coherent quasi-module
on
X
is a sheaf of bimodules
over
O_{X}
satisfying the equivalent conditions of
Proposition
2.4
.
In order to produce a quasi-coherent quasi-module
A
over
X
,
it suffices to specify a quasi-module
A(U)
over
O(U)
for every
affine open subscheme
U\subset X
and a morphism of

O(U)
-
O(U)
-bimodules
A(U)\longrightarrow A(V)
for every pair of affine
open subscheme
V\subset U\subset X
in such a way that the induced
map
O(V)\otimes_{O(U)}A(U)\longrightarrow A(V)
is an isomorphism and
the triangle diagram
A(U)\longrightarrow A(V)\longrightarrow A(W)
is commutative
for every triple of affine open subschemes

W\subset V\subset U\subset X
(cf. the description of
quasi-coherent sheaves in
[
20
, Section 2]
).

Quasi-coherent quasi-modules on
X
can be thought of as
“quasi-coherent torsion sheaves on the formal completion of
the diagonal
X
in
X\times_{\Spec\mathbb{Z}}X
” in the sense
of
[
9
, Section 7.11]
and
[
46
, Section 2]

(but one needs to explain what this means).

Morphisms of quasi-coherent quasi-modules
on
X
are defined
simply as their morphisms as sheaves of bimodules over
O_{X}
.
The category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent quasi-modules on
X
is
a Grothendieck abelian category.
It is endowed with two forgetful functors
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}\rightrightarrows X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
(the left and the right one) to
the Grothendieck abelian category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent sheaves
on
X
.
Both the forgetful funcators are faithful, exact, and preserve
colimits.

Any quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
, viewed as a sheaf of bimodules over

O_{X}
in which the left and right actions of
O_{X}
agree, becomes
a quasi-coherent quasi-module over
X
.
So the category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
is also naturally a full subcategory in

X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(a)
For any quasi-coherent quasi-modules
A
and
B
on
X
,
the tensor product of sheaves of
O_{X}
-
O_{X}
-bimodules

A\otimes_{O_{X}}B
is a quasi-coherent quasi-module on
X
.
For any affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, there is a natural
isomorphism
(A\otimes_{O_{X}}\penalty B)(U)\simeq A(U)\otimes_{O(U)}B(U)
of
quasi-modules over
O(U)
.

(b)
For any quasi-coherent quasi-module
A
and any
quasi-coherent sheaf
M
on
X
, the tensor product
A\otimes_{O_{X}}M
,
endowed with the
O_{X}
-module structure induced by the left

O_{X}
-module structure on
A
, is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
.
For any affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, there is a natural
isomorphism of
O(U)
-modules
(A\otimes_{O_{X}}M)(U)\simeq A(U)\otimes_{O(U)}M(U)
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from
Lemmas
2.1

and
2.2
.
Part (b) can be viewed as a particular case of part (a).
∎

The operation of tensor product over
O_{X}
, described in
Lemma
2.5
(a), makes

X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
an (associative, noncommutative) tensor category.
The structure sheaf
O_{X}\in X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}\subset X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
of the scheme
X

is the unit object of this tensor category structure.
The tensor product operation described in
Lemma
2.5
(b) makes

X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
is a (left) module category over the tensor category

X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.

A
quasi-coherent quasi-algebra

A
on
X
is a sheaf of rings
endowed with a morphism of sheaves of rings
O_{X}\longrightarrow A
such that
the resulting sheaf of
O_{X}
-
O_{X}
-bimodules
A
is
a quasi-coherent quasi-module on
X
.
Equivalently, one can say that a quasi-coherent quasi-algebra on
X

is an associative, unital algebra (monoid) object in the tensor
category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.
This means that
A
is a quasi-coherent quasi-module on
X
endowed
with morphisms of quasi-coherent quasi-modules
O_{X}\longrightarrow A
and

A\otimes_{O_{X}}A\longrightarrow A
satisfying the conventional associativity
and unitality equations.

A
quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra

A^{*}
on
X
is
a sheaf of graded rings
A^{*}=(A^{n})_{n\in\Gamma}
on
X
endowed
with a morphism of sheaves of rings
O_{X}\longrightarrow A^{0}
such that
the resulting structure of a sheaf of
O_{X}
-
O_{X}
-bimodules on
the sheaf of abelian groups
A^{n}
makes
A^{n}
a quasi-coherent
quasi-module for every
n\in\Gamma
.
Equivalently, a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra is
an associative, unital
\Gamma
-graded algebra object in
the tensor category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.
This means that
(A^{n})_{n\in\Gamma}
is a family of quasi-coherent
quasi-modules on
X
endowed with morphisms of quasi-coherent
quasi-modules
O_{X}\longrightarrow A^{0}
and
A^{n}\otimes_{O_{X}}A^{m}\longrightarrow A^{n+m}
for all
n
,
m\in\Gamma
satisfying the conventional
associativity and unitality equations.

A
quasi-coherent left module

M
over a quasi-coherent
quasi-algebra
A
on
X
is a sheaf of left modules over the sheaf
of rings
A
whose underlying sheaf of
O_{X}
-modules
M
is
quasi-coherent.
Equivalently, one can say that a quasi-coherent left module
M
over
is a module object in the module category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
over the algebra
object
A
in the tensor category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.
This means that
M
is a quasi-coherent sheaf on
X
endowed
with a morphism of quasi-coherent sheaves
A\otimes_{O_{X}}M\longrightarrow M

satisfying the conventional associativity and unitality equations
together with the structure morphisms
O_{X}\longrightarrow A
and
A\otimes_{O_{X}}A\longrightarrow A
of the quasi-coherent quasi-algebra
A
.

The category
A{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent left modules over
a quasi-coherent quasi-algebra
A
on
X
is a Grothendieck
abelian category.
The forgetful functor
A{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}\longrightarrow X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
is faithful, exact,
and preserves colimits.

A
quasi-coherent graded left module

M^{*}
over a quasi-coherent
graded quasi-algebra
A^{*}
on
X
is a sheaf of graded left modules
over the sheaf of graded rings
A^{*}
such that the sheaf of

O_{X}
-modules
M^{n}
is quasi-coherent for every
n\in\Gamma
.
Equivalently, one can say that a quasi-coherent graded left module

M^{*}
is a graded module object in the module category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qcoh}}}
over
the graded algebra object
A^{*}
in the tensor category
X{\operatorname{\mathrm{--qqcoh}}}
.
This means that
(M^{m})_{m\in\Gamma}
is a family of quasi-coherent
sheaves on
X
endowed with morphisms of quasi-coherent sheaves

A^{n}\otimes_{O_{X}}M^{m}\longrightarrow M^{n+m}
,
n
,
m\in\Gamma
, satisfying
the associativity and unitality equations.
Here, as in Lemma
2.5
(b),
the
O_{X}
-module structure on the tensor product
A^{n}\otimes_{O_{X}}M^{m}
is
induced by the left
O_{X}
-module structure on
A^{n}
.

Given a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
A^{*}
, the graded category
of quasi-coherent graded left modules
A^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--qcoh}}}
and the abelian
category of quasi-coherent graded left modules
A^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
are defined
similarly to Section
2.2
.
The graded abelian group of morphisms in
A^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--qcoh}}}
from
a quasi-coherent graded module
L^{*}
to a quasi-coherent graded
module
M^{*}
is denoted by
\Hom_{A^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--qcoh}}}}^{*}(L^{*},M^{*})=\Hom_{A^{*}}^{*}(L^{*},M^{*})
.

2.4.
Quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebras

The definition of a
quasi-coherent CDG-algebra
over a scheme
goes back to
[
35
, Section B.1]
and
[
18
, Section 2.1]
.
The extension to quasi-algebras is straightforward; we spell it out
below in this section for the sake of completeness of the exposition.

First we need to complete the discussion of
Section
2.2
by defining
morphisms
of CDG-rings
.
Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d_{R},h_{R})
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(S^{*},d_{S},h_{S})
be
two CDG-rings.
A morphism of CDG-rings
(f,a)\colon\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is a pair

(f,a)
, where
f\colon R^{*}\longrightarrow S^{*}
is a morphism of graded rings and

a\in S^{1}
is an element for which the following two equations
are satisfied:

(v)

f(d_{R}(r))=d_{S}(f(r))+[a,f(r)]
for all
r\in R^{|r|}

(where
[x,y]=xy-(-1)^{|x||y|}yx
is the commutator with signs);

(vi)

f(h_{R})=h_{S}+d_{S}(a)+a^{2}
.

The element
a\in S^{1}
is called the
change-of-connection
element
.
The
composition
of two morphisms of CDG-rings

(R^{*},d_{R},h_{R})\xrightarrow{(f,a)}(S^{*},d_{S},h_{S})\xrightarrow{(g,b)}(T^{*},d_{T},h_{T})
is given by the rule
(g,b)\circ(f,a)=(g\circ f,\>b+g(a))
.

All CDG-ring morphisms of the form
(\mathrm{id},a)\colon(S^{*},d^{\prime},h^{\prime})\longrightarrow(S^{*},d,h)
are isomorphisms.
They are called
change-of-connection isomorphisms
;
while morphisms of the form
(f,0)\colon(R^{*},d_{R},h_{R})\longrightarrow(S^{*},d_{S},h_{S})

are called
strict morphisms
.
Any morphism of CDG-rings can be uniquely decomposed into a strict
morphism followed by a change-of-connection isomorphism.

The inclusion functor from the category of DG-rings to the category
of CDG-rings is faithful, but
not
fully faithful.
A morphism of DG-rings is essentially the same thing as a strict
morphism between the related CDG-rings.
Still two quite different DG-rings may be isomorphic as CDG-rings,
connected by a change-of-connection isomorphism (otherwise known
as a
Maurer–Cartan twist
).

A rule for transforming CDG-modules under morphisms of CDG-rings is
needed for the discussion of sheaves of CDG-modules over sheaves of
CDG-rings, which is our aim in this section.
Let
(f,a)\colon\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a morphism of CDG-rings, and
let
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(L^{*},d_{L})
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
be left CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, respectively.
Then a
map of CDG-modules
g\colon\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

compatible with the given morphism of CDG-rings
(f,a)
is a morphism
of graded modules
L^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
compatible with the morphism
f

of graded rings and satisfying the equation

(vii)

g(d_{L}(x))=d_{M}(g(x))+ag(x)
for all
x\in L^{*}
.

Similarly, let
