\boldsymbol{N}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(N^{*},d_{N})
and
\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(Q^{*},d_{Q})
be
right CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, respectively.
Then a map of CDG-modules
g\colon\boldsymbol{N}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
compatible
with the given morphism of CDG-rings
(f,a)
is a morphism of graded
modules
N^{*}\longrightarrow Q^{*}
compatible with the morphism
f
of graded
rings and satisfying the equation

(viii)

g(d_{N}(y))=d_{Q}(g(y))-(-1)^{|y|}g(y)a
for all
y\in N^{|y|}
.

Let
X
be a scheme.
A
quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra

\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
X
is
the following set of data:

•

a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
B^{*}
over
X
is given;

•

for each affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, an odd derivation

d_{U}\colon B^{*}(U)\longrightarrow B^{*}(U)
of degree
1
and a curvature element

h_{U}\in B^{2}(U)
are given;

•

for each pair of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
,
a change-of-connection element
a_{VU}\in B^{1}(V)
is given;

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(ix)

for each affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, the triple

(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})
is a CDG-ring;

(x)

for each pair of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
,
the pair
(\rho_{VU},a_{VU})
is a morphism of CDG-rings

(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})\longrightarrow(B^{*}(V),d_{V},h_{V})
, where
\rho_{VU}\colon B^{*}(U)\longrightarrow B^{*}(V)
denotes the restriction morphism in the sheaf of
graded rings
B^{*}
;

(xi)

for each triple of affine open subschemes
W\subset V\subset U\subset X
, one has
(\rho_{WV},a_{W,V})\circ(\rho_{VU},a_{VU})=(\rho_{WU},a_{WU})\colon(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})\longrightarrow(B^{*}(W),d_{W},\allowbreak h_{W})
; in other words, the equation
a_{WU}=a_{WV}+\rho_{WV}(a_{VU})

holds in
B^{1}(W)
.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
X
be a scheme endowed with an open covering
X=\bigcup_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
, and let
B^{*}
be a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
over
X
.
Suppose that a CDG-ring structure
(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})
on the graded
ring
B^{*}(U)
is given
for all affine open subschemes
U\subset X

for which there exists
\alpha
such that
U\subset Y_{\alpha}
, and
change-of-connection elements
a_{VU}\in B^{1}(V)
are chosen for all

V\subset U
with
U\subset Y_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha
in such a way
that the conditions (x–xi) are satisfied whenever
U\subset Y_{\alpha}
.
Then this set of data can be (uniquely in a natural sense) extended
to a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra structure
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
on
B^{*}
,
in the sense of the definition above.

Indeed, it suffices to consider the case when
X=\bigcup_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
is an affine open covering of an affine scheme
X
.
In this case, the formula
c_{\alpha\beta}=a_{U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta},U_{\alpha}}-a_{U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta},U_{\beta}}
defines a Čech

1
-cocycle
c
with the coefficients in the sheaf of abelian groups

B^{1}
on the Zariski topology of the affine scheme
X
with the chosen
affine open covering
\{Y_{\alpha}\}
.
The point is that, since
B^{1}
is a quasi-coherent sheaf (e. g.,
with the left action of
O_{X}
, or alternatively with the right action
of
O_{X}
), all such cocycles are coboundaries (in the same Čech
complex for the fixed covering
X=\bigcup_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
);
see, e. g.,
[
22
, Tag 01X8]
.
This allows to solve the problem of defining a CDG-ring structure
on the graded ring
B^{*}(X)
together with a compatible collection
of change-of-connection elements
a_{Y_{\alpha},X}\in B^{1}(Y_{\alpha})

for all
\alpha
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra over
X
.
A
quasi-coherent left CDG-module

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is the following set of data:

•

a quasi-coherent graded left module
M^{*}
over
the quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
B^{*}
is given;

•

for each affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, an odd derivation

d_{U,M}\colon M^{*}(U)\longrightarrow M^{*}(U)
of degree
1
on the graded module

M^{*}(U)
compatible with the odd derivation
d_{U}
on the graded ring

B^{*}(U)
is given;

such that the following condition is satisfied:

(xii)

for each pair of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
,
the restriction map
M^{*}(V)\longrightarrow M^{*}(U)
in the sheaf of graded
modules
M^{*}
is a map of CDG-modules
(M^{*}(U),d_{U,M})\longrightarrow(M^{*}(V),d_{V,M})
compatible with the morphism of CDG-rings

(\rho_{VU},a_{VU})\colon(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})\longrightarrow(B^{*}(V),d_{V},h_{V})
.

Let
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
and
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be two quasi-coherent left CDG-modules
over a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
over
X
, and
let
L^{*}
and
M^{*}
be their underlying quasi-coherent graded modules
over the quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
B^{*}
.
Then the graded abelian group
\Hom_{B^{*}}^{*}(L^{*},M^{*})
of homogeneous
maps of quasi-coherent graded modules
L^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
is endowed
with a natural differential
d
of degree
1
defined as follows.

Let
f\in\Hom_{B^{*}}^{|f|}(L^{*},M^{*})
be a homogeneous map
of degree
|f|
between the two quasi-coherent graded modules.
In order to define the homogeneous map

d(f)\in\Hom_{B^{*}}^{|f|+1}(L^{*},M^{*})
, consider an affine open
subscheme
U\subset X
, and denote by
f_{U}\colon L^{*}(U)\longrightarrow M^{*}(U)

the action of
f
on the sections over
U
.
Following Section
2.2
, there is
a natural differential
d
on the graded abelian group

\Hom_{B^{*}(U)}(L^{*}(U),M^{*}(U))
, defined in terms of the odd
derivations
d_{U,L}\colon L^{*}(U)\longrightarrow L^{*}(U)
and

d_{U,M}\colon M^{*}(U)\longrightarrow M^{*}(U)
.
We put
d(f)_{U}=d(f_{U})
.
It is straightforward to check that the resulting homogeneous
maps of graded modules
d(f)_{U}\in\Hom_{B^{*}(U)}^{|f|+1}(L^{*}(U),M^{*}(U))

form commutative square diagrams with the restriction maps

L^{*}(U)\longrightarrow L^{*}(V)
and
M^{*}(U)\longrightarrow M^{*}(V)
for any pair
of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
.

Hence we obtain a complex of abelian groups, denoted by

\Hom_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.
In the
DG-category
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent left
CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, the quasi-coherent left CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
are the objects, and

\Hom^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})=\Hom_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
is the complex of morphisms
from
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
to
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

The DG-category
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
is additive with shifts and twists,
and with infinite coproducts.
The category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})
of closed morphisms of degree
0

in
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
is a Grothendieck abelian category.
It is called the
abelian category of quasi-coherent left
CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.

2.5.
Factorization categories

Factorizations

[
17
, Section 6 and Appendix A]
,

[
5
]
can be thought of as a category-theoretic generalization
of CDG-modules over a CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
such that
R^{n}=0

for
n
odd (i. e.,
n\in\Gamma\setminus 2\Gamma
), the multiplication
maps
R^{n}\otimes_{R^{0}}R^{m}\longrightarrow R^{n+m}
are isomorphisms for all

n
,
m\in 2\Gamma
, and
d=0
, while
h\in R^{2}
is a central
element in
R^{*}
.
In the exposition below in this paper, we take up a slightly
more general notational system for matrix factorizations than
in
[
5
]
, following an approach which has been used in
the algebro-geometric literature on matrix factorizations over stacks;
see, e. g.,
[
59
, Section 2.2]
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a preadditive category and
\Lambda\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
be
an autoequivalence.
We will consider two cases concerning the grading group
\Gamma
:
either
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, or
\Lambda
is an involutive autoequivalence
and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
In the latter case, a natural isomorphism
\theta\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
of functors
\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
is presumed to be given
such that the equation
\theta_{\Lambda(X)}=\Lambda(\theta_{X})

is satisfied for all objects
X\in\mathsf{A}
.

More generally, one could consider the
2p
-periodic case of

\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2p
, where
p
is a positive integer.
Then a natural isomorphism
\theta\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{2p}
of functors
\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
is needed satisfying
the equation
\theta_{\Lambda(X)}=\Lambda(\theta_{X})
for all objects

X\in\mathsf{A}
.
In all these cases, the group
\Gamma
acts on the category
\mathsf{A}

by powers of the functor
\Lambda
: for every
n\in\Gamma
there is
the functor
\Lambda^{n}\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
, and the natural isomorphisms

\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{0}
and
\Lambda^{n}\circ\Lambda^{m}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{n+m}
for all
n
,
m\in\Gamma
are
defined and satisfy all the natural compatibilities.

A
potential

w
for
\Lambda
is a natural transformation

w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
satifying the equation
w_{\Lambda(X)}=\Lambda(w_{X})
for all
X\in\mathsf{A}
.
Our aim is to construct the DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)

of
factorizations
of
w
.
On the way to factorizations, we start with constructing the graded
category
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
of
\Lambda
-periodic objects

in
\mathsf{A}
.

By definition, a
\Lambda
-periodic object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}
in
\mathsf{A}
is
a collection of objects
(X^{i}\in\mathsf{A})_{i\in\Gamma}
endowed with
isomorphisms
\lambda_{X}^{i,j}\colon\Lambda^{i-j}(X^{j})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}X^{i}
defined for all
i
,
j\in\Gamma
.
The equation
\lambda_{X}^{i,j}\circ\Lambda^{i-j}(\lambda^{j,k}_{X})=\lambda^{i,k}_{X}
needs to be satisfied for all
i
,
j
,
k\in\Gamma
.
The collection of all objects
(X^{i}\in\mathsf{A})_{i\in\Gamma}
with
the periodicity isomorphisms
\lambda_{X}^{i,j}
forgotten defines
the underlying graded object
X^{*}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
of
a
\Lambda
-periodic object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
.
The shift
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n]
of a
\Lambda
-periodic object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}

by a degree
n\in\Gamma
is a
\Lambda
-periodic object defined
by the rules
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n]^{i}=X^{n+i}
and
\lambda_{X[n]}^{i,j}=\lambda_{X}^{n+i,n+j}
for all
n
,
i
,
j\in\Gamma
.

Let us first define the preadditive category
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
of

\Lambda
-periodic objects in
\mathsf{A}
.
In both
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
and
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, the objects are
the
\Lambda
-periodic objects in
\mathsf{A}
.
For any two objects
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}
and
Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
,
the abelian group
\Hom_{\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
is defined
as the subgroup in the group
\Hom_{\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},Y^{*})
consisting of
all the morphisms
(f_{i}\colon X^{i}\to Y^{i})_{i\in\Gamma}
satisfying
the equations
\lambda^{i,j}_{Y}\circ\Lambda^{i-j}(f_{j})=f_{i}\circ\lambda^{i,j}_{X}
for all
i
,
j\in\Gamma
.
The category
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
is naturally equivalent to
the category
\mathsf{A}
, with the equivalence provided by the functor
taking a
\Lambda
-periodic object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)

to the object
X^{0}\in\mathsf{A}
.

The graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{*}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})

of morphisms in the graded category
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
is a homogeneous
subgroup of the graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},Y^{*})
.
Similarly to Section
2.1
, it is defined by
the rule
\Hom^{n}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})=\Hom_{\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},\allowbreak Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n])

for all
n\in\Gamma
.
The composition of morphisms in the categories
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)

and
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
agrees with the one in the categories

\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})
, respectively.

Unlike
the preadditive category
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, the graded
category
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
cannot be recovered from the category
\mathsf{A}

alone; it depends on the autoequivalence
\Lambda
in an essential way.

The categories
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
are additive
whenever the preadditive category
\mathsf{A}
is.
Assuming
\mathsf{A}
is additive, the categories
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
and

\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
are idempotent-complete whenever the category

\mathsf{A}
is.
The categories
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
have infinite
coproducts or products whenever the category
\mathsf{A}
has.

A
factorization

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{M})
of the potential
w
is
a
\Lambda
-periodic object endowed with a homogeneous endomorphism
of degree
1
, i. e.,
d_{M}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
,
such that, for every
n\in\Gamma
, the composition

(\lambda_{M}^{n+2,n})^{-1}d_{M,n+1}d_{M,n}\colon M^{n}\longrightarrow M^{n+2}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Lambda^{2}(M^{n})
is equal to the morphism

w_{M^{n}}\colon M^{n}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(M^{n})
.
For any two factorizations
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(L^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{L})
and

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{M})
of the same potential
w
, the graded abelian
group
\Hom^{*}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(L^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},M^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
is endowed with
a natural differential
d
of degree
1
defined by the usual rule

d(f)=d_{M}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d_{L}
.
Using the assumption that
w
is a natural transformation, one
can check that
d^{2}=0
.
Hence we obtain a complex of abelian groups, denoted by

\Hom_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
.

In the
DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
of factorizations
of
w
, the factorizations are the objects, and

\Hom_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})
is the complex
of morphisms from
\boldsymbol{L}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
to
\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
We denote by
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w))

the additive category of factorizations and closed morphisms
of degree
0
between them, and by
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)=\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w))
the homotopy category of factorizations
of
w
.

The DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
always has shifts and twists.
When
\mathsf{A}
is an additive category, the DG-category

\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
is additive as well; hence it also has cones,
and the homotopy category
\mathsf{K}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
is triangulated in
this case.
When an additive category
\mathsf{A}
is idempotent-complete, so is
the DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
.
The DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
has infinite products or
coproducts whenever the category
\mathsf{A}
has.
When
\mathsf{A}
is an abelian category, so is
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
.

Remark 2.7
.

The more conventional notion of factorizations in the sense
of
[
5
]
can be recovered as a particular case of the definition
above in the following way.

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a preadditive category and
\Delta\colon\mathsf{B}\longrightarrow\mathsf{B}

be an autoequivalence.
Let
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}/2p
be a grading group as above.
In the latter case, assume that a natural isomorphism

\theta\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{B}}\longrightarrow\Delta^{p}
is given and the equation

\theta_{\Delta(A)}=\Delta(\theta_{A})
is satisfied for all
A\in\mathsf{B}
.
Then the subgroup
2\Gamma\subset\Gamma
acts on the category
\mathsf{B}

by powers of the functor
\Delta
: for every
n\in 2\Gamma
one
can define the functor denoted formally by
\Delta^{n/2}\colon\mathsf{B}\longrightarrow\mathsf{B}
, together with the natural isomorphisms of unitality
and multiplicativity similar to the ones above,
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{B}}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Delta^{0/2}
and
\Delta^{n/2}\circ\Delta^{m/2}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Delta^{(n+m)/2}
for all
n
,
m\in 2\Gamma
.
A
potential

v
for
\Delta
is a natural transformation

v\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{B}}\longrightarrow\Delta
satifying the equation
v_{\Delta(A)}=\Delta(v_{A})
for all
A\in\mathsf{B}
.

A
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}
in
\mathsf{B}
is
a collection of objects
(A^{i}\in\mathsf{B})_{i\in\Gamma}
endowed with
isomorphisms
\delta_{A}^{i,j}\colon\Delta^{(i-j)/2}(A^{j})\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}A^{i}
defined for all
i
,
j\in\Gamma
with
i-j\in 2\Gamma
.
The equation
\delta_{A}^{i,j}\circ\Delta^{(i-j)/2}(\delta^{j,k}_{A})=\delta^{i,k}_{A}
has to be satisfied for all
i
,
j
,
k\in\Gamma

such that
i-j\in 2\Gamma\ni j-k
.
The shift
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n]
of a
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}

by a degree
n\in\Gamma
is a
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object defined
by the rules
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n]^{i}=A^{n+i}
and
\delta_{A[n]}^{i,j}=\delta_{A}^{n+i,n+j}
for all
n
,
i
,
j\in\Gamma
.
The preadditive category
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
and the graded category

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
of
2
-
\Delta
-periodic objects in
\mathsf{B}
are
defined similarly to the constructions above.

In particular, the collection of all objects

(A^{i}\in\mathsf{B})_{i\in\Gamma}
with the periodicity isomorphisms

\delta_{A}^{i,j}
forgotten defines the underlying graded object

A^{*}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{B})
of a
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
.
For any two objects
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}
and
B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
,
the abelian group
\Hom_{\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
is defined
as the subgroup in the group
\Hom_{\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{B})}(A^{*},B^{*})
consisting of
all the morphisms
(f_{i}\colon A^{i}\to B^{i})_{i\in\Gamma}
satisfying
the equations
\delta^{i,j}_{B}\circ\Delta^{(i-j)/2}(f_{j})=f_{i}\circ\delta^{i,j}_{A}
for all
i
,
j\in\Gamma
with
i-j\in 2\Gamma
.
The category
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
is equivalent to the Cartesian
product
\mathsf{B}^{\Gamma/2\Gamma}=\mathsf{B}\times\mathsf{B}
of two copies of
the preadditive category
\mathsf{B}
indexed by representatives of
the cosets of the group
\Gamma
modulo
2\Gamma
.

The graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)}^{*}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})

of morphisms in the graded category
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
is a homogeneous
subgroup of the graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{B})}(A^{*},B^{*})

defined by the rule
\Hom^{n}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})=\Hom_{\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},B^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[n])
for all
n\in\Gamma
.
Unlike the preadditive category
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
, the graded
category
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
cannot be recovered from the category
\mathsf{B}

alone; it depends on the autoequivalence
\Delta
.

A
factorization

\boldsymbol{N}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{N})
of the potential
v

(in the sense of
[
5
]
) is a
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object
endowed with a homogeneous endomorphism of degree
1
, i. e.,

d_{N}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)}(N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},N^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
, such that, for
every
n\in\Gamma
, the composition
(\delta_{N}^{n+2,n})^{-1}d_{N,n+1}d_{N,n}\colon N^{n}\longrightarrow N^{n+2}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\Delta(N^{n})

is equal to the morphism
v_{N^{n}}\colon N^{n}\longrightarrow\Delta(N^{n})
.
The DG-category
\mathbf{F}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta,v)
of factorizations of
v
in
the sense of
[
5
]
is defined similarly to the construction
above in the main body of this section.

Now consider the Cartesian square
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{B}\times\mathsf{B}
of
the preadditive category
\mathsf{B}
.
Define the autoequivalence
\Lambda\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
by the rule

\Lambda(B^{0},B^{1})=(B^{1},\Delta(B^{0}))
for all
B^{0}
,
B^{1}\in\mathsf{B}
.
Then one has
\Lambda^{2p}\simeq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}
whenever
\Delta^{p}\simeq\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{B}}
.
Define the potential
w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
by the rule

w_{(B^{0},B^{1})}=(v_{B^{0}},v_{B^{1}})\colon(B^{0},B^{1})\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(B^{0},B^{1})=(\Delta(B^{0}),\Delta(B^{1}))
.

Then the additive category
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
is naturally equivalent
to
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, the graded category
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
is
naturally equivalent to
\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, and the DG-category

\mathbf{F}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta,v)
is naturally equivalent to the DG-category

\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
.
These equivalences assign to a
2
-
\Delta
-periodic object

B^{\circ}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathsf{B},\Delta)
the
\Lambda
-periodic object

X^{\circ}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
with the components
X^{i}=(B^{i},B^{i+1})

for all
i\in\Gamma
.

3.
The Almost Involution on DG-Categories

The construction assigning the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
to
a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is presented below in
Section
3.2
.
In the important example of the DG-category of CDG-modules over
a CDG-ring, this construction is closely related to the construction
assigning the acyclic DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to a CDG-ring

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Before proceeding to introduce the main category-theoretic
construction, we start with Section
3.1

discussing the related construction for curved DG-rings.

The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
comes together with a ladder of adjunctions
connecting the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
We construct these adjoint functors in the case of CDG-modules over
CDG-rings in Proposition
3.1
.

Furthermore, the counterexamples presented in
Examples
3.2
–
3.3
in
Section
3.1
demonstrate that the full
subcategory
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}\subset R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
misses many objects
and is not well-behaved in general.
This illustrates the need for the construction of the DG-category

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural}
as a way to eventually recover the whole
abelian category
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\simeq\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
,
as per Example
3.17

in Section
3.4
.

3.1.
CDG-modules revisited

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring.
The following construction plays an important role.
One adjoins an element
\delta
of degree
1
to
R^{*}
and imposes
the relations

•

\delta r-(-1)^{|r|}r\delta=d(r)
for all
r\in R^{|r|}
;

•

\delta^{2}=h
.

The resulting graded ring, spanned by
R^{*}
and
\delta
with
the relations above, is denoted by
R^{*}[\delta]
.
Explicitly, the elements of degree
n
in
R^{*}[\delta]
are formal
linear combinations
r+s\delta
with
r\in R^{n}
and
s\in R^{n-1}
.
The addition in
R^{*}[\delta]
is defined in the obvious way,
and the multiplication is defined in the way dictated by
the relations above, namely

(r+s\delta)(u+v\delta)=(ru+sd(u)+(-1)^{|v|}svh)+(rv+(-1)^{|u|}su+sd(v))\delta.

The graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
is endowed with an odd derivation

\partial=\partial/\partial\delta
of degree
-1
, defined by the rules
\partial(r)=0

for
r\in R^{*}\subset R^{*}[\delta]
and
\partial(\delta)=1
.
Explicitly,
\partial(r+s\delta)=(-1)^{|s|}s
.
We will denote by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*}
the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]

with the odd derivation
\partial
and the changed sign of the grading:
so
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{n}=R^{*}[\delta]^{-n}
.

One has
\partial^{2}=0
, so
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*},\partial)
is
a DG-ring.
Moreover, the cohomology ring
H^{*}_{\partial}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of
the DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
vanishes (since the unit
1
belongs
to the image of
\partial
).
Both the kernel and the image of the odd derivation
\partial
on

R^{*}[\delta]
coincide with the subring
R^{*}\subset R^{*}[\delta]
.
So the map
\partial
decomposes as
R^{*}[\delta]\twoheadrightarrow R^{*}\rightarrowtail R^{*}[\delta]
.

We denote the resulting surjective map by
\rho\colon R^{*}[\delta]\twoheadrightarrow R^{*}
.
So
\rho
is a surjective homogeneous map of degree
-1

respecting the graded
R^{*}
-
R^{*}
-bimodule structures.
(Notice that there a sign rule involved in the notion of
a homogeneous map of graded left modules or the shift of a graded
left module; see Section
2.2
.)

The correspondence assigning the DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to
a CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
is an equivalence of categories
between the category of CDG-rings and the category of DG-rings with
vanishing cohomology
[
45
, Section 4.2]
.

We recall the notation
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
for the abelian category of
graded
R^{*}
-modules (and homogeneous morphisms of degree
0

between them), as well as the notation
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
for
the abelian category of CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
(and closed
morphisms of degree
0
between them); see
Sections
1.3

and
2.2
.
There is an obvious exact, faithful forgetful functor

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})\longrightarrow R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
assigning to a CDG-module

\boldsymbol{M}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(M^{*},d_{M})
its underlying graded module
M^{*}
.
We are interested in the left and right adjoint functors to
the forgetful functor.

In the case when
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is a DG-ring, the assertions
of the following proposition can be found in
[
24
, proof of
Lemma 2.2]
.

Proposition 3.1
.

(a)
The forgetful functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})\longrightarrow R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
has an exact, faithful left adjoint functor

G^{+}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
and an exact, faithful
right adjoint functor
G^{-}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
.

(b)
For any graded left
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
, there are
natural short exact sequences of graded left
R^{*}
-modules

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow M^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G^{+}(M^{*})\longrightarrow M^{*}[-1]\longrightarrow 0,

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow M^{*}[1]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow G^{-}(M^{*})\longrightarrow M^{*}\longrightarrow 0.

(c)
The functors
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
only differ from each
other by a shift: for any graded left
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
,
there is a natural closed isomorphism of degree
0
between
the two CDG-modules

G^{-}(M^{*})\simeq G^{+}(M^{*})[1]

transforming the two short exact sequences in (b) into each other.

Proof.

In order to describe the two adjoint functors, observe that
the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
is naturally equivalent
(in fact, isomorphic) to the abelian category of graded modules
over the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
, that is

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})\simeq R^{*}[\delta]{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
The equivalence assigns to a CDG-module
(M^{*},d_{M})
over

(R^{*},d,h)
the graded module
M^{*}
over
R^{*}[\delta]
with
the action of
R^{*}
in
M^{*}
unchanged and the action of
\delta

in
M^{*}
given by the obvious rule
\delta x=d_{M}(x)
for
all
x\in M^{*}
.
Taking this equivalence of abelian categories into account,
the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})\longrightarrow R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is
interpreted as the functor of restriction of scalars

R^{*}[\delta]{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
corresponding to
the inclusion morphism of graded rings
R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}[\delta]
.

Now it is clear how to construct the adjoint functors.
The functor
G^{+}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
left adjoint
to the forgetful functor takes a graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
to
the CDG-module corresponding to the graded
R^{*}[\delta]
-module

R^{*}[\delta]\otimes_{R^{*}}M^{*}
.
The functor
G^{-}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
right adjoint
to the forgetful functor takes a graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
to
the CDG-module corresponding to the graded
R^{*}[\delta]
-module

\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}(R^{*}[\delta],M^{*})
.
A more explicit description of the functors
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
can be
found in
[
35
, proof of Theorem 3.6]
.

There is a natural short exact sequence of graded

R^{*}
-
R^{*}
-bimodules

(1)

0\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muR^{*}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muR^{*}[\delta]\overset{\rho}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}R^{*}[-1]\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu0.

All the terms of (
1
) are finitely generated
projective (in fact, free) both as graded left
R^{*}
-modules
and as graded right
R^{*}
-modules.
All the assertions of (a–b) follow from that.
In particular, the short exact sequences in (b) are induced
by (
1
).
The functors
G^{+}
and
G^{-}
are faithful, because any graded
left
R^{*}
-module
M
is naturally a submodule in
G^{+}(M)
and
a quotient module of
G^{-}(M)
.

The natural isomorphism in (c) is constructed as the composition

\Hom_{R^{*}}(R^{*}[\delta],M^{*})\simeq\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}(R^{*}[\delta],R^{*})\otimes_{R^{*}}M^{*}\simeq R^{*}[\delta][1]\otimes_{R^{*}}M^{*},

where the leftmost isomorphism holds because
R^{*}[\delta]
is
a finitely generated projective graded left
R^{*}
-module, while
the rightmost isomorphism is induced by the following natural
isomorphism of graded
R^{*}
-
R^{*}
-modules (in fact, of graded

R^{*}[\delta]
-
R^{*}
-bimodules)

\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}(R^{*}[\delta],R^{*})\simeq R^{*}[\delta][1]
.
Consider the composition

(2)

R^{*}[\delta]\otimes_{R^{*}}R^{*}[\delta]\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muR^{*}[\delta]\overset{\rho}{\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu}R^{*}[-1]

of the multiplication map
R^{*}[\delta]\otimes_{R^{*}}R^{*}[\delta]\longrightarrow R^{*}[\delta]
and the surjective map
\rho\colon R^{*}[\delta]\longrightarrow R^{*}[-1]
.
One easily checks that the
R^{*}
-
R^{*}
-bimodule map

R^{*}[\delta]\otimes_{R^{*}}R^{*}[\delta]\longrightarrow R^{*}[-1]

(
2
) is a “perfect pairing”, in the sense that
both the induced
R^{*}
-
R^{*}
-bimodule morphisms
R^{*}[\delta]\longrightarrow\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}(R^{*}[\delta],R^{*})[-1]
and
R^{*}[\delta]\longrightarrow\Hom_{R^{*}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{*}(R^{*}[\delta],R^{*})[-1]
are isomorphisms.
Here
\Hom_{R^{*}}^{*}({-},{-})
is the graded abelian group of morphisms
of graded left
R^{*}
-modules, while
\Hom_{R^{*}{}^{\mathrm{op}}}^{*}
denotes
the graded abelian group of morphisms of graded right
R^{*}
-modules.
∎

Our next aim is to illustrate the fact, mentioned in
Section
2.2
, that not every graded

R^{*}
-module admits a structure of CDG-module over
(R^{*},d,h)
.
For this purpose, we will present and discuss two classes
of examples.

Examples 3.2
.

Take
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, and let
R^{*}=k[x,x^{-1}]
be the graded ring of
Laurent polynomials in one variable
x
of cohomological degree

\deg x=2
over a field
k
.
Endow
R^{*}
with the zero differential
d=0
and the curvature
element
h=x
.
Then
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
is a CDG-ring.
The free
R
-module with one generator
M^{*}=R^{*}
does
not

admit a CDG-module structure over the CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Indeed, any homogeneous differential
d_{M}\colon M^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
of
degree
1
vanishes for the simple reason of
M^{*}
being concentrated
in the even cohomological degrees.
Hence the squared differential
d_{M}^{2}\colon M^{*}\longrightarrow M^{*}
also vanishes.
This contradicts the equation (iii) in the definition of a CDG-module
in Section
2.2
, as the action of
the curvature element
h=x
in
M^{*}
is nonzero.
This example admits some obvious variations: one can take

R^{*}=k[x]
instead of
k[x,x^{-1}]
, and
M^{*}=k[x]
or
k[x,x^{-1}]

or
k[x,x^{-1}]/k[x]
.

Notice that
k[x,x^{-1}]
is both a projective and an injective
module over the graded ring
k[x,x^{-1}]
.
Over the graded ring
k[x]
, the graded module
k[x]
is projective,
while the graded module
k[x,x^{-1}]/k[x]
is injective and
the graded module
k[x,x^{-1}]
is (flat and) injective.
Thus in all these examples the graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
is either
projective or injective.
Hence both the short exact sequences of
R^{*}
-modules in
Proposition
3.1
(b) split for the graded

R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
, and
M^{*}
is a direct summand of the graded

R^{*}
-modules
G^{+}(M^{*})
and
G^{-}(M^{*})
which come endowed with
structures of CDG-modules over
(R^{*},d,h)
.
Thus the class of all graded
R^{*}
-modules admitting a CDG-module
structure over the given CDG-ring
(R^{*},d,h)
is
not
closed
under direct summands, generally speaking.

We will see below in Example
3.17
that for
any CDG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
there is an acyclic DG-ring
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

such that the DG-category of (left) CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

is equivalent to the DG-category of DG-modules over
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,
that is
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}\simeq\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
.
Consequently, Examples
3.2

imply that, even for an acyclic DG-ring
\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, the class of
all graded
S^{*}
-modues admitting a DG-module structure over

\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
need not be closed under direct summands.

Examples 3.3
.

Take again
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, and let
R^{*}=k[\epsilon,x]
be the free
graded commutative ring generated by two elements
\epsilon\in R^{-1}

and
x\in R^{0}
.
So the relations
x\epsilon=\epsilon x
and
\epsilon^{2}=0
are
imposed in
R^{*}
.
Let
d\colon R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}
be the odd derivation of degree
1
defined
by the rules
d(\epsilon)=x
and
d(x)=0
.
Then we have
d^{2}=0
; so
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*},d)
is a DG-ring.
One can consider it as a CDG-ring with
h=0
, if one wishes.

Let
M^{*}
be a graded
R^{*}
-module annihilated by
\epsilon
,
that is
\epsilon M^{*}=0
.
If
M^{*}
is also annihilated by
x
, that is
xM^{*}=0
, then one can
introduce the differential
d_{M}=0
on
M^{*}
, making
(M^{*},d_{M})

a DG-module over
(R^{*},d)
.
Let us show that if
M^{*}
is annihilated by
\epsilon
but not by
x

(e. g.,
M^{*}=R^{*}/R^{*}\epsilon
or
M^{*}=R^{*}/(R^{*}\epsilon+R^{*}x^{2})
),
then the graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
is
not
a direct summand
of any graded
R^{*}
-module
N^{*}
admitting a DG-module structure

(N^{*},d_{N})
over
(R^{*},d)
.

Indeed, let
(N^{*},d_{N})
be a DG-module over
(R,d)
such that

N^{*}\simeq M^{*}\oplus L^{*}
as a graded
R^{*}
-module.
Let
m\in M^{*}
be a homogeneous element for which
xm\neq 0
.
Then
d_{N}(m,0)
is some homogeneous element in
N^{*}
;
denote it by
d_{N}(m,0)=(m^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, where
m^{\prime}\in M^{*}
and
l^{\prime}\in L^{*}
.
Now we have (since
\epsilon M^{*}=0
by assumption):

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=d_{N}(0,0)=d_{N}(\epsilon m,0)=d_{N}(\epsilon(m,0))=d(\epsilon)(m,0)-\epsilon d_{N}(m,0)

\displaystyle=x(m,0)-\epsilon(m^{\prime},l^{\prime})=(xm,0)-(\epsilon m^{\prime},\epsilon l^{\prime})=(xm,0)-(0,\epsilon l^{\prime})=(xm,-\epsilon l^{\prime})\neq 0.

The contradiction proves that no such DG-module
(N^{*},d_{N})
exists.

Notice that
(R^{*},d)
, as any DG-ring, is a DG-module over itself.
Futhermore, the injective graded
R^{*}
-module
\Hom_{k}(R^{*},k)
,
endowed with the differential induced by
d
, becomes a DG-module
over
(R^{*},d)
.
Now these examples show that the class of all graded
R^{*}
-modules
which can be obtained as direct summands of graded
R^{*}
-modules
admitting a DG-module structure over
(R^{*},d)
is
not
closed
either under extensions, or under cokernels, or under kernels
in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.

Furthermore, let us drop the equation
\epsilon^{2}=0
from the list
of defining relations of the graded
k
-algebra
R^{*}
(keeping only
the relation
x\epsilon=\epsilon x
).
Then the graded ring
R^{*}
is no longer graded commutative, but
otherwise all the claims and arguments above in this example
remain applicable with this modification.
In this new context, we have short exact sequences of graded left

R^{*}
-modules
0\longrightarrow R^{*}[1]\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}R^{*}\longrightarrow R^{*}/R^{*}\epsilon\longrightarrow 0
and
0\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(R^{*}/\epsilon R^{*},k)\longrightarrow\Hom_{k}(R^{*},k)\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}\Hom_{k}(R^{*},k)\longrightarrow 0
.
It follows that the class of all graded
R^{*}
-modules admitting
a DG-module structure over
(R^{*},d)
is not even closed under
the cokernels of monomorphisms or under the kernels of epimorphisms,
in this case.

Finally, one can observe that the category
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}

of all graded
R^{*}
-modules admitting a DG-module structure over

(R^{*},d)
is not abelian, and in fact, the monomorphism

R^{*}[1]\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}R^{*}
in the latter example has

no
cokernel in
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
.
Indeed, there are clearly enough injective objects in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}

belonging to
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
; in particular, one can find
an injective graded
R^{*}
-module
J^{*}
admitting a DG-module
structure
(J^{*},d_{J}^{*})
over
(R^{*},d)
such that
R^{*}/R^{*}\epsilon

is a graded
R^{*}
-submodule in
J^{*}
.
So there is a right exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R^{*}[1]\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}R^{*}\longrightarrow J^{*}
in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that the morphism

R^{*}[1]\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}R^{*}
has a cokernel
L^{*}
in

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
.
Then the monomorphism
R^{*}/R^{*}\epsilon\longrightarrow J^{*}
factorizes
through the object
L^{*}
in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, and it follows that
the action of
x
in
L^{*}
is nonzero.
Consequently, the action of
\epsilon
in
L^{*}
has to be nonzero
as well.
Now the map
f\colon L^{*}\longrightarrow L^{*}[-1]
defined by the rule

f(l)=(-1)^{|l|}\epsilon l
is a morphism of graded
R^{*}
-modules,
and this morphism is nonzero.
But the composition
R^{*}\longrightarrow L^{*}\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}L^{*}[-1]
vanishes,
so the morphism
R^{*}\longrightarrow L^{*}
is
not
an epimorphism and
consequently not a cokernel in
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
.
Similarly one can show that the epimorphism
\Hom_{k}(R^{*},k)\overset{*\epsilon}{\longrightarrow}\Hom_{k}(R^{*},k)
has no kernel
in
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{0}
.

For another somewhat similar (but simpler) example, see
Example
4.28
below.

3.2.
Main Construction

The aim of this section is to spell out the construction and results
of
[
35
, Section 3.2]
with sufficient amount of detail.
This construction plays a fundamental role in the theory developed
in this paper.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a DG-category.
The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is constructed as follows.
The objects of
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
are pairs
X^{\natural}=(X,\sigma_{X})
, where
X

is an object of
\mathbf{A}
and
\sigma_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(X,X)
is a cochain
in the complex of endomorphisms such that
d(\sigma_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{X}

and
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0
.
So
\sigma_{X}
is a chosen contracting homotopy for
X
with
zero square.

Given two objects
X^{\natural}=(X,\sigma_{X})
and
Y^{\natural}=(Y,\sigma_{Y})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, the complex of morphisms

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is constructed as follows.
For every
i\in\Gamma
, the abelian group
\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is the group of all closed morphisms

X\longrightarrow Y
of degree
-i
in
\mathbf{A}
, that is

\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})=\Hom^{-i}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})}(X,Y)
.
The differential
d^{\natural}\colon\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})\longrightarrow\Hom^{i+1}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is defined as the graded
commutator with the contracting homotopies
\sigma
; explicitly,
it is given by the formula
d^{\natural}(f)=\sigma_{Y}f-(-1)^{i}f\sigma_{X}
.
One computes that

d(\sigma_{Y}f-(-1)^{i}f\sigma_{X})=d(\sigma_{Y})f-\sigma_{Y}d(f)\\
-(-1)^{i}d(f)\sigma_{X}-fd(\sigma_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{Y}f-f\mathrm{id}_{X}=0,

so
d^{\natural}(f)\in\Hom^{i+1}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
whenever

f\in\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
(as desired).
Furthermore,
(d^{\natural})^{2}=0
, since
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0=\sigma_{Y}^{2}
.
The composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is induced by
the composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
, and the identity morphisms
in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
are the identity morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.

All twists always exist in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
Indeed, let
a\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}^{1}(X^{\natural},X^{\natural})
be
a Maurer–Cartan cochain in the complex of endomorphisms of
an object
X^{\natural}=(X,\sigma_{X})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
; this means that

a\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(X,X)
,
d(a)=0
, and

d^{\natural}(a)+a^{2}=\sigma_{X}a+a\sigma_{X}+a^{2}=0
in
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-2}(X,X)
.
Then the object
X^{\natural}(a)\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
can be constructed as

X^{\natural}(a)=(X,\sigma_{X}+a)
.

The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
has shifts whenever a DG-category

\mathbf{A}
has.
The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is additive whenever a DG-category

\mathbf{A}
is.
Assuming
\mathbf{A}
is additive, the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is
idempotent-complete whenever the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is.
The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
has infinite coproducts or products
whenever a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has.
All these operations in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
are induced
by the respective operations in
\mathbf{A}
(the only caveat is that
the shift
[1]
on
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is induced by the inverse
shift
[-1]
on
\mathbf{A}
).

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then there is a faithful additive functor
\Phi=\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
given by the rule
A\longmapsto\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])

for all objects
A\in\mathbf{A}
.
The functor
\Phi
has faithful adjoint functors on both sides, with
the left adjoint
\Psi^{+}=\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

and the right adjoint
\Psi^{-}=\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
given by
\Psi^{+}(X,\sigma_{X})=X
and

\Psi^{-}(X,\sigma_{X})=X[1]
(so the functors
\Psi^{+}
and
\Psi^{-}

only differ from each other by a shift).

Proof.

Let us make our notation more explicit.
Given an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
, the object
E=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])

is specified by the datum of closed morphisms

\iota\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,E)
and
\pi\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(E,A)

and morphisms
\pi^{\prime}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(E,A)
and

\iota^{\prime}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,E)
satisfying the equations

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}\iota^{\prime}=0=\pi\iota,\quad\pi^{\prime}\iota=\mathrm{id}_{A}=\pi\iota^{\prime},\quad\iota\pi^{\prime}+\iota^{\prime}\pi=\mathrm{id}_{E},

\displaystyle d(\pi^{\prime})=\pi,\quad d(\iota^{\prime})=\iota.

The endomorphism
\sigma_{E}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(E,E)
is given by
the formula
\sigma_{E}=\iota^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}
.
Then
\sigma_{E}^{2}=0
and
d(\sigma_{E})=d(\iota^{\prime})\pi^{\prime}+\iota^{\prime}d(\pi^{\prime})=\iota\pi^{\prime}+\iota^{\prime}\pi=\mathrm{id}_{E}
; so
E^{\natural}=(E,\sigma_{E})
is an object
of
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
We set
\Phi(A)=E^{\natural}
.
The action of the functor
\Phi
on morphisms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

(i. e., on the closed morphisms of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
) is defined
in the obvious way.

The construction of the functors
\Psi^{+}
and
\Psi^{-}
needs
no additional explanations.
It is also clear that the functors
\Psi^{+}
,
\Psi^{-}
, and
\Phi

are faithful.

Let us explain the adjunctions.
Given two objects
X^{\natural}=(X,\sigma_{X})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
and
A\in\mathbf{A}
,
the natural isomorphism between the abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,A)
and
\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}(X^{\natural},E^{\natural})

assigns to a morphism
f\colon X\longrightarrow A
the morphism
g\colon X\longrightarrow E

given by the formula
g=\iota^{\prime}f+\iota f\sigma_{X}
.
Conversely, to a morphism
g\colon X\longrightarrow E
the morphism

f=\pi g
is assigned.
The natural isomorphism between the abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(A,X[1])
and
\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}(E^{\natural},X^{\natural})

assigns to a closed morphism
f\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(A,X)
the closed
morphism
g=f\pi^{\prime}+\sigma_{X}f\pi\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}^{0}(E^{\natural},X^{\natural})
.
Conversely, to a morphism
g\colon E\longrightarrow X
the morphism

f=g\iota
is assigned.
∎

Now we consider what happens when one applies the construction

\mathbf{A}\longmapsto\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
twice.

Proposition 3.5
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, there is
a fully faithful DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}

given by the rule
A\longmapsto\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
.
The DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is an equivalence
of DG-categories whenever
\mathbf{A}
is an idempotent-complete additive
DG-category with twists.

Proof.

Let us first describe the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
more
explicitly.
The objects of
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
are triples
W^{\natural\natural}=(W,\sigma,\tau)
, where
W
is an object of
\mathbf{A}
, while
\sigma\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(W,W)
and
\tau\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(W,W)
are two
endomorphisms of the degrees
-1
and
1
, respectively, satisfying
the following equations:

\sigma^{2}=0=\tau^{2},\quad\sigma\tau+\tau\sigma=\mathrm{id}_{W},\quad d(\sigma)=\mathrm{id}_{W},\quad d(\tau)=0.

Here
W^{\natural}=(W,\sigma)
is an object of
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, and

\tau\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}^{-1}(W^{\natural},W^{\natural})
is an endomorphism
with
d^{\natural}(\tau)=\mathrm{id}_{W}
(and
\tau^{2}=0
).

Given two objects
U^{\natural\natural}=(U,\sigma_{U},\tau_{U})
and

V^{\natural\natural}=(V,\sigma_{V},\tau_{V})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
, the complex
of morphisms
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(U^{\natural\natural},V^{\natural\natural})

has the term
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}}^{i}(U^{\natural\natural},V^{\natural\natural})

which is a subgroup in
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i}(U,V)
consisting of all
the morphisms
f\colon U\longrightarrow V
of degree
i
such that

d(f)=0
and
d^{\natural}(f)=\sigma_{V}f-(-1)^{i}f\sigma_{U}=0
.
The differential
d^{\natural\natural}
on

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(U^{\natural\natural},V^{\natural\natural})
is given
by the formula
d^{\natural\natural}(f)=\tau_{V}f-(-1)^{|f|}f\tau_{U}
.

Let us explain the basic intuition behind the assertions of
the proposition.
Leaving the differential structures aside, one can say that
the objects of
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
are representations of the graded
ring of
2\times 2
matrices (with the entries in
\mathbb{Z}
) in
the graded category
\mathbf{A}^{*}
.
The endomorphisms
\sigma
and
\tau
, subject to the equations
above, define an action of
2\times 2
matrices in the object
W
.
The basic Morita theory tells that the category of representations
of a ring of matrices in a given additive category
\mathsf{A}
is
equivalent to
\mathsf{A}
, under mild assumptions on
\mathsf{A}
.
This is a bare-bones version of our argument below.

The DG-functor
\natural\natural
assigns to an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
the object

E=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
endowed with the endomorphisms
\sigma_{E}=\iota^{\prime}\pi^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(E,E)
and
\tau_{E}=\iota\pi\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(E,E)

(in the notation from the previous proof).
So
\natural\natural(A)=(E,\sigma_{E},\tau_{E})
.

Now suppose that we are given two objects
A
and
B\in\mathbf{A}
; put

E=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
and
F=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{B}[-1])
, and let

\iota_{A}
,
\pi_{A}
,
\iota^{\prime}_{A}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{A}
and
\iota_{B}
,
\pi_{B}
,

\iota^{\prime}_{B}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{B}
denote the related morphisms.
Then the functor
\natural\natural
assigns to a morphism

f\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i}(A,B)
the morphism
g=\natural\natural(f)\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i}(E,F)

given by the formula

(3)

g=(-1)^{i}\iota^{\prime}_{B}f\pi_{A}+\iota_{B}f\pi^{\prime}_{A}+\iota^{\prime}_{B}d(f)\pi^{\prime}_{A}.

One readily checks that
d(g)=0
,
d^{\natural}(g)=\sigma_{F}g-(-1)^{i}g\sigma_{E}=0
, and
d^{\natural\natural}(g)=\tau_{F}g-(-1)^{i}g\tau_{E}=\natural\natural(df)
.
The equation of compatibility with the compositions,

\natural\natural(f_{1}\circ f_{2})=\natural\natural(f_{1})\circ\natural\natural(f_{2})
for any
two composable morphisms
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
(of some degrees
i_{1}

and
i_{2}
) in
\mathbf{A}
also needs to be checked.

Clearly, the map of abelian groups
\natural\natural\colon\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,B)\longrightarrow\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}}(E,F)
is injective.
To check surjectivity, consider a morphism
g\in\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}}(E,F)
.
Then one has
g=\iota^{\prime}_{B}g_{00}\pi_{A}+\iota^{\prime}_{B}g_{01}\pi^{\prime}_{A}+\iota_{B}g_{10}\pi_{B}+\iota_{B}g_{11}\pi^{\prime}_{B}
for certain (uniquely
defined) morphisms
g_{st}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{i-s+t}(A,B)
, where

s
,
t\in\{0,1\}
.
Then (computing the
00
-component of) the equation
d^{\natural}(g)=0

implies
g_{10}=0
, and (computing the
01
-component of)
the same equation implies
g_{00}=(-1)^{i}g_{11}
.
The equation
d(g)=0
implies
(-1)^{i}d(g_{00})=g_{01}=d(g_{11})
.
Thus the DG-functor
\natural\natural
induces an isomorphism of
the complexes of morphisms
\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,B)\simeq\Hom^{i}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}}(\natural\natural(A),\natural\natural(B))
for all
objects
A
,
B\in\mathbf{A}
.

Finally, assume that
\mathbf{A}
is an idempotent-complete additive
DG-category with twists, and let
(W,\sigma,\tau)\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}

be an object.
Then, since
d(\tau)=0=\tau^{2}
, the element
-\tau\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(W,W)

is a Maurer–Cartan cochain.
Consider the related twist
U=W(-\tau)
.
Then the graded abelian group
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{*}}^{*}(U,U)
is naturally
identified with
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{*}}^{*}(W,W)
, while the differentials on
the two complexes
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(U,U)
and
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(W,W)
are
connected by the formula
d_{U}=d_{W}-[\tau,{-}]
, or more explicitly

d_{U}(f)=d_{W}(f)-\tau f+(-1)^{|f|}f\tau
.

Now we have
d_{U}(\sigma)=d_{U}(\tau)=0
, so
\sigma\tau\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(U,U)
is a closed endomorphism of degree
0
.
Furthermore,
(\sigma\tau)^{2}=(\sigma\tau+\tau\sigma)\sigma\tau=\sigma\tau
.
So
\sigma\tau\colon U\longrightarrow U
is an idempotent endomorphism of
the object
U\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The image
A
of this idempotent endomorphism recovers an object

A\in\mathbf{A}
such that
W=\natural\natural(A)
.
In fact, the image of the complementary idempotent

\tau\sigma=\mathrm{id}-\sigma\tau
is isomorphic to
A[-1]
as an object
of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
; the endomorphisms
\sigma
and
\tau
provide
the requisite closed isomorphism.
So we have
U=A\oplus A[-1]
and
W=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
in
\mathbf{A}
.
∎

3.3.
Properties of the main construction

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the DG-category

\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
only differs from
\mathbf{A}
by adjoining all twists and
some of their direct summands.
The aim of this section is to explain the meaning of this (somewhat
informal) statement.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the composition
of the additive functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural})
induced by the DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
with the additive functor

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

is naturally isomorphic to the additive functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
,

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\circ\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}.

Proof.

Follows immediately from the constructions of the respective functors
in Lemma
3.4

and Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the composition
of the additive functor
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

with the additive functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural})
induced by the DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is naturally isomorphic to the additive
functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural})
,

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}.

Proof.

Let
(X,\sigma_{X})
be an object of the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}

(so
\sigma_{X}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X)
and
d(\sigma_{X})=\mathrm{id}_{X}
,
while
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0
).
Then
\Psi^{-}(X,\sigma_{X})=X[1]\in\mathbf{A}
.
By the definition (see the proof of
Proposition
3.5
), the object

\natural\natural(X[1])\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is the triple
(E,\sigma_{E},\tau_{E})
,
where
E=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{X})\in\mathbf{A}
, while
\sigma_{E}=\iota^{\prime}_{E}\pi^{\prime}_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(E,E)
and
\tau_{E}=\iota_{E}\pi_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(E,E)
.
Here
\iota_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(X,E)
and
\pi_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(E,X)

are closed morphisms, while
\pi^{\prime}_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(E,X)
and

\iota^{\prime}_{E}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(X,E)
are morphisms satisfying
the equations similar to the ones in the proof of
Lemma
3.4
, except for one sign:

\displaystyle\pi^{\prime}_{E}\iota^{\prime}_{E}=0=\pi_{E}\iota_{E},\quad\pi^{\prime}_{E}\iota_{E}=\mathrm{id}_{X}=\pi_{E}\iota^{\prime}_{E},\quad\iota_{E}\pi^{\prime}_{E}+\iota^{\prime}_{E}\pi_{E}=\mathrm{id}_{E},

\displaystyle d(\pi^{\prime}_{E})=-\pi_{E},\quad d(\iota^{\prime}_{E})=\iota_{E}.

On the other hand, following the constructions in
Section
3.2
, the object

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X,\sigma_{X})
is the triple
(S,\sigma_{S},\tau_{S})
,
where
S=X\oplus X[1]\in\mathbf{A}
, while
\sigma_{S}=-\iota^{\prime}_{S}\sigma_{X}\pi_{S}+\iota_{S}\sigma_{X}\pi^{\prime}_{S}+\iota^{\prime}_{S}\pi^{\prime}_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(S,S)
and

\tau_{S}=\iota_{S}\pi_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(S,S)
.
Here
\iota_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(X,S)
,
\pi_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(S,X)
,

\pi^{\prime}_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(S,X)
, and
\iota^{\prime}_{S}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(X,S)

are closed morphisms satisfying the equations

\pi^{\prime}_{S}\iota^{\prime}_{S}=0=\pi_{S}\iota_{S},\quad\pi^{\prime}_{S}\iota_{S}=\mathrm{id}_{X}=\pi_{S}\iota^{\prime}_{S},\quad\iota_{S}\pi^{\prime}_{S}+\iota^{\prime}_{S}\pi_{S}=\mathrm{id}_{S}.

Now
s=\iota^{\prime}_{S}\pi_{E}+\iota_{S}\pi^{\prime}_{E}+\iota_{S}\sigma_{X}\pi_{E}\colon E\longrightarrow S
is a closed isomorphism of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}

satisfying the equations
\sigma_{S}s=s\sigma_{E}
and
\tau_{S}s=s\tau_{E}
.
The inverse (closed) isomorphism is
e=\iota^{\prime}_{E}\pi_{S}+\iota_{E}\pi^{\prime}_{S}-\iota_{E}\sigma_{X}\pi_{S}\colon S\longrightarrow E
.
∎

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, denote by

\Xi=\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
the additive functor taking
every object
A\in\mathbf{A}
to the object
\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
(and acting
on the morphisms in the obvious way).

Lemma 3.8
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the composition

\Psi_{\mathbf{A}}^{+}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
of the additive
functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is naturally isomorphic
to the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}
,

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}.

The composition
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
of the additive functor
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and the additive functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
is naturally
isomorphic to the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}
,

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}.

Proof.

The first assertion follows immediately from the constructions of
the functors in Lemma
3.4
.
A proof of the second assertion is contained in the proof of
Lemma
3.7
.
Alternatively, one can formally deduce the second assertion from
the first assertion for the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
by applying
the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}
to both sides of the natural isomorphism
in Lemma
3.7
and taking
into account Lemma
3.6
,

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\circ\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\circ\Psi_{\mathbf{A}}^{-}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\simeq\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}.

∎

Lemma 3.9
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the additive
functor
\Phi\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
can be extended
in a natural way to a fully faithful additive functor

\widetilde{\Phi}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathbf{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Similarly, the additive functors
\Psi^{+}
and

\Psi^{-}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
can be extended in a natural
way to fully faithful additive functors
\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}=\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}=\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})^{0}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
(still satisfying

\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}=\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}[1]
).

Proof.

The construction of the functor
\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}
is essentially
obvious.
By the definition, we have
\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}(X,\sigma_{X})=\Psi^{+}(X,\sigma_{X})=X
for all objects
(X,\sigma_{X})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
;
and also by definition,
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}^{0}((X,\sigma_{X}),(Y,\sigma_{Y}))=\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})}^{0}(X,Y)=\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,Y)
for all

(X,\sigma_{X})
and
(Y,\sigma_{Y})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
This natural isomorphism of the Hom groups provides the action of
the functor
\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}
on morphisms.
The functor
\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}
is defined as
\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}=\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}[1]
.

Concerning the functor
\widetilde{\Phi}
, let
A
and
B\in\mathbf{A}
be
two objects, and let
\Phi(A)=(E,\sigma_{E})
and
\Phi(B)=(F,\sigma_{F})

be their images in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
Let
f\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,B)
be a (not necessarily closed) morphism
of degree
0
.
Then we put
\widetilde{\Phi}(f)=g
, where
g\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(E,F)
is
the morphism constructed in the proof of
Proposition
3.5
(take
i=0
in
the formula (
3
)).
Following the arguments in the proof of
Proposition
3.5
, this assignment is
a well-defined isomorphism of abelian groups
\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,B)\simeq\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}(E,F)
compatible with the compositions of
morphisms; hence the fully faithful additive functor
\widetilde{\Phi}
.
Alternatively, one can refer to
Lemma
3.6
and simply
put
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}=\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\circ(\natural\natural)^{0}
.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

Both the isomorphisms of additive functors in
Lemmas
3.6

and
3.7
remain
valid for the extended functors from
Lemma
3.9
, i. e., one has

\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}\circ(\natural\natural)^{0}\simeq\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\natural\natural)\circ\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}.

The former isomorphism was essentially explained already in
the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
The latter one is verified by a direct computation continuing
the computations in the proof of
Lemma
3.7
.
Let
(X,\sigma_{X})
and
(Y,\sigma_{Y})
be two objects of
the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, and let
z\colon(X,\sigma_{X})\longrightarrow(Y,\sigma_{Y})
be (not necessarily closed) morphism of degree
0

in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
; in other words,
z\colon X\longrightarrow Y
is a closed
morphism of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
.
Put
(E,\sigma_{E},\tau_{E})=\natural\natural(X)
and
(F,\sigma_{F},\tau_{F})=\natural\natural(Y)
; then the morphism
g=\natural\natural(z)\colon E\longrightarrow F
is given
by the rule
g=\iota^{\prime}_{F}z\pi_{E}+\iota_{F}z\pi^{\prime}_{E}
.
Furthermore, put
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(X,\sigma_{X})=(S,\sigma_{S},\tau_{S})

and
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(Y,\sigma_{Y})=(T,\sigma_{T},\tau_{T})
; then
the morphism
u=\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(z)\colon S\longrightarrow T
is given by
the formula
u=\iota^{\prime}_{T}z\pi_{S}+\iota_{T}z\pi^{\prime}_{S}+\iota_{T}(\sigma_{Y}z-z\sigma_{X})\pi_{S}
.
Then one can readily check that
tg=us
, or equivalently,
ge=fu
,
where the mutually inverse closed isomorphisms
s
and
e
were
defined in the proof of
Lemma
3.7
, while
the mutually inverse closed isomorphisms
t\colon F\longrightarrow T
and

f\colon T\longrightarrow F
are given by the similar formulas

t=\iota^{\prime}_{T}\pi_{F}+\iota_{T}\pi^{\prime}_{F}+\iota_{T}\sigma_{Y}\pi_{F}
and

f=\iota^{\prime}_{F}\pi_{T}+\iota_{F}\pi^{\prime}_{T}-\iota_{F}\sigma_{Y}\pi_{T}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the additive
functor
\Phi\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
transforms
twists into isomorphisms, and so do the additive functors

\Psi^{+}
and
\Psi^{-}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
All these functors also transform the shifts
[n]
into the inverse
shifts
[-n]
in the respective categories.

Proof.

The assertions about the shifts are obvious from the construction of
the complexes of morphisms in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
The assertions about the twists, claiming esstentially that all
the three functors take objects connected by a not necessarily closed
isomorphism of degree
0
in the respective DG-category to objects
connected by a closed isomorphism of degree
0
, follow immediately
from Lemma
3.9
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the additive
functors
\Phi\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and

\Psi^{+}
,
\Psi^{-}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
are conservative
(i. e., they take nonisomorphisms to nonisomorphisms).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.9
, all the three
functors in question are compositions of the inclusion functors

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{0}
or
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})^{0}

with fully faithful functors
\widetilde{\Phi}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}^{+}
,
or
\widetilde{\Psi}^{-}
.
So it suffices to observe that, for any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
,
the inclusion functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{0}
is conservative
(since the inverse morphism to a closed morphism, if it exists,
is also a closed morphism), any fully faithful functor is conservative,
and a composition of two conservative functors is conservative.
∎

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category.
Then the
idempotent completion

\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
of
\mathbf{A}
is
constructed as the DG-category whose objects are pairs
(A,e_{A})
,
where
A
is an object of
\mathbf{A}
and
e_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,A)
is
a closed idempotent endomorphism of degree
0
.
By the definition, the complex of morphisms
(A,e_{A})\longrightarrow(B,e_{B})
in
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
is the subcomplex

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}((A,e_{A}),(B,e_{B}))=e_{B}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,B)e_{A}\subset\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,B).

The composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
is induced by
that in
\mathbf{A}
.
The DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
is additive and idempotent-complete,
there is a fully faithful DG-functor
\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
taking
each object
A\in\mathbf{A}
to
(A,\mathrm{id}_{A})\in\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
, and every object
of
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
is a direct summand of an object coming from
\mathbf{A}
.
If the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has shifts, twists, cones, infinite
coproducts, or infinite products, then so has
the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a DG-category.
Then the
twist completion
(or “Maurer–Cartan completion”)

\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
is constructed as the DG-category whose objects are
pairs
(A,a_{A})
, where
A
is an object of
\mathbf{A}
and

a\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,A)
is a Maurer–Cartan cochain.
By the definition, the underlying graded abelian group of the complex
of morphisms
(A,a_{A})\longrightarrow(B,a_{B})
in
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
coincides with
the underlying graded abelan group of the complex of morphisms

A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathbf{A}
, that is

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{*}}^{*}((A,a_{A}),(B,a_{B}))=\Hom_{\mathbf{A}^{*}}^{*}(A,B).

The differential
d_{(a_{A},a_{B})}
in the complex

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}((A,a_{A}),(B,a_{B}))
is obtained from
the differential
d
in the complex
\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}(A,B)
by the rule

d_{(a_{A},a_{B})}(f)=d(f)+a_{B}f-(-1)^{|f|}fa_{A}.

The composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
is induced by
that in
\mathbf{A}
.
All twists exist in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
, there is a fully
faithful DG-functor
\mathbf{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
taking each object
A\in\mathbf{A}

to the object
(A,0)\in\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
, and every object of
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
is
a twist of an object coming from
\mathbf{A}
.

If the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is additive, then so is
the DG-category
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
.
If the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has shifts, cones, infinite coproducts,
or infinite products, then so has the DG-category
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}

(in fact, if
\mathbf{A}
is additive with shifts, then it already
follows that
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
has cones).
However, the passage from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
need not preserve
idempotent-completeness.

Examples 3.13
.

(1) Let
k
be a field and
\mathbf{A}
be the DG-category of acyclic
complexes of
k
-vector spaces, i. e., the full DG-subcategory in
the DG-category of complexes of
k
-vector spaces
\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})

whose objects are the acyclic complexes.
Then
\mathbf{A}
is an idempotent-complete DG-category, but
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}

is not.
The reason is that not every complex (of vector spaces) can be
endowed with a new differential making it an acyclic complex,
but every complex is a direct summand of a complex which can be so
endowed; hence
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}\varsubsetneq\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}=\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
.

(2) For any idempotent-complete additive DG-category
\mathbf{B}
with
twists, consider the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{A}\subset\mathbf{B}

of contractible objects in
\mathbf{B}
.
Then one has an equivalence of DG-categories
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}\simeq\mathbf{B}
, because every object
B\in\mathbf{B}
is a direct summand of
a twist
B\oplus B[1]\in\mathbf{B}
of the object
\cone(\mathrm{id}_{B})\in\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
can be naturally viewed
as an intermediate full DG-subcategory between
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}
and

\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
,

\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}\subset\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}\subset\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}.

If the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is idempotent-complete, then

\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
.

Proof.

We recall from Section
3.2
that,
for any DG-category
\mathbf{B}
, the DG-category
\mathbf{B}^{\natural}
has twists;
furthermore,
\mathbf{B}^{\natural}
is additive and idempotent-complete
whenever
\mathbf{B}
is.
Now in the situation at hand, let us consider the commutative diagram
of fully faithful DG-functors

(4)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.83331pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.3472pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.3472pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.06943pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-14.61806pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\natural\natural}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.73611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.06943pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.61101pt\raise-15.86806pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\natural\natural}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-10.83331pt\raise-32.72614pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.83331pt\raise-32.72614pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.83331pt\raise-32.72614pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 34.83331pt\raise-32.72614pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus\natural\natural}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Here the rightmost vertical functor
\natural\natural
is an equivalence
of DG-categories by Proposition
3.5
,
as the DG-category
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
is additive and
idempotent-complete with twists.
It follows that
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is a full DG-subcategory in

\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
.
On the other hand, all twists exist in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
; so
\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}\subset\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
.
Finally, if
\mathbf{A}
is idempotent-complete, then
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}
is
idempotent-complete, too; hence
\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
.
∎

Examples 3.15
.

(1) The following example shows that the DG-category
