\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}

need not be idempotent-complete (for an additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}

which is not idempotent-complete).
Let
\mathbf{A}
be the following full DG-subcategory in the DG-category
of complexes of
k
-vector spaces
\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
, with the grading
group
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
.
The objects of
\mathbf{A}
are all the finite complexes of
finite-dimensional vector spaces whose terms are vector spaces of
dimension divisible by
m
, where
m\geq 2
is a fixed integer.
Then one has
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}=\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}\varsubsetneq\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
.
The reason is that, for any finite complex of finite-dimensional
vector spaces
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, if all the terms of the complex

\cone(\mathrm{id}_{C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}[-1])
have dimensions divisible by
m
,
then the complex
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
has the same property.

(2) On the other hand, let
\mathbf{A}
be the full DG-subcategory in

\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
consisting of all the finite complexes of
finite-dimensional vector spaces whose Euler characteristic is
divisible by
m
.
Then
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}\varsubsetneq\mathbf{A}^{\natural\natural}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
.
Here the reason is that the complex
\cone(\mathrm{id}_{C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}[-1])
has
zero Euler characteristic for any finite complex of finite-dimensional
vector spaces
C^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
In both the examples (1) and (2),
\mathbf{A}^{\oplus}=\mathbf{A}\boldsymbol{(}\mathbf{mc}\boldsymbol{)}^{\oplus}
is,
of course, the full DG-subcategory in
\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
consisting of
all finite complexes of finite-dimensional vector spaces.

3.4.
Examples

In this section we compute the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
and
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
for the concrete examples
of DG-categories
\mathbf{A}
described in Section
2
.

Example 3.16
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a preadditive category and
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
be the DG-category
of complexes in
\mathsf{A}
.
We will use the notation from Section
2.1
: in
particular,
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
is the preadditive category of graded objects
in
\mathsf{A}
(and homogeneous morphisms of degree
0
), while

\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A}))
is the preadditive category of complexes
in
\mathsf{A}
(and closed morphisms of degree
0
between them).

To begin with, the following description of the DG-category

\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
is obtained directly from the definitions.
An object of
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
is a graded object
X^{*}
in
\mathsf{A}
endowed
with a differential
d_{X}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},X^{*})
and
an endomorphism
\sigma_{X}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},X^{*})
such that

d_{X}^{2}=0=\sigma_{X}^{2}
and
d_{X}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{X}d_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
.
The complex of morphisms from an object
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})

to an object
Y^{\natural}=(Y^{*},d_{Y},\sigma_{Y})
in the DG-category

\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
is constructed as follows: the group

\Hom_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}}^{n}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is the subgroup in

\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{-n}(X^{*},Y^{*})
consisting of all homogeneous
morphisms
f
such that
d_{Y}f-(-1)^{n}fd_{X}=0
; the differential
d^{\natural}

in the complex
\Hom_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is given
by the rule
d^{\natural}(f)=\sigma_{Y}f-(-1)^{|f|}f\sigma_{X}
.

One can observe that all objects of the DG-category

\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
are contractible.
Indeed, for any object
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})\in\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}

the endomorphism
d_{X}
belongs to the subgroup

\Hom_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}}^{-1}(X^{\natural},X^{\natural})\subset\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{1}(X^{*},X^{*})
, since
d_{X}^{2}=0
.
Furthermore, one has
d^{\natural}(d_{X})=\sigma_{X}d_{X}+d_{X}\sigma_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{\natural}}
; so
d_{X}
is a contracting homotopy for the object

X^{\natural}\in\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
.
In other words, this means that the DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural}
is
quasi-equivalent to the zero DG-category.

On the other hand, if
\mathsf{A}
is an idempotent-complete additive
category, then
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
is an idempotent-complete additive
DG-category with twists.
So the DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural\natural}
is equivalent to
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})

by Proposition
3.5
.
This example demonstrates that the passage from a DG-category
\mathbf{A}

to the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
does
not
preserve
quasi-equivalences of DG-categories, in general.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be an additive category.
Our next aim is to compute the additive category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural})
, at least, in the case when
\mathsf{A}
is
idempotent-complete.
Similarly to Proposition
3.1
, the faithful,
conservative forgetful functor
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
has faithful,
conservative left and right adjoint functors
G^{+}
and

G^{-}\colon\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
Specifically, the functor
G^{+}
assigns to a graded object
A^{*}\in\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
the complex
G^{+}(A^{*})
with the components
G^{+}(A^{*})^{n}=A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}
and the differential
d_{G^{+},n}\colon A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}\oplus A^{n}
whose only nonzero component is the identity
map
A^{n}\longrightarrow A^{n}
.
Dually, the complex
G^{-}(A^{*})
has the components
G^{-}(A^{*})^{n}=A^{n}\oplus A^{n+1}
and the differential
d_{G^{-},n}\colon A^{n}\oplus A^{n+1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}\oplus A^{n+2}
whose only nonzero component is
the identity map
A^{n+1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}
.
So one has
G^{-}(A^{*})\simeq G^{+}(A^{*})[1]
.

The additive functor
\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{\mathsf{A}}\colon\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural})
is constructed as follows.
For any object
A^{*}\in\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
, put
X^{*}=G^{+}(A^{*})
and
d_{X}=d_{G^{+}}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},X^{*})
.
Let
\sigma_{X}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}(X^{*},X^{*})
be the endomorphism
defined by the rule that the morphism
\sigma_{X,n}\colon X^{n}=A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n-1}\oplus A^{n-2}=X^{n-1}
has
the identity map
A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n-1}
as its only nonzero component.
Then one has
d_{X}^{2}=0=\sigma_{X}^{2}
and
d_{X}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{X}d_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
, so
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})
is an object of

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural})
.
We put
\Upsilon(A^{*})=X^{\natural}
.
The action of the functor
\Upsilon
on morphisms is defined in
the obvious way.

There are commutative diagrams of additive functors

and

Here the upper horizontal double arrow
in (
5
) is the obvious equivalence
of additive categories mentioned in Section
2.1
,
while the lower horizontal double line
in (
6
) is essentially the definition of
the additive category of complexes
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
The leftmost diagonal arrow in (

)
is the fully faithful functor from

3.9
, while
the rightmost vertical arrow in (
6
) is
the faithful functor from Lemma
3.4
.

The additive functor
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{A}}
is always fully faithful
(since so is the additive functor
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})}
).
When the additive category
\mathsf{A}
is idempotent-complete, the functor

\Upsilon
is an equivalence of additive categories.
Given an object
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})^{\natural})
,
the corresponding object
A^{*}\in\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
can be recovered as
the image of the idempotent endomorphism
\sigma_{X}d_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{A})}^{0}(X^{*},X^{*})
.
Consequently, the additive functor
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})}
is
also an equivalence of categories in this case.

Example 3.17
.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring.
We will use the notation from Sections
2.2

and
3.1
; in particular, recall the notation

\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*},\partial)
for the graded ring

R^{*}[\delta]
with the changed sign of the grading,

\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{n}=R^{*}[\delta]^{-n}
, endowed with
the differential
\partial=\partial/\partial\delta
.
So
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is an acyclic DG-ring (the cohomology ring of
the DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
vanishes).

Consider the DG-category
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
of left CDG-modules
over
(R^{*},d,h)
.
We claim that the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is naturally equivalent
(in fact, isomorphic) to the DG-category of left DG-modules
over
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,

(7)

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}.

Indeed, by the definition, the objects of the DG-category

(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural}
are triples
(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})
, where
X^{*}

is a graded left
R^{*}
-module and
d_{X}
,
\sigma_{X}\colon X^{*}\longrightarrow X^{*}

are homogeneous endomorphisms of the graded abelian group
X^{*}

of the degrees
1
and
-1
, respectively.
In fact,
(X^{*},d_{X})
has to be a left CDG-module over
(R^{*},d,h)
,
so the equations

•

d_{X}(rx)=d(r)x+(-1)^{|r|}rd_{X}(x)
and

•

d_{X}^{2}(x)=hx

must be satisfied for all
r\in R^{|r|}
and
x\in X^{|x|}
.
Furthermore,
\sigma_{X}
must be a contracting homotopy with zero
square for the CDG-module
(X^{*},d_{X})
, which means the equations

•

\sigma_{X}(rx)=(-1)^{|r|}r\sigma_{X}(x)
,

•

d_{X}\sigma_{X}(x)+\sigma_{X}d_{X}(x)=x
, and

•

\sigma_{X}^{2}(x)=0

for all
r\in R^{|r|}
and
x\in X^{|x|}
.

On the other hand, the objects of the DG-category

\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
are pairs
(N^{*},\partial_{N})
, where
N^{*}
is
a graded left
R^{*}[\delta]
-module (with the sign of the grading
changed) and
\partial_{N}:N^{*}\longrightarrow N^{*}
is an odd derivation compatible
with the odd derivation
\partial
on
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*}
.
To establish the equivalence of
DG-categories (
7
) on the level of objects,
one assigns to a DG-module
(N^{*},\partial_{N})
over
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

the same graded abelian group
X^{*}=N^{*}
.
The graded
R^{*}
-module structure on
X^{*}
agrees with the one
on
N^{*}
, the differential
d_{X}
on
X^{*}
is given by the action
of the element
\delta\in R^{*}[\delta]
on
N^{*}
, and
the contracting homotopy
\sigma_{X}
on
(X^{*},d_{X})
is given
by the action of the differential
\partial_{N}
on
N^{*}
.
Symbolically, we put

d_{X}=\delta\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{X}=\partial_{N}.

We leave it to the reader to establish the equivalence of
DG-categories (
7
) on the level of
the complexes of morphisms.
Similarly one constructs an isomorphism of DG-categories

({\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}})^{\natural}={\operatorname{\mathbf{mod--}}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
for right CDG-modules
and DG-modules.

Notice that the DG-functor
\natural\natural\colon\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural\natural}
is an equivalence of DG-categories by
Proposition
3.5
.
On the other hand, viewing
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
as a CDG-ring with
zero curvature, one can apply to it the same construction which
produced
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
from
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, thus obtaining an acyclic
DG-ring
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

The underlying graded ring
R^{*}[\delta][\epsilon]
of
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is obtained by adjoining an element
\epsilon
of degree
-1
to
the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
, and imposing the relations

\epsilon\hat{r}-(-1)^{|\hat{r}|}\hat{r}\epsilon=\partial(\hat{r})
for all

\hat{r}\in R^{*}[\delta]
(where
\partial=\partial/\partial\delta
) and
\epsilon^{2}=0
.
One can introduce the notation
d^{\natural}=\partial=\partial/\partial\delta
, and then
have
d^{\natural\natural}=\partial/\partial\epsilon
denote the differential in
the DG-ring
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

Explicitly, one has
\deg\delta=1
and
\deg\epsilon=-1
, and
the defining relations in the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta][\epsilon]
are

\delta r-(-1)^{|r|}r\delta=d(r)
,
\epsilon r-(-1)^{|r|}r\epsilon=0
,

\delta^{2}=h
,
\epsilon^{2}=0
, and
\epsilon\delta+\delta\epsilon=1
,
for all
r\in R^{|r|}
.
The resulting DG-ring is
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*}[\delta][\epsilon],d^{\natural\natural})
, where the differential

d^{\natural\natural}=\partial/\partial\epsilon\colon R^{*}[\delta][\epsilon]\longrightarrow R^{*}[\delta][\epsilon]
is defined by the rules
d^{\natural\natural}(r)=0

for all
r\in R^{*}
,
d^{\natural\natural}(\delta)=0
, and

d^{\natural\natural}(\epsilon)=1
.

Applying (
7
) twice, we obtain an isomorphism of
DG-categories
(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural\natural}=\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.
Thus
the DG-category of CDG-modules over any CDG-ring

(in particular, the DG-category of DG-modules over any DG-ring)

is equivalent to the DG-category of DG-modules over
an acyclic DG-ring
.

Let us now compute the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
.
The additive functor
\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
is constructed as follows.
Let
M^{*}
be a graded left
R^{*}
-module.
Consider the CDG-module
(X^{*},d_{X})=(G^{+}(M^{*}),d_{G^{+}})
over

\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, as constructed in Proposition
3.1
(a),
and endow it with the endomorphism

\sigma_{X}\in\Hom_{R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathit{--mod}}}}^{-1}(M^{*},M^{*})
constructed as
the composition
G^{+}(M^{*})\longrightarrow M^{*}[-1]\longrightarrow G^{+}(M^{*})[-1]
of
the maps in the short exact sequence of
Proposition
3.1
(b).

Explicitly, the elements of the group
X^{n}=G^{+}(M^{*})^{n}
,

n\in\Gamma
, are formal expressions
m+\delta m^{\prime}
,
with
m\in M^{n}
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{n-1}
.
The action of
R^{*}
in
X^{*}=G^{+}(M^{*})
is given by the formula

r(m+\delta m^{\prime})=rm-(-1)^{|r|}d(r)m^{\prime}+(-1)^{|r|}\delta rm^{\prime}
for all

r\in R^{|r|}
, while the differential on
G^{+}(M^{*})
is

d_{X}(m+\delta m^{\prime})=d_{G^{+}}(m+\delta m^{\prime})=hm^{\prime}+\delta m
.
The endomorphism
\sigma_{X}\colon X^{*}\longrightarrow X^{*}
is given by the rule

\sigma_{X}(m+\delta m^{\prime})=m^{\prime}+\delta 0
.
Then one has
\sigma_{X}(rx)=(-1)^{|r|}r\sigma_{X}(x)
for all

r\in R^{|x|}
and
x\in X^{*}
,
d_{X}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{X}d_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
,
and
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0
, so
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})
is an object
of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
.
We put
\Upsilon(M^{*})=X^{\natural}
.
The action of the functor
\Upsilon
on morphisms is defined in
the obvious way.

The functor
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
is an equivalence of abelian categories.
Given an object
X^{\natural}=(X^{*},d_{X},\sigma_{X})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
, the corresponding graded
R^{*}
-module

M^{*}
can be recovered as the image of the homogeneous map

\sigma_{X}\in\Hom_{R^{*}}^{-1}(X^{*},X^{*})
, or as the image of
the idempotent homogeneous map
\sigma_{X}d_{X}\in\Hom_{R^{*}}^{0}(X^{*},X^{*})
.
Indeed, put
\upsilon_{X}=d_{X}-h\sigma_{X}
; then the pair of homogeneous
maps of graded abelian groups
\sigma_{X}
and
\upsilon_{X}\colon X^{*}\longrightarrow X^{*}
of degrees
-1
and
1
, respectively, satisfies the equations

\sigma_{X}^{2}=0=\upsilon_{X}^{2}
and
\sigma_{X}\upsilon_{X}+\upsilon_{X}\sigma_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{*}}
; so it defines a representation of a graded
2\times 2

matrix ring in
X^{*}
, similarly to the discussion in the proof of
Proposition
3.5
.

There are commutative diagrams of additive functors

and

(9)

Here the upper horizontal arrow
in (
8
) is the inclusion of
the full subcategory mentioned at the end of
Section
2.2
, while the leftmost
diagonal arrow in (
8
) is
the fully faithful functor from
Lemma
3.9
.
The functor
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
is an equivalence of categories,
but the other two fully faithful functors
in (
8
) are
not

essentially surjective, as it was explained in
Examples
3.2

and
3.3
.
The diagonal arrows in (
9
) are
the faithful functors from Proposition
3.1

and Lemma
3.4
.

Example 3.18
.

We are building up on the theory developed in
Sections
2.3
–
2.4

and Example
3.17
.
Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d_{R},h_{R})
and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(S^{*},d_{S},h_{S})
be
two CDG-rings, and let
(f,a)\colon\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a morphism
between them (so
a\in S^{1}
).
Consider the graded rings
R^{*}[\delta_{R}]
and
S^{*}[\delta_{S}]
with
their odd derivations
\partial_{R}=\partial/\partial\delta_{R}
and
\partial_{S}=\partial/\partial\delta_{S}

of degree
-1
with zero squares.
Then, following
[
45
, Section 4.2]
, the morphism of
CDG-rings
(f,a)
induces a morphism of graded rings

\hat{f}\colon R^{*}[\delta_{R}]\longrightarrow S^{*}[\delta_{S}]
given by the rules

\hat{f}(r)=f(r)
for all
r\in R^{*}
and
f(\delta_{R})=\delta_{S}+a
.
The map
\hat{f}
commutes with the differentials
\partial_{R}
and
\partial_{S}

on
R^{*}[\delta]
and
S^{*}[\delta]
, i. e.,
f\circ\partial_{R}=\partial_{S}\circ f
.

Let
L^{*}
and
M^{*}
be left graded modules over the graded rings

R^{*}
and
S^{*}
, respectively; and let
g\colon L^{*}\longrightarrow S^{*}
be a map
of graded modules compatible with the morphism of graded rings

f\colon R^{*}\longrightarrow S^{*}
.
Then the induced map of CDG-modules
G^{+}(g)\colon G^{+}(L^{*})\longrightarrow G^{+}(M^{*})
compatible with the morphism of CDG-rings

(f,a)\colon\allowbreak\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
is defined by the formula

G^{+}(g)^{n}(l+\delta_{R}l^{\prime})=g(l)+ag(l^{\prime})+\delta_{S}g(l^{\prime})
for all
l\in L^{n}

and
l^{\prime}\in L^{n-1}
.
The map
G^{+}(g)
commutes with the structural endomorphisms

\sigma_{X}
and
\sigma_{Y}
of the objects

\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}(L^{*})=X^{\natural}=(X,d_{X},\sigma_{X})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
and
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}(M^{*})=Y^{\natural}=(Y,d_{Y},\sigma_{Y})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})^{\natural})
, that is,

\sigma_{Y}\circ G^{+}(g)=G^{+}(g)\circ\sigma_{X}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
be a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra over
a scheme
X
, and let
B^{*}
be its underlying sheaf of graded rings.
Then one can construct the sheaf of graded rings
B^{*}[\delta]

assigning to every affine open subscheme
U\subset X
the graded ring

B^{*}[\delta](U)=B^{*}(U)[\delta]
associated with the CDG-ring

(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})
.
The restriction maps
B^{*}[\delta](U)\longrightarrow B^{*}[\delta](V)
for pairs
of affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
are induced by
the restriction morphisms of CDG-rings
(\rho_{VU},a_{VU})\colon(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})\longrightarrow(B^{*}(V),d_{V},h_{V})
in the way explained above.
The composition
O_{X}\longrightarrow B^{0}\longrightarrow B^{*}[\delta]^{0}
defines
a morphism of sheaves of rings
O_{X}\longrightarrow B^{*}[\delta]^{0}
making

B^{*}[\delta]
a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra over
X

(as one can see from the short exact sequence (
1
)).

Furthermore, the differential
\partial=\partial/\partial\delta
is well-defined in
the sheaf of graded rings
B^{*}[\delta]
, making
B^{*}[\delta]

a sheaf of DG-rings over
X
(with the differential of degree
-1
).
Changing the sign of the grading, one puts

\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{n}=B^{*}[\delta]^{-n}
for all
n\in\Gamma
; then

\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{*},\partial)
is a sheaf of DG-rings on
X

with the differential of degree
1
(as in our usual convention).
The complex of sheaves (or quasi-modules)
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
acyclic as a complex of sheaves, and in fact, its sections over
every affine open subscheme
U\subset X
form an acyclic complex;
but the complex
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)
of global sections of

\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
X
need
not
be acyclic,
generally speaking.

In fact, the DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)
is acyclic if and only if
the quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
“admits a global
connecton”, meaning that it is isomorphic (in a suitable sense, i. e.,
change-of-connection isomorphic) to a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra

{}^{\prime}\!\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
with vanishing change-of-connection elements
a^{\prime}_{VU}=0

for all affine open subschemes
V\subset U\subset X
.
In particular, one can view
\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
as a quasi-coherent
CDG-quasi-algebra over
X
and apply the same construction again,
producing the sheaf of DG-rings
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
over
X
.
Its DG-ring of global sections
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)
is acyclic (and so
are the DG-rings of sections
\widehat{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}}{\vphantom{\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{t}}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y)
over all open subschemes

Y\subset X
).

Similarly to Example
3.17
, there is a natural
equivalence (in fact, isomorphism) of DG-categories

(10)

(\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})^{\natural}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}.

We leave the details to the reader.

Let us compute the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})^{\natural})
.
Similarly to the previous examples, we start with constructing
the adjoint functors to the exact, faithful forgetful functor

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})\longrightarrow B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
.
The left adjoint functor
G^{+}\colon B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})

assigns to a quasi-coherent graded left
B^{*}
-module
M^{*}

the quasi-coherent left CDG-module
G^{+}(M^{*})
over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
defined
by applying the functor
G^{+}
from Proposition
3.1

for the CDG-ring
(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})
to the graded
B^{*}(U)
-module

M^{*}(U)
for every affine open subset
U\subset X
.
In other words, we put
G^{+}(M^{*})(U)=G^{+}(M^{*}(U))
.
The restriction maps
G^{+}(M^{*})(U)\longrightarrow G^{+}(M^{*})(V)
are induced
by the restriction morphisms of CDG-rings
(\rho_{VU},a_{VU})\colon(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})\longrightarrow(B^{*}(V),d_{V},h_{V})
in the way explained above.
The underlying graded
B^{*}
-module of
G^{+}(M^{*})
is quasi-coherent
(i. e., quasi-coherent as an
O_{X}
-module) in view of
the short exact sequence in Proposition
3.1
(b),
because the full subcategory of quasi-coherent sheaves is closed
under extensions in the category of all sheaves of
O_{X}
-modules.
The right adjoint functor
G^{-}
is constructed similarly, and
only differs by a shift,
G^{-}=G^{+}[1]
.

The additive functor
\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}\colon B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})^{\natural})
is constructed as follows.
Let
M^{*}
be a quasi-coherent graded
B^{*}
-module.
Put
\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=G^{+}(M^{*})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})
, and let

Q^{*}\in B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
denote the underlying quasi-coherent graded

B^{*}
-module of
G^{+}(M^{*})
.
Then the homogeneous endomorphism
\sigma_{Q}\in\Hom_{B^{*}}^{-1}(Q^{*},Q^{*})

is defined by the rule
\sigma_{Q}(U)=\sigma_{Q(U)}
for all affine open
subschemes
U\subset X
, where
(\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}(U),\sigma_{Q(U)})=(Q^{*}(U),d_{Q(U)},\sigma_{Q(U)})=\Upsilon_{(B^{*}(U),d_{U},h_{U})}(M^{*}(U))
.
Finally, we put
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}(M^{*})=(\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\sigma_{Q})
.
The action of the functor
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
on morphisms is easy
to define.

The functor
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
is an equivalence of abelian
categories.
Given an object
Q^{\natural}=(\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}},\sigma_{Q})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}})^{\natural})
, the corresponding quasi-coherent
graded
B^{*}
-module
M^{*}
can be recovered as the image of
the homogeneous map of quasi-coherent graded
B^{*}
-modules

\sigma_{Q}\colon Q^{*}[1]\longrightarrow Q^{*}
(where
Q^{*}
is the underlying
quasi-coherent graded
B^{*}
-module of the quasi-coherent
CDG-module
\boldsymbol{Q}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
).
Locally over an affine open subscheme
U\subset X
,
the
B^{*}(U)
-module
M^{*}(U)
can be also recovered as the image
of the idempotent homogeneous map
\sigma_{Q(U)}d_{Q(U)}\in\Hom^{0}_{B^{*}(U)}(Q^{*}(U),Q^{*}(U))
.

There are commutative diagrams of additive functors

Here all the functors in (
11
) are
fully faithful, but only
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
is essentially surjective
(generally speaking).
The upper horizontal arrow in (
11
) is
the obvious inclusion, while the leftmost diagonal arrow
in (
11
) is the fully faithful functor
from Lemma
3.9
.
The diagonal arrows in (
12
) are faithful
functors; the leftmost one was defined above, while the rightmost one
is the one from Lemma
3.4
.

Example 3.19
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a preadditive category and
\Lambda\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
be
an autoequivalence.
We will use the notation from Section
2.5
,
and assume that either
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, or
\Lambda
is involutive and

\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Let
w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
be a potential.

The following description of the DG-category
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural}

is obtained directly from the definitions.
An object of
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural}
is a
\Lambda
-periodic
object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}
in
\mathsf{A}
endowed with a differential
d_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{1}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
and an endomorphism

\sigma_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{-1}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})

such that
\boldsymbol{X}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{X})
is a factorization of
w
and
the equations
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0
,
d_{X}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{X}d_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}

are satisfied.
The complex of morphisms from an object
X^{\natural}=(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{X},\sigma_{X})

to an object
Y^{\natural}=(Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{Y},\sigma_{Y})
in the DG-category

\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural}
is constructed as follows: for any

n\in\Gamma
, the group
\Hom^{n}_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is the subgroup in
\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{-n}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
consisting of all homogeneous morphisms
f
of
degree
-n
(in the
\Gamma
-graded category of
\Lambda
-periodic
objects) for which the equation
d_{Y}f-(-1)^{n}fd_{X}=0
holds.
The differential
d^{\natural}
in the complex

\Hom^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural}}(X^{\natural},Y^{\natural})
is given by
the usual rule
d^{\natural}(f)=\sigma_{Y}f-(-1)^{|f|}f\sigma_{X}
.

Assume that
\mathsf{A}
is an additive category.
Our aim is to compute the additive category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural})
, at least, in the case when
\mathsf{A}
is
idempotent-complete.
Similarly to Proposition
3.1
and
Example
3.16
, the faithful, conservative
forgetful functor
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)\longrightarrow\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)

has faithful, conservative left and right adjoint functors

G^{+}
and
G^{-}\colon\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
.

Specifically, the functor
G^{+}
assigns to a
\Lambda
-periodic
graded object
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
the factorization

G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
whose underlying
\Lambda
-periodic graded object
is
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\oplus A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[-1]
; so the grading components of
G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})

are
G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})^{n}=A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}\simeq A^{n}\oplus\Lambda^{-1}(A^{n})
.
The differential
d_{G^{+}}^{n}\colon A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}\oplus A^{n}
has two nonzero components: the identity map

A^{n}\longrightarrow A^{n}
and the map
A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}
whose
composition with the periodicity isomorphism

(\lambda_{A}^{n+1,n-1})^{-1}\colon A^{n+1}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(A^{n-1})
is
the map
w_{A^{n-1}}\colon A^{n-1}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(A^{n-1})
.
Dually, the factorization
G^{-}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
has the underlying

\Lambda
-periodic graded object
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\oplus A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[1]
,
so its grading components are
G^{-}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})^{n}=A^{n}\oplus A^{n+1}\simeq A^{n}\oplus\Lambda(A^{n})
.
The differential
d_{G^{-}}^{n}\colon A^{n}\oplus A^{n+1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}\oplus A^{n+2}
has two nonzero components: the identity map

A^{n+1}\longrightarrow A^{n+1}
and the map
A^{n}\longrightarrow A^{n+2}
whose
composition with the periodicity isomorphism

(\lambda_{A}^{n+2,n})^{-1}\colon A^{n+2}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(A^{n})
is
the map
w_{A^{n}}\colon A^{n}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(A^{n})
.
One has
G^{-}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})\simeq G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})[1]
.

The additive functor
\Upsilon=\Upsilon_{\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w}\colon\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural})
is constructed
as follows (cf. the similar construction in
Example
3.16
).
For any object
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, put
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}=G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})

and
d_{X}=d_{G^{+}}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
.
Let
\sigma_{X}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
be
the endomorphism defined by the rule that the morphism

\sigma_{X,n}\colon A^{n}\oplus A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n-1}\oplus A^{n-2}

has the identity map
A^{n-1}\longrightarrow A^{n-1}
as its only nonzero
component.
Then one has
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0
and
d_{X}\sigma_{X}+\sigma_{X}d_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}
,
so
X^{\natural}=(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{X},\sigma_{X})
is an object of

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural})
.
We put
\Upsilon(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})=X^{\natural}
.
The action of the functor
\Upsilon
on morphisms is defined in
the obvious way.

There are commutative diagrams of additive functors

and

(14)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.70024pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.11111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 19.11113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.03809pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.70024pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 115.22685pt\raise-16.4075pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.88115pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Psi_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)}^{+}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.22685pt\raise-24.17499pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 48.70024pt\raise-32.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 80.5683pt\raise-32.81502pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Here all the functors in (
13
)
are fully faithful.
The upper horizontal arrow in (
13
)
is the obvious inclusion, while the lower horizontal double line
in (
14
) is the definition of the additive
category of factorizations
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
.
The leftmost diagonal arrow in (
13
)
is the fully faithful functor from
Lemma
3.9
, while the rightmost
vertical arrow in (
14
) is the faithful
functor from Lemma
3.4
.

The inclusion of
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{0}
into
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
is

not
essentially surjective, generally speaking, as
not
every
\Lambda
-periodic object admits a differential making
it a factorization of
w
.
This was explained in
Examples
3.2
(take

R^{*}=k[x,x^{-1}]
and
h=x
, or equivalently, the category

\mathsf{A}=k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}\times k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
with its natural autoequivalence
\Lambda

switching the factors
k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}}
and the potential
w=\mathrm{id}
;
cf. Remark
2.7
).
However, every object of
\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
is a direct summand of
an object coming from
\mathsf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{0}
; in fact,
any
\Lambda
-periodic object
A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
is
a direct summand of the underlying
\Lambda
-periodic object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}

of the factorization
\boldsymbol{X}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=G^{+}(A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
.
So the counterexample in
Examples
3.3
has no counterpart
for factorizations.

The additive functor
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w}
is always fully
faithful (one can deduce this, e. g., from full-and-faithfulness of
the functor
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)}
from
Lemma
3.9
).
When the additive category
\mathsf{A}
is idempotent-complete,
the functor
\Upsilon
is an equivalence of additive categories.
Given an object
X^{\natural}=(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},d_{X},\sigma_{X})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)^{\natural})
, the corresponding object

A^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
can be recovered as the image of
the idempotent endomorphism
\sigma_{X}d_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{0}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
.

Indeed, one can define an endomorphism
\upsilon_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{1}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
by subtracting from
d_{X}

the composition of the action of the natural transformation

w_{X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\colon X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
with
the periodicity isomorphism
\lambda_{X}^{*,*+2}\colon\Lambda^{2}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})\simeq X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}[2]
and with the endomorphism

\sigma_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)}^{-1}(X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}},X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}})
.
Then the pair of homogeneous endomorphisms
\sigma_{X}

and
\upsilon_{X}
of degrees
-1
and
1
, respectively, satisfies
the equations
\sigma_{X}^{2}=0=\upsilon_{X}^{2}
and
\sigma_{X}\upsilon_{X}+\upsilon_{X}\sigma_{X}=\mathrm{id}_{X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}
; so it defines an action of
of the graded
2\times 2
matrix ring in the graded category
object
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda)
, similarly to the discussion
in the proof of Proposition
3.5

(cf. Example
3.17
).

4.
Abelian and Exact DG-Categories

4.1.
The functor of cone of identity endomorphism

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Recall the notation
\Xi=\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

introduced in Section
3.3

for the functor
\Xi(A)=\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1])
.

Lemma 4.1
.

The endofunctor
\Xi\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is left and
right adjoint to its own shift, viz., to the endofunctor

\Xi[1]\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Consequently, the endofunctor
\Xi
preserves all limits and
colimits (and in particular, all kernels and cokernels) that exist
in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

For any objects
A
and
B\in\mathbf{A}
, there are natural isomorphisms
of complexes of abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1]),B)\simeq\cone(\mathrm{id}_{\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,B)})\simeq\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,\cone(\mathrm{id}_{B}))

and

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(B,\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A}[-1]))\simeq\cone(\mathrm{id}_{\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(B,A)}[-1])\simeq\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\cone(\mathrm{id}_{B}),A).

The desired adjunction is obtained by passing to the groups of
degree
0
cocycles in these isomorphisms of complexes.
∎

There is an obvious pair of natural transformations of additive
endofunctors
\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}

on the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
assigning to an object
A\in\mathsf{A}

the natural closed morphisms
A[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi(A)\longrightarrow A

of degree
0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any object
A\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, in the pair of natural morphisms

A[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi(A)\longrightarrow A
, the former morphism is a kernel
of the latter one and the latter morphism is a cokernel of
the former one in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

More generally, for any closed morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
of
degree
0
in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
, in the pair of natural morphisms

B[-1]\longrightarrow\cone(f[-1])\longrightarrow A
, the former morphism is a kernel of
the latter one and the latter morphism is a cokernel of the former one
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Let us check the first assertion.
For any object
X\in\mathbf{A}
, we have a short exact sequence of
complexes of abelian groups

0\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,B[-1])\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,\cone(f[-1]))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,A)\longrightarrow 0.

Passing to the groups of degree
0
cocycles and using the fact
that the kernel functor is left exact in the category of abelian
groups, we obtain a left exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,B[-1])\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,\cone(f[-1]))\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(X,A),

as desired.
The second assertion is dual.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

The endofunctor
\Xi\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is faithful
and conservative.

Proof.

The assertions follow from Lemma
3.8
,
telling that
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
, together with
Lemmas
3.4
and
3.12
,
claiming that the functors
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
are faithful
and conservative.
To give a direct proof, the assertion that
\Xi
is faithful follows
immediately from Lemma
4.2
.
To show that
\Xi
is conservative, one observes that the inclusion
functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{A}^{0}
is conservative and
the composition of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\overset{\Xi}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\rightarrowtail\mathbf{A}^{0}
has the inclusion functor as a direct summand.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
be a composable pair
of morphisms in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
In this context

(a)
if the morphism
\Xi(f)
is a kernel of the morphism

\Xi(g)
, then the morphism
f
is a kernel of the morphism
g
;

(b)
if the morphism
\Xi(g)
is a cokernel of
the morphism
\Xi(f)
, then the morphism
g
is a cokernel of
the morphism
f
.

Proof.

The functor
\Xi
is faithful by
Lemma
4.3
; so the equation

\Xi(g)\circ\Xi(f)=0
implies
g\circ f=0
.
To prove part (a) (part (b) is dual), consider the commutative diagram

(15)

If the morphism
\Xi(f)
is a monomorphism, then so is the morphism

\Xi(f)[1]
; since
A\rightarrowtail\Xi(A)[1]
is a monomorphism
as well, it follows from commutativity of the upper leftmost square
that
f
is a monomorphism.
Now if
\Xi(f)
is a kernel of
\Xi(g)
, then
\Xi(f)[1]
is
a kernel of
\Xi(g)[1]
.
Given an object
X\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and a morphism
X\longrightarrow B

for which the composition
X\longrightarrow B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
vanishes,
consider the composition
X\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\Xi(B)[1]
and notice
that the composition
X\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\Xi(B)[1]\longrightarrow\Xi(C)[1]

vanishes.
So the composition
X\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\Xi(B)[1]
factorizes through

\Xi(f)[1]
, and we obtain a morphism
X\longrightarrow\Xi(A)[1]
.
Continuing the diagram chase and using
Lemma
4.2
, one proves that the morphism

X\longrightarrow B
factorizes through
f
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones, the additive
functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
can be extended in
a natural way to a faithful additive functor
\widetilde{\Xi}=\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathbf{A}^{0}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

The assertion follows immediately from Lemmas
3.8

and
3.9
.
It suffices to put
\widetilde{\Xi}_{\mathbf{A}}=\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\circ\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{A}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an idempotent-complete additive DG-category with shifts
and twists.
In this setting

(a)
if
g
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and the morphism

\Xi(g)
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, then the morphism
g
has
a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
;

(b)
if
f
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and the morphism

\Xi(f)
has a cokernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, then the morphism
f
has
a cokernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Let
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
such that
the morphism
\Xi(g)\colon\Xi(B)\allowbreak\longrightarrow\Xi(C)
has a kernel

h\colon E\longrightarrow\Xi(B)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Let
A\in\mathbf{A}
be an object.
Recall the notation
\iota_{A}
,
\pi_{A}
,
\iota^{\prime}_{A}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{A}
from
the proofs of Lemma
3.4

and Proposition
3.5
.
In particular,
\iota_{A}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,\Xi(A))
and

\pi_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Xi(A),A)
are closed morphisms,

d(\iota_{A})=0=d(\pi_{A})
, while
\iota^{\prime}_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,\Xi(A))
and

\pi^{\prime}_{A}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,\Xi(A))
are morphisms satisfying

d(\pi^{\prime}_{A})=\pi_{A}
and
d(\iota^{\prime}_{A})=\iota_{A}
.

Introduce the notation
s_{A}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(A,A[-1])
for the canonical
shift isomorphism (by the definition of a shift, one has
d(s_{A})=0
).
Put
\phi_{A}=(\iota^{\prime}_{A},\iota_{A}s_{A}^{-1})\colon A\oplus A[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi(A)

and
\psi_{A}=(\pi_{A},s_{A}\pi^{\prime}_{A})\colon\Xi(A)\longrightarrow A\oplus A[-1]
; then

\phi_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A\oplus A[-1],\>\Xi(A))
and

\psi_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Xi(A),\>A\oplus A[-1])
are mutually inverse
isomorphisms in the additive category
\mathbf{A}^{0}
.
As usually, we put
\tau_{A}=\iota_{A}\pi_{A}\in\Hom^{1}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Xi(A),\Xi(A))
;
then
d(\phi_{A})=(\iota_{A},0)=\tau_{A}\phi_{A}
and
d(\psi_{A})=(0,-s_{A}\pi_{A})=-\psi_{A}\tau_{A}
.
So
-\tau_{A}
is a Maurer–Cartan cochain (indeed,
d(\tau_{A})=0=\tau_{A}^{2}
), and the morphisms
\phi_{A}
and
\psi_{A}
establish
an isomorphism
A\oplus A^{-1}=\Xi(A)(-\tau_{A})
in
the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
.

Let
X\in\mathbf{A}
be an object.
Introduce further notation
t_{X}\in\Hom^{-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(X,X[1])

for the canonical shift isomorphism in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A})
connecting
the objects
X
and
X[1]
(so
d(t_{X})=0
).
Then
t_{\Xi(A)}\tau_{A}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Xi(A),\Xi(A)[1])
is a morphism
in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Returning to the situation at hand, we have a commutative diagram of
solid arrows in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{\Xi(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)}
\scriptstyle{t_{\Xi(B)}\tau_{B}}
\textstyle{\Xi(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{\Xi(C)}\tau_{C}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h[1]}
\scriptstyle{a[1]}
\textstyle{\Xi(B)[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)[1]}
\scriptstyle{(t_{\Xi(B)}\tau_{B})[1]}
\textstyle{\Xi(C)[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(t_{\Xi(C)}\tau_{C})[1]}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h[2]}
\textstyle{\Xi(B)[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)[2]}
\textstyle{\Xi(C)[2]}

Since the morphism
h
is a kernel of
\Xi(g)
and the morphism
h[1]

is a kernel of
\Xi(g)[1]
, there exists a unique morphism

a\colon E\longrightarrow E[1]
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
making the leftmost square
commutative.
Since the composition
(t_{\Xi(B)}\tau_{B})[1]\circ(t_{\Xi(B)}\tau_{B})=t_{\Xi(B)}[1]t_{\Xi(B)}\tau_{B}^{2}
vanishes in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, so
does the composition
a[1]\circ a
.

Put
\alpha=t_{E}^{-1}a\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(E,E)
.
Then
d(\alpha)=0=\alpha^{2}
, so
-\alpha
is a Maurer–Cartan cochain.
By assumption, there exists an object
S=E(-\alpha)\in\mathbf{A}

together with mutually inverse isomorphisms

\tilde{\phi}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(S,E)
and
\tilde{\psi}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(E,S)

such that
d(\tilde{\phi})=\alpha\tilde{\phi}

and
d(\tilde{\psi})=-\tilde{\psi}\alpha
.
Put
u=\psi_{B}h\tilde{\phi}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(S,\>B\oplus B[-1])
.
Then
d(u)=-\psi_{B}\tau_{B}h\tilde{\phi}+\psi_{B}h\alpha\tilde{\phi}=0
,
so
u\colon S\longrightarrow B\oplus B[-1]
is a morphism in
the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
We have a composable pair of morphisms

(16)

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{B\oplus B[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\oplus g[-1]}
\textstyle{C\oplus C[-1]}

in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Our next aim is to prove that the morphism
u
is a kernel of
the morphism
g\oplus g[-1]
in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
For this purpose, we observe that, in the ambient additive
category
\mathbf{A}^{0}
, the composable pair of
morphisms (
16
) is isomorphic to
the composable pair of morphisms

(17)

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\Xi(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)}
\textstyle{\Xi(C),}

where
h
is a kernel of
\Xi(g)
by our original assumption.
Indeed, the morphisms
\tilde{\psi}
,
\psi_{B}
, and
\psi_{C}
,
together with their inverse morphisms
\tilde{\phi}
,
\phi_{B}
,
and
\phi_{C}
, provide an isomorphism
between (
16
) and (
17
)
in
\mathbf{A}^{0}
.

By Lemma
4.5
, it follows that the pair of morphisms
obtained by applying
\Xi
to (
16
) is
isomorphic to the pair of morphisms obtained by applying
\Xi

to (
17
) in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Since the functor
\Xi
preserves kernels by Lemma
4.1
,
the morphism
\Xi(h)
is a kernel of the morphism
\Xi(\Xi(g))
,
and therefore the morphism
\Xi(u)
is a kernel of the morphism

\Xi(g\oplus g[-1])
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
According to Lemma
4.4
(a), we can conclude
that the morphism
u
is a kernel of
g\oplus g[-1]
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

The rest of the proof is now straightforward.
Given an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
, denote by
p_{A}\colon A\oplus A[-1]\longrightarrow A\oplus A[-1]
the idempotent projector on the direct summand
A

in
A\oplus A[-1]
.
Then we have
p_{C}\circ(g\oplus g[-1])=(g\oplus g[-1])\circ p_{B}
.
An argument similar to the one above, using the fact that
u
is
a kernel of
g\oplus g[-1]
, shows that there exists a unique
morphism
q\colon S\longrightarrow S
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
such that
p_{B}u=uq

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Moreover, one similarly shows that the equation
p_{B}^{2}=p_{B}

implies
q^{2}=q
.

Since the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is idempotent-complete
by assumption, the idempotent endomorphism
q\colon S\longrightarrow S
has
an image
A\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
There exists a unique morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

such that the composition
A\rightarrowtail S\overset{u}{\longrightarrow}B\oplus B[-1]
is equal to the composition
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\rightarrowtail B\oplus B[-1]
.
One easily shows that the morphism
f
is a kernel of
g

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
This proves part (a); part (b) is dual.
∎

4.2.
DG-compatible exact structures

An
exact category

\mathsf{E}
(in the sense of Quillen) is an additive
category endowed with a class of (
admissible
)
short exact
sequences
(or “conflations”)
0\longrightarrow E^{\prime}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0
satisfying the axioms for which we refer the reader
to
[
23
, Appendix A]
,
[
14
]
, or
[
36
, Appendix A]
.
If
0\longrightarrow E^{\prime}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short
exact sequence, then the morphism
E^{\prime}\longrightarrow E
is said to be
an
admissible monomorphism
(or “inflation”), while
the morphism
E\longrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}
is said to be an
admissible
epimorphism
(or “deflation”).

Given two exact categories
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}
, an additive functor

\Theta\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{F}
is said to be
exact
if it preserves
admissible short exact sequences, i. e., takes admissible short exact
sequences to admissible short exact sequences.
Obviously, an exact functor
\Theta
also takes admissible
monomorphisms to admissible monomorphisms and admissible epimorphisms
to admissible epimorphisms.

We will say that an additive functor
\Theta\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{F}
acting
between two exact categories
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{F}

reflects admissible
short exact sequences
if, for any composable pair of morphisms

E^{\prime}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathsf{E}
, the condition that
0\longrightarrow\Theta(E^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(E)\longrightarrow\Theta(E^{\prime\prime})\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible
short exact sequence in
\mathsf{F}
implies that
0\longrightarrow E^{\prime}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow E^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow 0
is an admissble short exact sequence in
\mathsf{E}
.
An additive functor
\Theta
is said to
reflect admissible
monomorphisms
if, given a morphism
f
in
\mathsf{E}
, the condition that

\Theta(f)
is an admissible monomorphism in
\mathsf{F}
implies that
f
is
an admissible monomorphism in
\mathsf{E}
.
Dually,
\Theta

reflects admissible epimorphisms
if
a morphism
g
in
\mathsf{E}
is an admissible epimorphism whenever

\Theta(g)
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
We will say that an exact category structure on the additive category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is
DG-compatible
(with the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
)
if the following two conditions hold:

•

the exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is preserved by
the shift functors
[1]
and
[-1]
;

•

for any object
E\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, the natural sequence

0\longrightarrow E[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(E)\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
from
Section
4.1
is an admissible short exact
sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then an exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
that is preserved
by the shift functors
[1]
and
[-1]
is DG-compatible
if and only if the additive functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
preserves and reflects admissible short exact sequences.

Proof.

“If”: the point is that the sequence
0\longrightarrow E[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi(E)\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
becomes split exact after the functor
\Xi
is
applied.
Indeed, this sequence is split exact in the additive category

\mathbf{E}^{0}
, so it remains to use Lemma
4.5

(cf. Lemma
3.11
).
All split short exact sequences are always admissible; so if
\Xi

reflects admissible short exact sequences, then
0\longrightarrow E[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi(E)\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
is admissible.

“Only if”: both the preservation and reflection of admissible
short exact sequences can be seen from
the commutative diagram (
15
).
Let us redraw the diagram, omitting the unnecessary shift:

(18)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 161.05896pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 123.00342pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 161.05896pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Here
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
is an arbitrary
composable pair of morphisms in
\mathbf{E}
.
By assumption, the columns in (
18
)
are admissible short exact sequences.

Now if
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible
short exact sequence, then so is
0\longrightarrow A[-1]\longrightarrow B[-1]\longrightarrow C[-1]\longrightarrow 0
.
Furthermore, notice that
\Xi(g)\circ\Xi(f)=\Xi(g\circ f)=0
,
since
\Xi
is an additive functor.
It remains to recall that the class of all admissible short
exact sequences is closed under (admissible) extensions within
the class of all pairs of morphisms with zero composition in
any exact category
[
14
, Corollary 3.6(ii)]
.

Conversely, assume that the short sequence
0\longrightarrow\Xi(A)\longrightarrow\Xi(B)\longrightarrow\Xi(C)\longrightarrow 0
is admissible.
The point is that it then follows
from (
18
) that the upper and lower rows are
admissible short exact sequences as well, because they are each
other’s shifts, so their exactness properties are the same.
Specifically, applying the following lemma to
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

allows to finish the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category endowed with an autoequivalence

[1]\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}
such that both
[1]
and its inverse
functor
[-1]
preserve admissible short exact sequences.
Suppose that we are given a commutative diagram in
\mathsf{E}

(19)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern
