167.9902pt\raise-55.61111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower-2.04439pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.6102pt\raise-56.77779pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.86108pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.80554pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 76.86108pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.87112pt\raise-57.70139pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.05896pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 123.00342pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 161.05896pt\raise-62.88889pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where the columns and the middle row are admissible short exact
sequences.
Then the upper and lower rows are admissible short exact sequences
as well.

Proof.

Let us be lazy and use the embedding
theorem
[
23
, Proposition A.2]
,
[
14
, Appendix A]
,

[
36
, Section A.6]
.
It is convenient to use
[
23
, Lemma A.3]
(or rather its obvious
extension to exact categories with a shift), which allows to restrict
the consideration to a small subcategory, thus avoiding
set-theoretical issues.
So we can assume that there exists an abelian category
\mathsf{A}
and
a fully faithful functor
\theta\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
representing
\mathsf{E}
as
a fully exact subcategory in
\mathsf{A}
.
Notice that the construction in the embedding theorem is natural
(with respect to equivalences of exact categories), and therefore
the shift autoequivalence
[1]\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}
induces a similar
autoequivalence
[1]\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
such that the embedding
functor
\theta
commutes with the shifts in
\mathsf{E}
and in
\mathsf{A}
.

Applying
\theta
to (
19
), we get a commutative

3\times 3
diagram in
\mathsf{A}
with exact columns and an exact middle row.
Now it follows that the upper leftmost morphism
\theta(A[-1])\longrightarrow\theta(B[-1])
is a monomorphism; hence so is the lower leftmost
morphism
\theta(A)\longrightarrow\theta(B)
.
Dually, it follows that the lower rightmost morphism
\theta(B)\longrightarrow\theta(C)
is an epimorphism; hence so is the upper rightmost morphism

\theta(B[-1])\longrightarrow\theta(C[-1])
.
Using the cohomology long exact sequence for our short exact sequence
of complexes in
\mathsf{A}
, one concludes that all the rows are exact in

\mathsf{A}
, hence also in
\mathsf{E}
.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then, for any closed morphism
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
of degree
0
in

\mathbf{E}
, the natural pair
B\longrightarrow\cone(f)\longrightarrow A[1]
of closed
morphisms of degree
0
in
\mathbf{E}
is an admissible short exact
sequence
0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\cone(f)\longrightarrow A[1]\longrightarrow 0
in any
DG-compatible exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

The sequence
0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\cone(f)\longrightarrow A[1]\longrightarrow 0
is split
exact in the additive category
\mathbf{E}^{0}
.
By Lemma
4.5
(cf. Lemma
3.11
),
it follows that the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}
takes this sequence to a split
short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
In view of the “only if” assertion of
Proposition
4.7
, we can conclude that
0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\cone(f)\longrightarrow A[1]\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact
sequence in any DG-compatible exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Alternatively, one can construct the desired admissible short exact
sequence
0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\cone(f)\longrightarrow A[1]\longrightarrow 0
as
the pushout of the admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow\cone(\mathrm{id}_{A})\longrightarrow A[1]\longrightarrow 0
by the morphism
f
, or as
the pullback of the admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\cone(\mathrm{id}_{B})\longrightarrow B[1]\longrightarrow 0
by the morphism
f[1]\colon A[1]\longrightarrow B[1]
.
∎

Let us say that an additive DG-category
\mathbf{E}
is
weakly
idempotent-complete
if the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is weakly
idempotent-complete in the sense of
[
14
, Section 7]
.
One can easily see that if an additive DG-category
\mathbf{E}
is
weakly idempotent-complete, then so is the DG-category
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a weakly idempotent-complete additive DG-category
with shifts and cones.
Then, for any DG-compatible exact category structure on

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
reflects admissible monomorphisms and admissible
epimorphisms.

Proof.

Let
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that
\Xi(f)

is an admissible monomorphism.
Then the morphism
\Xi(f)[1]
is an admissible monomorphism as well.
The natural morphism
A\longrightarrow\Xi(A)[1]
is an admissible monomorphism
by the definition of a DG-compatible exact structure.
Hence the composition
A\longrightarrow\Xi(A)[1]\longrightarrow\Xi(B)[1]
is
an admissible monomorphism.
This composition factorizes as
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow\Xi(B)[1]
.
Since the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is weakly idempotent-complete
by assumption, it follows that the morphism
A\longrightarrow B
is
an admissible monomorphism (use the result dual
to
[
14
, Proposition 7.6]
).
Thus
\Xi
reflects admissible monomorphisms; the argument for
admissible epimorphisms is dual.
∎

4.3.
\natural
-compatible exact structures

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Assume that we are given two exact category structures, one on
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and the other one on
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
We will say that the two exact structures are

\natural
-compatible
(with each other) if

•

both the exact structures are preserved by the shifts
[1]

and
[-1]
;

•

both the additive functors

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

from Lemma
3.4

preserve and reflect admissible short exact sequences.

Clearly, in a pair of
\natural
-compatible exact structures on

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
any one of the two exact
structures determines uniquely the other one.

We define an
exact DG-category

\mathbf{E}
as an additive
DG-category with shifts and cones endowed with a
\natural
-compatible
pair of exact structures on the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Lemma 4.11
.

A pair of exact structures on the additive categories

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is
\natural
-compatible if and
only if the following two conditions hold: both the exact structures
are DG-compatible (in the sense of the definition in
Section
4.2
) with the respective
DG-categories
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
, and both the additive
functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
are exact with respect to
these exact category structures.

Proof.

Assuming that both the exact category structures are preserved by
the shifts and both the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserve
admissible short exact sequences, we have to prove that both these
functors reflect admissible short exact sequences if and only if
both the exact structures are DG-compatible.
By Proposition
4.7
, the condition that both
the exact structures are DG-compatible means that both the functors

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}
preserve and reflect admissible
short exact sequences.
By Lemma
3.8
, we have

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}

(where
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}=\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}[1]
).

Now if both the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserve
(respectively, reflect) admissible short exact sequences, then
their compositions also preserve (resp., reflect) them.
Conversely, if the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves admissible
short exact sequences, while the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}

reflects them, then the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
also reflects them.
Similarly, if the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves admissible short
exact sequences, while the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}
reflects
them, then the functor
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}
also reflects them.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a weakly idempotent-complete additive DG-category
with shifts and cones.
Then, for any exact DG-category structure on
\mathbf{E}
, both
the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
reflect
admissible monomorphisms and admissible epimorphisms.

Proof.

Follows from Lemmas
4.10

and
4.11
.
To wit, if the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves admissible
short exact sequences (hence also admissible monomorphisms),
while the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}

reflects admissible monomorphisms, then the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}

also reflects admissible monomorphisms.
If the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves admissible short
exact sequences (hence also admissible monomorphisms), while
the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}

reflects admissible monomorphisms, then the functor
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}

also reflects admissible monomorphisms.
The same argument for admissible epimorphisms.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an additive category,
\mathsf{F}
be an exact category,
and
\Theta\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{F}
be a faithful, conservative additive
functor satisfying the following conditions:

•

if
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
is a morphism in
\mathsf{E}
such that

\Theta(g)
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}
, then
the morphism
g
has a kernel
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{E}
and
the morphism
\Theta(f)
is a kernel of
\Theta(g)
in
\mathsf{F}
;

•

if
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
is a morphism in
\mathsf{E}
such that

\Theta(f)
is an admissible monomorphism in
\mathsf{F}
, then
the morphism
f
has a cokernel
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
in
\mathsf{E}
and
the morphism
\Theta(g)
is a cokernel of
\Theta(f)
in
\mathsf{F}
.

Then the class of all composable pairs of morphisms
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
in
\mathsf{E}
whose image under
\Theta
is an admissible
short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\Theta(A)\longrightarrow\Theta(B)\longrightarrow\Theta(C)\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{F}
defines an exact category structure
on
\mathsf{E}
.
The functor
\Theta
preserves and reflects admissible short
exact sequences, admissible monomorphisms, and admissible epimorphisms
in this exact category structure on
\mathsf{E}
.

Proof.

Let
A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C
be a composable pair
of morphisms in
\mathsf{E}
such that
0\longrightarrow\Theta(A)\xrightarrow{\Theta(f)}\Theta(B)\xrightarrow{\Theta(g)}\Theta(C)\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{F}
.
Then the composition
gf
vanishes, since the composition

\Theta(g)\Theta(f)
vanishes and the functor
\Theta
is faithful.
Furthermore, the morphism
\Theta(f)
being a monomorphism in
\mathsf{F}

implies that the morphism
f
is a monomorphism in
\mathsf{E}
(also
since
\Theta
is faithful); and dually, the morphism
\Theta(g)

being an epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}
implies that the morphism
g
is
an epimorphism in
\mathsf{E}
.

Let us show that the morphism
f
is a kernel of the morphism
g
.
By assumption, since
\Theta(g)
is an admissible epimorphism,
the morphism
g
has a kernel
f^{\prime}\colon A^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
.
Then the morphism
f
factorizes as
A\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}A^{\prime}\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}B
.
By assumption, the morphism
\Theta(f^{\prime})
is a kernel of
the morphism
\Theta(g)
in
\mathsf{F}
.
But the morphism
\Theta(f)
is also a kernel of
\Theta(g)
, since

0\longrightarrow\Theta(A)\longrightarrow\Theta(B)\longrightarrow\Theta(C)\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence.
It follows that the morphism
\Theta(a)\colon\Theta(A)\longrightarrow\Theta(A^{\prime})

is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{F}
.
Since the functor
\Theta
is conservative, we can conclude that

a
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{E}
.
Dually one shows that the morphism
g
is a cokernel of
the morphism
f
.

By construction, the functor
\Theta
preserves and reflects
admissible short exact sequences; hence it also preserves admissible
monomorphisms and admissible epimorphisms.
Let us show that
\Theta
reflects admissible epimorphisms.
Let
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
be a morphism in
\mathsf{E}
such that
\Theta(g)

is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}
.
By assumption, it follows that the morphism
g
has a kernel

f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{E}
and
0\longrightarrow\Theta(A)\xrightarrow{\Theta(f)}\Theta(B)\xrightarrow{\Theta(g)}\Theta(C)\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{F}
.
Hence
0\longrightarrow A\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}C\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{E}
, and
g
is an admissible
epimorphism.
Dually,
\Theta
reflects admissible monomorphisms.

It follows immediately that the classes of admissible monomorphisms
and admissible epimorphisms in
\mathsf{E}
are closed under compositions.

Let us check that the class of admissible epimorphisms in
\mathsf{E}

is stable under pullbacks.
Let
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
be an admissible epimorphism and
c\colon C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C

be a morphism in
\mathsf{E}
.
Then the admissible epimorphism
\Theta(g)\colon\Theta(B)\longrightarrow\Theta(C)

in
\mathsf{F}
has a pullback by the morphism
\Theta(c)\colon\Theta(C^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(C)
.
Furthermore, the morphism
(\Theta(g),\Theta(c))\colon\Theta(B)\oplus\Theta(C^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(C)
is also an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}

(use
[
23
, 1st step of the proof in Section A.1]
or
the assertion dual to
[
14
, Proposition 2.12]
).

By assumption, this implies that the morphism
(g,c)\colon B\oplus C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C
has a kernel
(b,-g^{\prime})\colon B^{\prime}\longrightarrow B\oplus C^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{E}

whose image
(\Theta(b),-\Theta(g^{\prime}))\colon\Theta(B^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(B)\oplus\Theta(C^{\prime})
is a kernel of the morphism
(\Theta(g),\Theta(c))

in
\mathsf{F}
.
Then
g^{\prime}\colon B^{\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
is a pullback of the morphism
g\colon B\longrightarrow C

by the morphism
c\colon C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C
in
\mathsf{E}
, and
\Theta(g^{\prime})\colon\Theta(B^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(C^{\prime})
is a pullback of the morphism
\Theta(g)\colon\Theta(B)\longrightarrow\Theta(C)
by the morphism
\Theta(c)\colon\Theta(C^{\prime})\longrightarrow\Theta(C)
in
\mathsf{F}
.
It follows that
\Theta(g^{\prime})
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{F}
,
and therefore
g^{\prime}
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{E}
.
Dually one produces the pushouts of admissible monomorphisms in
\mathsf{E}
.
∎

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a weakly idempotent-complete additive DG-category
with shifts and cones.
Then a given DG-compatible exact category structure on

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
admits a (necessarily unique)
\natural
-compatible
exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
if and only if
the following two conditions hold:

•

any morphism
g
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which
\Phi(g)
is
an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
has a kernel
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
;

•

any morphism
f
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which
\Phi(f)
is
an admissible monomorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
has a cokernel
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

The “only if” assertion follows immediately from
Lemma
4.12
.
To prove the “if”, we need to construct an exact category
structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
The definition is obvious: let us say that
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

if
0\longrightarrow\Phi(A)\longrightarrow\Phi(B)\longrightarrow\Phi(C)\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Then the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves and reflects admissible
short exact sequences by the definition.
Since the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}

preserves and reflects admissible short exact sequences by assumption
and Proposition
4.7
, it then follows that
the functor
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}
also preserves and reflects admissible short
exact sequences.

It remains to check that the class of admissible short exact
sequences in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
that we have defined satisfies the axioms
of an exact category structure.
For this purpose, we apply
Lemma
4.13
to
the additive functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
(which is faithful and conservative
by Lemmas
3.4

and
3.12
).
We only have to check the assumptions of
Lemma
4.13
.

Let
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which

\Phi(g)
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Then, by the assumption of the theorem, the morphism
g

has a kernel
f\colon A\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
The morphism
\Phi(f)
is a kernel of the morphism
\Phi(g)

because the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
, being a right adjoint by
Lemma
3.4
, preserves all kernels.
The other assumption of
Lemma
4.13
is dual.
∎

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an idempotent-complete additive DG-category
with shifts and twists.
Then any DG-compatible exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})

admits a unique
\natural
-compatible exact category structure
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

This is a corollary of
Theorem
4.14
,
whose assumptions we have to check.
Let
g\colon B\longrightarrow C
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Then the morphism
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
The functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves kernels, being a right adjoint
(to
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}[-1]
) by Lemma
3.4
;
so it follows that the morphism
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)
has a kernel
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
By Lemma
3.8
, we have
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)\simeq\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)
; hence the morphism
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(g)
has a kernel.
It remains to apply Lemma
4.6
(a) in order to
conclude that the morphism
g
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
under
the assumptions of the corollary.
The other assumption of
Theorem
4.14
is dual.
∎

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
In this setting

(a)
if
g
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
the morphism
\Psi^{+}(g)
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, then
the morphism
g
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
;

(b)
if
f
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
the morphism
\Psi^{+}(f)
has a cokernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, then
the morphism
f
has a cokernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

Proof.

Let us prove part (a); part (b) is dual.
Let
Y^{\natural}=(Y,\sigma_{X})
and
Z^{\natural}=(Z,\sigma_{Z})
be two objects
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Then
\Psi^{+}(Y^{\natural})=Y
and
\Psi^{+}(Z^{\natural})=Z
.
Let
g\colon Y^{\natural}\longrightarrow Z^{\natural}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
;
this means that
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and

g\sigma_{Y}=\sigma_{Z}g
.
Assume that the morphism
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
has a kernel
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Let us construct a kernel of the morphism
g\colon Y^{\natural}\longrightarrow Z^{\natural}

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

Given an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
, we will use the notation
\iota_{A}
,

\pi_{A}
,
\iota^{\prime}_{A}
,
\pi^{\prime}_{A}
from the proofs of
Lemmas
3.4

and
4.6
.
Given an object
T^{\natural}=(T,\sigma_{T})\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, consider
the morphism
\beta_{T}=\iota^{\prime}_{T}+\iota_{T}\sigma_{T}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(T,\Xi(T))
.
We have
d(\beta_{T})=\iota_{T}-\iota_{T}d(\sigma_{T})=0
, so
\beta_{T}
is
a morphism
T\longrightarrow\Xi(T)
in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Furthermore,
\pi_{T}\colon\Xi(T)\longrightarrow T
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
,
and the equation
\pi_{T}\beta_{T}=\mathrm{id}_{T}
holds in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

We need to express the equation
\sigma_{T}^{2}=0
, which is a part of
the definition of an object of the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, as
an equation on morphisms in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Notice that
d(\sigma_{T})=\mathrm{id}_{T}\neq 0
, so
\sigma_{T}
cannot be
directly viewed as a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
(even after the shift
is taken care of).
Let us start with the shift: recall the notation

s_{A}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{1}(A,A[-1])
from the proof of
Lemma
4.6
.
So
s_{T}\sigma_{T}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{0}(T,T[-1])
is a morphism in
the category
\mathbf{A}^{0}
.

Applying the functor
\widetilde{\Xi}
from Lemma
4.5
,
we obtain a morphism
\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{T}\sigma_{T})\colon\Xi(T)\longrightarrow\Xi(T[-1])
in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Now the equation
\sigma_{T}^{2}=0
can be expressed as

\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{T}\sigma_{T})[-1]\circ\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{T}\sigma_{T})=\widetilde{\Xi}((s_{T}\sigma_{T})[-1]\circ s_{T}\sigma_{T})=\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{T[-1]}s_{T}\sigma_{T}^{2})=0
.
Conversely, if
U\in\mathbf{A}
is an object and
\sigma\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(U,U)
is an endomorphism of degree
-1
, then

s_{U}\sigma_{U}
is a morphism in
\mathbf{A}^{0}
and
\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{U}\sigma)

is a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
; the equation

\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{U}\sigma)[-1]\circ\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{U}\sigma)=0
holds
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
if and only if the equation
\sigma^{2}=0
holds
in
\mathbf{A}^{*}
(because the functor
\widetilde{\Xi}
is faithful).

Returning to the situation at hand, consider the commutative diagram
of solid arrows in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{Y}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{Z}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(f)}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{\Xi(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Y}}
\textstyle{\Xi(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Z}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z}

Since the morphism
f
is a kernel of
g
and the morphism
\Xi(f)

is a kernel of
\Xi(g)
(by Lemma
4.1
),
there exists a unique morphism
\beta\colon X\longrightarrow\Xi(X)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

making the leftmost square commutative.
The equation
\pi_{Y}\beta_{Y}=\mathrm{id}_{Y}
implies
\pi_{X}\beta=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.

Put
\sigma=\pi^{\prime}\beta\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{-1}(X,X)
.
Then we have
\beta=(\iota^{\prime}\pi+\iota\pi^{\prime})\beta=\iota^{\prime}+\iota\sigma
,
hence
0=d(\beta)=\iota-\iota d(\sigma)
.
Multiplying with
\pi^{\prime}
on the left, we deduce that
d(\sigma)=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
Furthermore, we have a commutative diagram in the category
\mathbf{A}^{0}

(20)

76.73428pt\raise-57.16666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f[-1]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 114.68051pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 84.68051pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 114.68051pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.13895pt\raise-57.16666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g[-1]}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 198.81937pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 168.81937pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 198.81937pt\raise-63.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z[-1]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

with the vertical compositions equal to
s_{X}\sigma
,
s_{Y}\sigma_{Y}
,
and
s_{Z}\sigma_{Z}
, respectively.
Collapsing the middle row, applying
\widetilde{\Xi}
and building up
the composition with a shift of the same diagram, we obtain
a commutative diagram in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(f)}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{X}\sigma)}
\textstyle{\Xi(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g)}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Y}\sigma_{Y})}
\textstyle{\Xi(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Z}\sigma_{Z})}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi(X[-1])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(f[-1])}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{X}\sigma)[-1]}
\textstyle{\Xi(Y[-1])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g[-1])}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Y}\sigma_{Y})[-1]}
\textstyle{\Xi(Z[-1])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Z}\sigma_{Z})[-1]}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi(X[-2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(f[-2])}
\textstyle{\Xi(Y[-2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(g[-2])}
\textstyle{\Xi(Z[-2])}

Now the equation
\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Y}\sigma_{Y})[-1]\circ\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{Y}\sigma_{Y})=0
implies

\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{X}\sigma)[-1]\circ\widetilde{\Xi}(s_{X}\sigma)=0
,
because
\Xi(f[-2])
is a monomorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Thus
\sigma^{2}=0
.

Finally, we can put
\sigma_{X}=\sigma
and
X^{\natural}=(X,\sigma_{X})
,
producing an object of the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
The equation
f\sigma_{X}=\sigma_{Y}f
follows easily from
the commutativity of (
20
);
so the morphism
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
defines
a morphism
f\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow Y^{\natural}
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

In order to check that
f
is a kernel of
g
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
,
it suffices to observe that
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(f)=\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}(f)

is a kernel of
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(g)=\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}(g)

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
(since
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}(f)=f[1]
is a kernel of

\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}(g)=g[1]
in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, and the functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
preserves kernels), and apply
Lemma
4.4
(a) for the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
∎

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then any DG-compatible exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

admits a unique
\natural
-compatible exact category structure
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Proof.

The definition of the exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is
obvious: we say that
0\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
if

0\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}(X)\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}(Y)\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}(Z)\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Then the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves and reflects admissible
short exact sequences by the definition.
Since the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
preserves
and reflects admissible short exact sequences by assumption, it then
follows that the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
also preserves and reflects
admissible short exact sequences.

To check that the class of admissible short exact sequences in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
that we have defined satisfies the axioms of
an exact category structure, we apply
Lemma
4.13
to
the additive functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
.
This functor is faithful and conservative by
Lemmas
3.4
and
3.12
.
We have to check the assumptions of
Lemma
4.13
.

Let
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
for which

\Psi^{+}(g)
is an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Then the morphism
\Psi^{+}(g)
has a kernel in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
By Lemma
4.16
, it follows that the morphism
g

has a kernel
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
The morphism
\Psi^{+}(f)
is a kernel of the morphism
\Psi^{+}(g)

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, because the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
, being a right adjoint
(to
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}[-1]
) by Lemma
3.4
,
preserves all kernels.
The other assumption of
Lemma
4.13
is dual.

Alternatively, one could assume that
\mathbf{E}
is idempotent-complete,
notice that the DG-category
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
always has all twists,
and argue similarly to the proof of
Theorem
4.14
combined
with Corollary
4.15
,
using Lemma
4.6
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

In view of Theorem
4.17
, we could have
simply defined an exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
as an additive DG-category
with shifts and cones endowed with a DG-compatible exact category
structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, in the sense of
Section
4.2
.
This would be a simple and straightforward definition.

Instead, we have made a long journey through the construction and
study of the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
in Section
3
,
the study of the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}
in Section
4.1
,
the proof of Lemma
4.16
, etc., only to come to
the conclusion that having a DG-compatible exact structure on

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is always sufficient to uniquely recover what we call
the exact DG-category structure.

The upside of the long journey is that we have also produced
an exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
together with the exact functors

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
along the way.
For naturally occurring DG-categories
\mathbf{E}
, having an exact structure
on “the category of underlying graded objects” (whose role is played
by the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
in our formalism;
cf. the examples in Section
3.4
) is no less
important than having an exact structure on “the category of
DG-objects”
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Arguably one could say that, e. g., the abelian exact structure on
the category of graded modules conceptually and logically precedes
the abelian exact structure on the category of DG-modules and
closed morphisms of degree
0
between them.
Having an exact structure on the category of underlying graded objects
is also important for the definitions and study of the derived
categories of the second kind for exact DG-categories, which is our
main aim.

The point is that complexes of projective or injective objects in exact
categories and graded-projective or graded-injective CDG-modules are
crucial for the definitions of derived categories of the second kind
in the sense of Becker
[
7
, Section 1.3]
,
[
53
]
and for
the most important properties of derived categories of the second kind
in the sense of
[
34
,
35
]
and the present paper.
This means objects whose underlying graded objects are projective
or injective.
For an exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
, the definitions of graded-projective
and graded-injective objects in
\mathbf{E}
refer to the projective and
injective objects in the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})

(see Section
5
, where these definitions
and the mentioned important properties are collected).
The exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
(or its exact subcategory
\mathsf{K}
,
in the context of an exact DG-pair
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
) also appears in
the formulations of the main results of
Sections
6
–
8
.

Example 4.19
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then, in any exact DG-category structure on
\mathbf{E}
, all the composable
pairs of morphisms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
taken to split short exact sequences
by the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
must be admissible short exact sequences,
and all the composable pairs of morphisms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
taken to
split short exact sequences by the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
must be
admissible short exact sequences.

Notice that iterating the functors
\Phi
and
\Psi
any further
does
not
expand the classes of “necessary admissible short
exact sequences”.
Specifically, if
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
is a composable pair of
morphisms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that
0\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is
a split short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, then
0\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is a split short exact
sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Indeed, additive functors take split short exact sequences to split
short exact sequences; so if
0\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is
a split short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, then

0\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is a split short exact
sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
The functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}
is isomorphic to the direct sum

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\oplus\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}[1]
by Lemma
3.11

(as
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(E)
is naturally a twist of the direct sum

E\oplus E[-1]
for every object
E\in\mathbf{E}
).
It remains to point out that a direct summand of a split short
exact sequence is always a split short exact sequence in order to
conclude that
0\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is a split short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Similarly, if
X^{\natural}\longrightarrow Y^{\natural}\longrightarrow Z^{\natural}
is a composable
pair of morphisms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
such that
0\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\natural})\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y^{\natural})\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(Z^{\natural})\longrightarrow 0
is a split short exact sequence
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
, then
0\longrightarrow\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(X^{\natural})\longrightarrow\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y^{\natural})\longrightarrow\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}(Z^{\natural})\longrightarrow 0
is a split short
exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
This is provable using the fact that the functor

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{E}}\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\circ\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}
is isomorphic to

\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\oplus\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{-}
.

One can now define the
minimal DG-compatible exact structure
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
as the exact structure given by the class of
all composable pairs of morphisms whose image under
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}

is a split short exact sequence.
Arguing similarly to the proof of
Theorem
4.17
, one shows that
Lemma
4.13
is
applicable to the functor
\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}\colon\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{F}
, with the split exact structure on
\mathsf{F}
.
Hence what we have defined is indeed an exact category structure
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Assuming that the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
is idempotent-complete and
has twists, one can similarly define the
minimal DG-compatible
exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
as the exact structure given by
the class of all composable pairs of morphisms whose image under

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
is a split short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Arguing similarly to the proofs of
Theorem
4.14

and Corollary
4.15
, one
checks that Lemma
4.13

is applicable to the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})=\mathsf{F}
, with the split exact structure on
\mathsf{F}
.
Hence the class of all composable pairs of morphisms turned into
split short exact sequences by
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
is indeed an exact
category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Finally, it is clear from the discussion of compositions of
the functors
\Phi
and
\Psi
above that the two minimal DG-compatible
exact structures on the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
are
\natural
-compatible with each other.
Thus we have described the
minimal exact DG-category structure

on an idempotent-complete additive DG-category
\mathbf{E}
with shifts
and twists.

4.4.
Inheriting exact DG-category structure

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category and
\mathsf{F}\subset\mathsf{E}
be a full additive
subcategory.
We will say that
\mathsf{F}

inherits an exact category structure

from
\mathsf{E}
if the class of all admissible short exact sequences
in
\mathsf{E}
whose terms belong to
\mathsf{F}
defines an exact category
structure on
\mathsf{F}
.

In particular, if the full subcategory
\mathsf{F}
is closed under extensions
in
\mathsf{E}
, then it inherits an exact category structure.
In this case, the category
\mathsf{F}
endowed with the inherited exact
category structure is called a
fully exact subcategory
in
\mathsf{E}

[
14
, Sections 10.5 and 13.3]
.
Another particular case occurs when
\mathsf{F}
is closed under both
the kernels of admissible epimorphisms and the cokernels of admissible
monomorphisms in
\mathsf{E}
.
In this case,
\mathsf{F}
also inherits an exact category structure
from
\mathsf{E}

[
36
, Section A.5(3)(b)]
.

Quite generally, full subcategories inheriting an exact category
structure are characterized by the following lemma (which can be
also found in
[
16
, Theorem 2.6]
).

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category and
\mathsf{F}\subset\mathsf{E}
be a full
additive subcategory.
Then
\mathsf{F}
inherits an exact category structure from
\mathsf{E}
if
and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

for any commutative diagram in
\mathsf{E}

, the object

also belongs to

;

3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.59897pt\raise-23.65775pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.04515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-8.15279pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.15279pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.15279pt\raise-31.62003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 73.0591pt\raise-5.55554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

with an admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
and a pushout square
A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow B^{\prime}
,
A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}\longrightarrow B^{\prime}
such that the objects
A
,
B
,
C
, and
A^{\prime}
belong
to
\mathsf{F}
, the object
B^{\prime}
also belongs to
\mathsf{F}
.

Proof.

“Only if”: assuming that
\mathsf{F}
inherits an exact category structure
from
\mathsf{E}
, let us prove (i).
Since
\mathsf{F}
is an exact category, the admissible short exact
sequence
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
has a pullback
by the morphism
C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C
in the category
\mathsf{F}
; denote this
pullback by
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0
.
Then
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible
short exact sequence in
\mathsf{F}
, and consequently in
\mathsf{E}
.
It follows that the square
B^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C
,
B^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C
is a pullback square in
\mathsf{E}

[
36
, Proposition A.2 and Corollary A.3]
, hence

B^{\prime}\simeq B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{F}
.
The proof of (ii) is dual.

“If”: the only nonobvious property to check is that the classes
of admissible monomorphisms and admissible epimorphisms in
\mathsf{F}
are
closed under compositions.
Here one observes that the kernel object of the composition of two
admissible epimorphisms
g=g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}
(in an exact category
\mathsf{E}
)
is a pullback of
g^{\prime\prime}
by the kernel of
g^{\prime}
, and similarly,
the cokernel object of the composition of two admissible monomorphisms

f=f^{\prime}f^{\prime\prime}
is a pushout of
f^{\prime}
by the cokernel of
f^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\Theta\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}
be an exact functor between exact
categories, and let
\mathsf{H}\subset\mathsf{G}
be a full additive subcategory
inheriting an exact category structure.
Then the full preimage
\mathsf{F}=\Theta^{-1}(\mathsf{H})\subset\mathsf{E}
is a full
additive subcategory inheriting an exact category structure
from
\mathsf{E}
.

Proof.

It suffices to check the conditions (i) and (ii) of
Lemma
4.20
for the full
subcategory
\mathsf{F}\subset\mathsf{E}
.
Suppose that we are given a pullback diagram (
21
)
in
\mathsf{E}
with
A
,
B
,
C
,
C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{F}
and
B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}
.
Then
0\longrightarrow B^{\prime}\longrightarrow B\oplus C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{E}
(see
[
23
, 1st step
of the proof in Section A.1]
or the assertion dual
to
[
14
, Proposition 2.12]
).
Applying the functor
\Theta
to (
21
), we obtain
a similar pullback diagram in the exact category
\mathsf{G}
(since
the functor
\Theta
preserves admissible short exact sequences).
Since the full subcategory
\mathsf{H}
inherits an exact category structure
from
\mathsf{G}
, by the other implication of
Lemma
4.20
we have
\Theta(B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{H}
, hence
B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{F}
.
The condition (ii) is dual.
Alternatively, one could refer to the more
general
[
36
, Section A.5(4)]
.
∎

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones, and let

\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be a full additive DG-subcategory closed under
shifts and cones.
Then the DG-category
\mathbf{F}^{\natural}
is naturally a full DG-subcategory
in
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
.
The functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{F}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
and

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{F}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
form commutative
square diagrams with the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
and
the additive inclusion functors
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
If the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}
is closed under direct summands
in
\mathbf{E}
, then the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}^{\natural}
is closed under
direct summands in
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
.

Assume that a pair of
\natural
-compatible exact category structures is
defined on the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
,
making
\mathbf{E}
an exact DG-category.
We will say that the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}

inherits an exact
DG-category structure
from
\mathbf{E}
if both the full additive
subcategories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
inherit exact category structures.
Obviously, in this case the inherited pair of exact structures
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
is
\natural
-compatible,
so
\mathbf{F}
becomes an exact DG-category.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a weakly idempotent-complete exact DG-category,
and let
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be a full additive DG-subcategory closed
under shifts, cones, and direct summands.
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
inherits
an exact category structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Then the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
inherits an exact category
structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
if and only if the following two
conditions hold:

•

for any admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the objects
B
and
C

belong to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and the object
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)
belongs to

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, the object
A
also belongs to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
;

•

for any admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the objects
A
and
B

belong to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and the object
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)
belongs to

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, the object
C
also belongs to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.

Proof.

“Only if”: the assumptions of the proposition imply that
the DG-category
\mathbf{F}
is weakly idempotent-complete.
If the additive subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
inherits an exact category
structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, then the inherited exact category
structure is DG-compatible.
By Lemma
4.10
, it
follows that the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{F}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})

reflects admissible monomorphisms and admissible epimorphisms.
Now if
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible
short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
B
,
C\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, then
0\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact
sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with the terms in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, so it is
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
It follows that
\Xi_{\mathbf{F}}(B)\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{F}}(C)
is an admissible
epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, hence
B\longrightarrow C
is an admissible
epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
So there exists an admissible short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
Since the exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
is inherited from

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, the latter sequence must be an admissible short
exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
as well, and we can conclude
that
A\simeq K\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
The second property is dual.

“If”: it suffices to check the conditions (i) and (ii) of
Lemma
4.20
for the full
subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Suppose that we are given a pullback diagram (
21
)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
A
,
B
,
C
,
C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and

B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Following the proof of Lemma
4.21

applied to the exact functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
and the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
, we see that
0\longrightarrow B^{\prime}\longrightarrow B\oplus C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
.
Consequently,
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(B^{\prime})\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
By assumption of the proposition, it follows that
B^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
The condition (ii) is dual.
Alternatively, one could refer to
Theorem
4.14
, but
that would be a more indirect, roundabout argument using
the weak idempotent-completeness assumption on
\mathbf{E}
.
∎

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category, and let
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be
a full additive DG-subcategory closed under shifts and cones.
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
inherits an exact
category structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Then the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
inherits an exact
category structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
So the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}
inherits an exact DG-category
structure from
\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

For any DG-category
\mathbf{E}
with shifts and cones, and any full
DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
closed under shifts and cones,
the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is
the full preimage of the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

under the functor
\Psi^{+}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Hence it suffices to apply Lemma
4.21

to the exact functor
\Theta=\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and the full subcategory
\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Alternatively, one could notice that a short sequence
0\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
is an admissible short
exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
if and only if
0\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{F}}^{+}(X)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{F}}^{+}(Y)\longrightarrow\Psi_{\mathbf{F}}^{+}(Z)\longrightarrow 0

is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, and apply
Theorem
4.17
to the DG-category
\mathbf{F}
.
∎

Corollary 4.24
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category, and let
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be
a full additive DG-subcategory closed under shifts, twists, and
direct summands.
Then the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
inherits an exact category
structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

if and only if
the full
subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
inherits an exact category structure
from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Hence the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}
inherits an exact DG-category
structure from
\mathbf{E}
if any one of the two conditions holds.

Proof.

The “only if” assertion is provided by
Proposition
4.23
.
To prove the “if”, it suffices to check that the assumptions
of Proposition
4.22
are
satisfied (notice that the weak idempotent-completeness assumption
was not used in the proof of the “if” implication in
Proposition
4.22
).
Indeed, if
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
is a full DG-subcategory closed under
twists and direct summands, and the object
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(G)
belongs to

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
for some
G\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, then
G
also belongs to

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, since
G
is a direct summand of a twist
G\oplus G[-1]

of the object
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(G)
.
∎

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category, and let
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be
a full additive DG-subcategory closed under shifts and cones.
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

is closed under extensions.
Then the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})

is closed under extensions.
The converse implication holds assuming additionally that
\mathbf{F}

is closed under twists and direct summands in
\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

To prove the direct implication, let
0\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 0
be an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})

with
X
,
Z\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
.
Then
0\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(X)\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y)\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(Z)\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(X)
,
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(Z)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
By assumption, it follows that
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(Y)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, hence

Y\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
.

To prove the converse, let
0\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
be
an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

with
A
,
C\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
Then
0\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\longrightarrow 0
is an admissible short exact sequence in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})

with
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(A)
,
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(C)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
.
By assumption, it follows that
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
, hence

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
As
B
is a direct summand of a twist
B\oplus B[-1]
of the object

\Xi_{\mathbf{E}}(B)
, we can conclude that
B\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
∎

The obvious analogue of
Lemma
4.25
, provable in
the same way, holds for the closedness under the kernels of admissible
epimorphisms or the cokernels of admissible monomorphisms in lieu of
the closedness under extensions.

Example 4.26
.

The following counterexample shows that the technical additional
assumptions are indeed necessary in
Theorem
4.14

and Proposition
4.22
, and
also that the assumptions of existence of twists or closedness
under twists are necessary in
Corollaries
4.15

and
4.24
, as well as
in Lemma
4.25
and in the previous
paragraph (concerning the kernels of admissible epimorphisms and
the cokernels of admissible monomorphisms).
It is an example of a well-behaved (in fact, abelian) DG-category

\mathbf{A}
with a badly behaved full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{B}\subset\mathbf{A}

whose induced full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{B}^{\natural}\subset\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is
well-behaved.

Choose a field
k
, and let
\mathsf{A}
denote the abelian category of
morphisms of
k
-vector spaces (i. e., representations of
the quiver
\bullet\to\bullet
).
Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
be the DG-category of complexes in
\mathsf{A}
.
Denote by
\mathsf{E}\subset\mathsf{A}
the fully exact subcategory of
injective morphisms of
k
-vector spaces (this is the full
subcategory of projective objects in
\mathsf{A}
).

Let
\mathbf{B}
be the full additive DG-subcategory in
\mathbf{A}
consisting
of all the direct summands (in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
) of those complexes
in
\mathsf{A}
that can be represented as a direct sum
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\oplus X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,
where
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\in\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{E})
is a complex in
\mathsf{E}
and
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
a contractible complex in
\mathsf{A}
.
The DG-subcategory
\mathbf{B}\subset\mathbf{A}
is obviously closed under shifts;
let us show that it is also closed under cones.
Indeed, let
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
F^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be two complexes from
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{E})
, and

X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be two contractible complexes in
\mathsf{A}
.
Then any morphism
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
or
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow F^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in

\mathbf{A}
is homotopic to zero.
In any DG-category
\mathbf{A}
, the cones of homotopic closed morphisms
are isomorphic as objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Consequently, in the situation at hand the cone of any closed morphism

E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\oplus X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow F^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\oplus Y^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathbf{A}
has the form

G^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\oplus Z^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, where
G^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\in\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{E})
and
Z^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
a contractible complex in
\mathsf{A}
.

The functor
\Upsilon_{\mathsf{A}}
from Example
3.16

provides an equivalence of additive categories
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})\simeq\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
(where
\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
is the category of graded objects
in
\mathsf{A}
) forming a commutative triangle diagram with the forgetful
functor
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})=\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
and the functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Hence both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

are abelian, so
\mathbf{A}
is indeed an abelian DG-category in the sense
of the next Section
4.5

(cf. Example
4.39
below).
Furthermore, one can easily see from the constructions that

\mathbf{B}^{\natural}=\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
(since all the contractible objects of
\mathbf{A}
belong
to
\mathbf{B}
).
Consequently, we have
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B}^{\natural})=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\simeq\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})
.

Let us introduce notation for the indecomposable objects

I_{01}=(0\to k)
,
I_{10}=(k\to 0)
, and
I_{11}=(k\to k)

of the abelian category
\mathsf{A}
.
So we have
I_{01}\in\mathsf{E}
and
I_{11}\in\mathsf{E}
, but
I_{10}\notin\mathsf{E}
.
Let us show that the one-term complex
I_{10}\in\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
does not belong to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})\subset\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
Indeed, suppose that
I_{10}
is a direct summand in a complex of
the form
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\oplus X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, where
E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{E})
and
X^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is a contractible complex.
Since any morphism from
I_{10}
to an object of the full
subcategory
\mathsf{E}\subset\mathsf{A}
vanishes, it follows that any morphism

I_{10}\longrightarrow E^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
vanishes in
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
Hence
I_{10}
is a direct summand of a contractible complex in

\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
, an obvious contradiction.

The additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
is
not
abelian, because
the monomorphism
i\colon I_{01}\longrightarrow I_{11}
has no cokernel in it.
Indeed, if such a cokernel existed, it would have to be preserved
by the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{B}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B}^{\natural})
,
which means just the forgetful functor
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{A})

restricted to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})\subset\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
.
This would imply that the cokernel of
i
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
agrees
with the cokernel of
i
in
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
; but the cokernel
I_{10}

of the morphism
i
in
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})
does not belong to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
.

Moreover, the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
does
not
inherit
an exact category structure from
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and does
not

admit an exact category structure
\natural
-compatible with the abelian
exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B}^{\natural})
.
Indeed, if it did, then such an exact structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})

would be DG-compatible, and by
Lemma
4.10
it would
follow that the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{B}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})

reflects admissible monomorphisms (since the DG-category
\mathbf{B}
is
additive and idempotent-complete with shifts and cones).

On the other hand, the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(I_{01})\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(I_{11})\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(I_{10})\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{C}(\mathsf{A})

obtained by applying
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}
to the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow I_{01}\longrightarrow I_{11}\longrightarrow I_{10}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{A}
would be
admissible in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
, since it is admissible in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

and its terms belong to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
.
But the monomorphism
i\colon I_{01}\longrightarrow I_{11}
cannot be admissible
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})
, as it has no cokernel in this category.

In the preceding results in this
Section
4.4
,
we were starting with a full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
.
The next proposition starts with an additive subcategory

\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Proposition 4.27
.

(a)
Let
\mathbf{E}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and
cones, and let
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
be a full additive
subcategory preserved by the shift functors
[n]
,
n\in\Gamma
.
Let
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
be the full DG-subcategory consisting of all
objects
F\in\mathbf{E}
such that
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(F)\in\mathsf{L}
, and let

\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}=\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}^{-1}(\mathsf{L})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
be
the full subcategory consisting of all objects
\widetilde{L}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
such that
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}(\widetilde{L})\in\mathsf{L}
.
Then
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
is a full additive DG-subcategory closed
under shifts and twists, and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})=\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
as
a full subcategory in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

(b)
In the context of (a), assume that the full subcategory

\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is closed under direct summands.
Then so is the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
.
Furthermore, given an object
F\in\mathbf{E}
, one has
F\in\mathbf{F}
if and
only if
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(F)\in\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
.

(c)
In the context of (a), assume that
\mathbf{E}
is an exact
DG-category and the full additive subcategory
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
inherits an exact category structure.
Then the full additive DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
inherits
an exact DG-category structure.

(d)
In the context of (c), assume that the full
subcategory
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is closed under extensions.
Then the inclusion
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})
holds.

Proof.

Part (a): the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
is additive and
preserved by the shifts, since the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is.
Furthermore,
\mathbf{F}
is closed under twists in
\mathbf{E}
because
the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
takes twists to isomorphisms
(see Lemma
3.11
).
Finally, an object
X\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
belongs to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})

if and only the object
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(X)
belongs to
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, which
in our context means that the object
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}(X)\simeq\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\Psi_{\mathbf{E}}^{+}(X)[1]
belongs to
\mathsf{L}
.

Part (b): the first assertion is obvious.
To prove the second one, notice that the functor
\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}\circ\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is isomorphic to

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\oplus\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}[-1]
(cf. Example
4.19
).
Hence, for any given object
F\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, one has
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(F)\in\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
if and only if
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}(F)\in\mathsf{L}
.

Part (c): in view of
Proposition
4.23

or Corollary
4.24
,
it suffices to check that the full additive subcategory

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
inherits an exact category structure.
For this purpose, one can apply
Lemma
4.21
to the exact functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
and
the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
In part (d), the inclusion
\mathsf{L}\subset\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
follows from
the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow L[1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{E}^{\natural}}(L)\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow 0
, which is admissible in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
for any
L\in\mathsf{L}

by the definition of a DG-compatible exact structure.
∎

Example 4.28
.

The construction of Proposition
4.27
does
provide full DG-subcategories inheriting exact DG-category
structures, but it does not always work quite as one would expect.
The following example is thematic.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring and
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
be
the DG-category of left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Then the functor
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
from
Example
3.17
provides an equivalence of
additive categories
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\simeq\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
(where

R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is the category of graded left
R^{*}
-modules) forming
a commutative triangle diagram with the forgetful functor

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})\longrightarrow R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
and the functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
The equivalence
\Upsilon_{\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}}
also forms a commutative
triangle diagram with the functor
G^{+}\colon R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}})
and the functor
\Psi_{\mathbf{A}}^{+}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are
abelian, so
\mathbf{A}
is an abelian DG-category in the sense of the next
Section
4.5
(cf. Example
4.40
).

Let
\mathsf{L}\subset R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
be the full subcategory of projective
(or flat) graded
R^{*}
-modules.
Then one certainly has
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}^{\natural}}(\mathsf{L})\subset\mathsf{L}
, as
the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is closed under extensions in

R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
For the same reason,
\mathsf{L}
also inherits an exact category structure
from the abelian exact structure on
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
The full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{A}
assigned to the class
\mathsf{L}

by the construction of Proposition
4.27

consists of all left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
whose underling graded

R^{*}
-modules are projective (respectively, flat).
However, the full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F}^{\natural})=\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}\subset R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
can be
wider
than
\mathsf{L}
.
In fact,
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}
is the full subcategory consisting of all
graded
R^{*}
-modules
\widetilde{L}
for which the underlying graded

R^{*}
-module of the CDG-module
G^{+}(\widetilde{L})
is projective
(resp., flat).

Here is a specific counterexample.
Consider the following DG-ring
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(R^{*},d)
.
The graded ring
R^{*}=k[\epsilon]
, where
\epsilon\in R^{-1}

and
\epsilon^{2}=0
, is the exterior algebra in one variable over
a field
k
.
The differential
d
is given by
d(\epsilon)=1
; so
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

is an acyclic DG-algebra over
k
.

Consider the graded
R^{*}
-module
L^{*}=k=R^{*}/\epsilon R^{*}
,
concentrated in the cohomological degree
0
.
By construction, the elements of cohomological degree
n\in\mathbb{Z}

in the graded
R^{*}
-module
G^{+}(L^{*})
are formal expressions

l+\delta l^{\prime}
with
l\in L^{n}
and
l^{\prime}\in L^{n-1}
.
The action of
R^{*}
in
G^{+}(L^{*})
is given by the rule

r(l+\delta l^{\prime})=rl-(-1)^{|r|}d(r)l^{\prime}+(-1)^{|r|}\delta rl^{\prime}
for all

r\in R^{|r|}
.
Now, denoting by
l_{0}=1\in L^{0}
a nonzero vector in the one-dimensional

k
-vector space
L^{*}
, we have
\epsilon(0+\delta l_{0})=l_{0}+\delta 0
.
Thus
G^{+}(L^{*})
is a free graded
R^{*}
-module with one
generator
0+\delta l_{0}\in G^{+}(L^{*})
of cohomological degree
1
.
But
L^{*}
is
not
a flat graded
R^{*}
-module.

In fact, in this example one has
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}=R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, as
the graded
R^{*}
-module
G^{+}(L^{*})
is projective for
any
graded

R^{*}
-module
L^{*}
.
Moreover, the graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}
is projective for any
DG-module
(M^{*},d_{M})
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
; so one has
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{A}
.
Indeed, viewed as a complex with the differential given by
the action of
\epsilon
, the underlying graded
R^{*}
-module
M^{*}

of any DG-module
(M^{*},d_{M})
is a contractible complex, with
a contracting homotopy
d_{M}
.

4.5.
Abelian DG-categories

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
We will say that the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is
abelian
if both
the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are
abelian.
(As we will see in Proposition
4.34

below, it suffices that the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
be abelian.)

Lemma 4.29
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an abelian DG-category.
Then the abelian exact category structure on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is

\natural
-compatible with the abelian exact category structure
on
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
So the pair of abelian exact category structures defines
an exact DG-category structure on
\mathbf{A}
.

The exact DG-category structure defined in the lemma is called
the
abelian exact DG-category structure
on an abelian
DG-category
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

Both the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\Psi_{\mathbf{A}}
have left and right
adjoints by Lemma
3.4
; so they preserve
kernels and cokernels.
Therefore, viewed as functors between the abelian (by assumption)
categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
, they are exact.
Furthermore, both the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\Psi_{\mathbf{A}}
are faithful
by Lemma
3.4
.
A faithful exact functor between abelian categories reflects short
exact sequences.
∎

Example 4.30
.

The following example shows that, in an idempotent-complete exact
DG-category
\mathbf{E}
, the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
can be abelian
with the abelian exact structure, while the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

is not abelian.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
be the DG-category of complexes of vector
spaces over a field
k
.
Then, following Example
3.16
, the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is equivalent to the category of graded
vector spaces
\mathsf{G}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
.
So both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})=\mathsf{C}(k{\operatorname{\mathrm{--mod}}})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are abelian, and
\mathbf{A}
is an abelian DG-category.
By Lemma
4.29
, the DG-category
\mathbf{A}

carries the abelian exact DG-category structure.

Consider the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{E}\subset\mathbf{A}
whose objects are
the acyclic complexes of
k
-vector spaces (cf. Examples
3.13
).
Then the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}
coincides with the whole
ambient DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
, that is
\mathbf{E}^{\natural}=\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
The full subcategory
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is closed under
extensions and direct summands (as well as the kernels of epimorphisms
and the cokernels of monomorphisms); so it inherits an exact
category structure.
Thus the full additive DG-subcategory
\mathbf{E}
inherits an exact
DG-category structure from
\mathbf{A}
.

So
\mathbf{E}
becomes an idempotent-complete exact DG-category in which
the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is abelian with the abelian
exact category structure.
But the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is far from being abelian:
it does not even have kernels or cokernels of some morphisms.

Lemma 4.31
.

(a)
Let
\mathsf{A}
be an additive category with kernels and
cokernels,
\mathsf{B}
be an abelian category, and
\Theta\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{B}

be a conservative additive functor preserving kernels and cokernels.
Then
\mathsf{A}
is also an abelian category.

(b)
Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be abelian categories endowed
with exact category structures, and let
\Theta\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{B}
be
an additive functor preserving kernels and cokernels and reflecting
admissible short exact sequences.
Assume that the exact structure on
\mathsf{B}
is the abelian exact
structure.
Then the exact structure on
\mathsf{A}
is also the abelian exact
structure.

Proof.

Both the assertions follow straightforwardly from the definitions.
∎

Proposition 4.32
.

(a)
Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts
and cones.
Assume that all kernels and cokernels exist in the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, while the additive category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is abelian.
Then the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is abelian, too.

(b)
Let
\mathbf{A}
be an exact DG-category.
Assume that all kernels and cokernels exist in the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, while the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

is abelian with the abelian exact category structure.
Then the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is also abelian with
the abelian exact category structure.

Proof.

Part (a): the additive functor
