104.00563pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 142.64058pt\raise-32.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G^{-}(\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}^{-*}(X^{\natural},\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 133.64207pt\raise-56.99002pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-65.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.29886pt\raise-65.98004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}^{-*}(X^{\natural},\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

with the isomorphisms (
30
)
and (
31
).

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
T
be the total object of a three-term complex
U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W
in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
, and let
X^{\natural}
be
an object of the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
Let
p\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(T)\simeq\Phi(U[1]\oplus V\oplus W[-1])
be
a closed morphism of degree
-n
in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
with
the components
(f,g,h)
, so
f\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(U)
,
g\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(V)
, and
h\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(W)
are closed
morphisms of degrees
-n-1
,
-n
, and
-n+1
, respectively.
Let
\tilde{p}\colon\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural})\longrightarrow T
be the closed morphism of
degree
n
in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
corresponding by adjunction
to
p
, and let
(\tilde{f},\tilde{g},\tilde{h})
be the three components
of
\tilde{p}
, as in Lemma
7.6
.
Assume that the component
h\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(W)
can be lifted
to a closed morphism
t\colon X^{\natural}\longrightarrow\Phi(V)
of degree
-n+1

in
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
.
Then the morphism
\tilde{h}\in\Hom^{n-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural}),W)
can be
lifted to some morphism
\tilde{t}\in\Hom^{n-1}_{\mathbf{A}}(\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural}),V)
.

Proof.

This is our version of
[
18
, Lemma 1.5.F]
.
The assumptions of the lemma only require
U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W
to be
a complex in a DG-category, but in all the applications of this lemma
below
0\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 0
will be a short exact
sequence; in particular, the morphism
U\longrightarrow V
will be the kernel
of the morphism
V\longrightarrow W
in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
In this context, by Lemma
7.6
(b),
the assertion of
Lemma
7.8
implies
that the morphism
\tilde{p}\colon\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural})\longrightarrow T
is homotopic to
zero in the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
.
This is trivial, however:
any
closed morphism from

\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural})
is homotopic to zero in
\mathbf{A}
, as the object

X=\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural})\in\mathbf{A}
is contractible by definition.
The preservation of liftability by adjunction claimed in the lemma
is stronger than this trivial observation, and we will need its full
strength.

Denote by
B^{*}
,
C^{*}
, and
D^{*}
the graded abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}^{-*}(X^{\natural},\Phi(U))
,

\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}^{-*}(X^{\natural},\Phi(V))
, and

\Hom_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}^{-*}(X^{\natural},\Phi(W))
, respectively.
Then we have a three-term complex of graded abelian groups

B^{*}\longrightarrow C^{*}\longrightarrow D^{*}
.
Applying the functor
G^{-}
produces a bicomplex with three rows

G^{-}(B^{*})\longrightarrow G^{-}(C^{*})\longrightarrow G^{-}(D^{*})
.
Lemma
7.7
implies a natural
isomorphism of complexes of abelian groups

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural}),T)\simeq\Tot(G^{-}(B^{*})\to G^{-}(C^{*})\to G^{-}(D^{*})).

Using this isomorphism, the cocycle

\tilde{p}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Psi^{+}(X^{\natural}),T)
can be viewed as
an element of the abelian group
G^{-}(B^{*})^{n+1}\oplus G^{-}(C^{*})^{n}\oplus G^{-}(D^{*})^{n-1}
.

The elements
\tilde{f}\in G^{-}(B^{*})^{n+1}
,
\tilde{g}\in G^{-}(C^{*})^{n}
, and
\tilde{h}\in G^{-}(D^{*})^{n-1}
are the respective
projections of the direct sum element
\tilde{p}
to
the direct summands.
Furthermore, the elements
f\in B^{n+1}
,
g\in C^{n}
, and

h\in D^{n-1}
are the respective projections of
\tilde{f}
,

\tilde{g}
, and
\tilde{h}
onto the first direct summands in

G^{-}(B^{*})^{n+1}=B^{n+1}\oplus B^{n+2}
,
G^{-}(C^{*})^{n}=C^{n}\oplus C^{n+1}
, and
G^{-}(D^{*})^{n-1}=D^{n-1}\oplus D^{n}
.
This is essentially explained in the discussion of the commutativity
of the rhombus diagram (
32
) in the paragraph
preceding the lemma.

\textstyle{B^{n-1}\oplus B^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{n-1}\oplus C^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{n-1}\oplus D^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{n}\oplus B^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{n}\oplus C^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{n}\oplus D^{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{n+1}\oplus B^{n+2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{n+1}\oplus C^{n+2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{n+1}\oplus D^{n+2}}

Now the cocycle equation for the element
\tilde{p}
in the total
complex implies that the
D^{n}
-component of
\tilde{h}
can be lifted
to the element
g\in C^{n}
.
Thus the element
\tilde{h}\in D^{n-1}\oplus D^{n}
is liftable to

C^{n-1}\oplus C^{n}
whenever the element
h\in D^{n-1}
is liftable
to
C^{n-1}
.
∎

7.4.
Full-and-faithfulness theorem

The idea of the following theorem, which is the main result of
Section
7
, goes back
to
[
18
, Proposition 1.5(a–c) and Remark 1.5]

and
[
38
, Proposition A.2.1]

(see also
[
48
, Theorem 5.5]
).

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair, and let
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})
,
(\mathbf{G},\mathsf{M})\subset(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be exact DG-subpairs.

(a)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is self-resolving
in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Furthermore, if the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact products
and the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
is closed under
products,
then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

(b)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{M}
is
self-coresolving in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Furthermore, if the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact coproducts
and the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{G}\subset\mathbf{E}
is closed under
coproducts,
then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

The proof of the theorem is based on the following proposition.
Given an exact DG-category
\mathbf{A}
, we denote by
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
the class of all totalizations of short exact sequences
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
So
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{A})
is the thick subcategory generated by the full
subcategory
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
in the triangulated category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
We use the terminology “approachable objects” defined in
Section
7.2
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})
and
(\mathbf{G},\mathsf{M})
be an exact DG-subpairs in
an exact DG-pair
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
.

(a)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is self-resolving
in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Then all the objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
in the triangulated category

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

(b)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{M}
is
self-coresolving in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Then all the objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are coapproachable to

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{G})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{G})
in the triangulated category

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

We will prove part (a) (part (b) is dual).
Let
0\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and
T
be its total object; so
T\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Let
F\longrightarrow T
be a closed morphism of degree
0
in
\mathbf{E}
from
an object
F\in\mathbf{F}
to the object
T
.
We have to show that there exists an object
G\in\mathbf{F}
and a morphism

G\longrightarrow F
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
with a cone belonging to
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})

such that the composition
G\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow T
vanishes in

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Following the discussion in the beginning of
Section
7.3
, the morphism
F\longrightarrow T
is
represented by a triple of (not necessarily closed) morphisms

f\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(F,U)
,
g\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(F,V)
, and

h\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{-1}(F,W)
in the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
.

Viewing
h
as a morphism
F\longrightarrow W[-1]
in the additive category

\mathbf{E}^{0}
and applying the functor
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{E}}
from
Lemma
3.9
, we produce
a morphism
\Phi(F)\longrightarrow\Phi(W[-1])
in the exact category
\mathsf{K}

with the object
\Phi(F)\in\mathsf{L}
.
Applying the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
to the admissible epimorphism

V[-1]\longrightarrow W[-1]
in the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, we obtain
an admissible epimorphism
\Phi(V[-1])\longrightarrow\Phi(W[-1])
in
\mathsf{K}
.
Let
K
be the related pullback object; so we have the commutative
square part of a diagram in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(V[-1])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\Phi}(h)}
\textstyle{\Phi(W[-1])}

with an admissible epimorphism
K\longrightarrow\Phi(F)
.
By assumption, there exists an object
L\in\mathsf{L}
together with
an admissible epimorphism
L\longrightarrow\Phi(F)
in
\mathsf{L}
and
a morphism
L\longrightarrow K
in
\mathsf{K}
such that the triangle diagram

L\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\Phi(F)
is commutative.

By Lemma
5.7
(b), the morphism

\Psi^{+}(L)\longrightarrow F
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
corresponding by adjunction
to the admissible epimorphism
L\longrightarrow\Phi(F)
in
\mathsf{L}
is
also an admissible epimorphism.
Let
R\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
denote the kernel of
\Psi^{+}(L)\longrightarrow F
;
so we have a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}(L)\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.
Denote by
G
the cone of the closed morphism
R\longrightarrow\Psi^{+}(L)

in the DG-category
\mathbf{F}
.
Then we have a natural closed morphism
G\longrightarrow F
in
\mathbf{F}

whose cone
\Tot(R\to\Psi^{+}(L)\to F)
belongs to
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.

It remains to show that the composition
G\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow T

is homotopic to zero in the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
.
For this purpose, we apply Lemma
7.6
,
which tells that it suffices to show that the component

\tilde{l}^{\prime}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(G,W[-1])
of our morphism
G\longrightarrow T

can be lifted to an element of
\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(G,V[-1])
.
In the category
\mathbf{E}^{0}
, the object
G
is naturally isomorphic
to the direct sum
R[1]\oplus\Psi^{+}(L)
, and the morphism
\tilde{l}^{\prime}

(and in fact, the whole morphism
G\longrightarrow T
, and indeed the morphism

G\longrightarrow F
) vanishes on the component
R[1]
.
Thus it suffices to show that the related element
\tilde{l}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(\Psi^{+}(L),W[-1])
can be lifted to an element of

\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(\Psi^{+}(L),V[-1])
.

The element
\tilde{l}
is the
W
-component of the composition

\Psi^{+}(L)\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow T
, which is a closed morphism in
\mathbf{E}
.
According to Lemma
7.8
,
we only need to check that the
\Phi(W[-1])
-component
l
of
the composition
L\longrightarrow\Phi\Psi^{+}(L)\longrightarrow\Phi(F)\longrightarrow\Phi(T)

can be lifted to a morphism
L\longrightarrow\Phi(V[-1])
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
But we are given such a lifting by construction; see the commutative
diagram above.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.9
.

Let us prove part (a) (part (b) is dual).
By Lemma
7.2
, in order to
prove that the triangulated functor
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

is fully faithful, it suffices to show that all the objects of the full
subcategory
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Indeed, by Proposition
7.10
(a),
all the objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})

via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, hence also via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
.
By Lemmas
7.3

and
7.5
, the class of all objects
approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
is a thick
subcategory in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
It follows that thick subcategory contains
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and
we are done.

Similarly, in order to prove that the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
is fully faithful, it suffices to
show that all the objects of the full subcategory
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
are
approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
By Lemmas
7.4
(a)
and
7.5
, the class of all objects
approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})
is a full
triangulated subcategory closed under infinite products in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
It follows that this triangulated subcategory contains
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
.
∎

7.5.
Triangulated equivalence for a resolving subcategory

The idea of the following result goes back to
[
18
, Remark 1.5]
,

[
37
, Theorem 4.2.1]
,
[
38
, Proposition A.3.1(b)]
,
and
[
41
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Theorem 7.11
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair, and let
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})
,
(\mathbf{G},\mathsf{M})\subset(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be exact DG-subpairs.

(a)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is resolving
in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Assume further that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has twists and
exact products, and the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}

is closed under twists and products.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is an equivalence of triangulated categories.

(b)
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{M}
is
coresolving in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Assume further that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has twists and
exact coproducts, and the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{G}\subset\mathbf{E}

is closed under twists and coproducts.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is an equivalence of triangulated categories.

Proof.

Let us prove part (a) (part (b) is dual).
By Theorem
7.9
(a),
the triangulated functor
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
is fully
faithful; so we only need to show that it is essentially surjective.
For this purpose, we will construct for any object
E\in\mathbf{E}

an object
F\in\mathbf{F}
together with a closed morphism
F\longrightarrow E

of degree
0
in
\mathbf{E}
whose cone belongs to
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
.

By Lemma
6.5
(a),
there exists a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with an object
F_{0}\in\mathbf{F}
.
Applying the same lemma to the object
E_{1}\in\mathbf{E}
, we obtain
a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with an object
F_{1}\in\mathbf{F}
.
Proceeding in this way, we construct an exact complex

\dotsb\longrightarrow F_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with objects
F_{i}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
.

Denote by
F=\Tot^{\sqcap}(F_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
the product totalization of
the complex
\dotsb\longrightarrow F_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow 0
in
the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
.
Then the object
F\in\mathbf{E}
is a twist of the object

\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}F_{i}[i]
, hence
F\in\mathbf{F}
by assumptions.
The cone of the natural morphism
F\longrightarrow E
in
\mathbf{E}
is the product
totalization of the bounded above exact complex

\dotsb\longrightarrow F_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, so it is contraacyclic by
Lemma
5.11
(b).
∎

7.6.
Examples

Let us formulate here the particular cases of
Theorems
7.9

and
7.11
arising in
the context of Examples
4.39
–
4.42

and
6.1
.

We refer to Section
6.4
for
the definitions of the absolute derived, coderived, and contraderived
categories of an exact category
\mathsf{E}
.
The following result can be found in
[
38
, Propositions A.2.1
and A.3.1(b)]
.

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category and
\mathsf{F}
be
a self-resolving subcategory in
\mathsf{E}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathsf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathsf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact categories
\mathsf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{E}

is fully faithful.

Furthermore, assume that the exact category
\mathsf{E}
has exact products
and the full subcategory
\mathsf{F}\subset\mathsf{E}
is closed under products.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathsf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathsf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact categories
\mathsf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{E}

is fully faithful.
Assuming additionally that
\mathsf{F}
is a resolving subcategory in

\mathsf{E}
, the latter functor is a triangulated equivalence.

Proof.

Put
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{E})
,
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{F})
,
\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{E})
,

\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{F})
, as in Examples
4.39

and
6.1
(4), and apply
Theorems
7.9
(a)
(for the first two assertions of the corollary)
and
7.11
(a)
(for the third one).
∎

Part (a) of the following corollary is a generalization of
an assertion from
[
18
, Remark 1.5]
.

Corollary 7.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring.
Let
\mathsf{K}\subset R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
be a full subcategory as in
the first paragraph of
Corollary
6.21
, and let

\mathbf{E}\subset\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
the related full DG-subcategory
endowed with the inherited exact DG-category structure.

(a)
Let
\mathsf{L}
be a self-resolving subcategory in
\mathsf{K}
.
Denote by
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
the full DG-subcategory consisting
of all the CDG-modules whose underlying graded
R^{*}
-modules
belong to
\mathsf{L}
, and endow
\mathbf{F}
with the inherited exact
DG-category structure.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Furthermore, assume that the full subcategories
\mathsf{L}
and
\mathsf{K}

are preserved by the infinite products in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.
Assuming additionally that
\mathsf{L}
is a resolving subcategory in

\mathsf{K}
, the latter functor is a triangulated equivalence.

(b)
Let
\mathsf{M}
be a self-coresolving subcategory in
\mathsf{K}
.
Denote by
\mathbf{G}\subset\mathbf{E}
the full DG-subcategory consisting
of all the CDG-modules whose underlying graded
R^{*}
-modules
belong to
\mathsf{M}
, and endow
\mathbf{G}
with the inherited exact
DG-category structure.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Furthermore, assume that the full subcategories
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathsf{K}

are preserved by the infinite direct sums in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.
Assuming additionally that
\mathsf{M}
is a coresolving subcategory in

\mathsf{K}
, the latter functor is a triangulated equivalence.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Corollary
6.21
.
∎

Part (a) of the next corollary is a generalization
of
[
18
, Proposition 1.5(a–c)]
.

Corollary 7.14
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B^{*},d,h)
be a quasi-coherent
CDG-quasi-algebra over
X
.
Let
\mathsf{K}\subset B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
be a full subcategory as in
the first paragraph of
Corollary
6.22
,
and let
\mathbf{E}\subset\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
the related full DG-subcategory
endowed with the inherited exact DG-category structure.

(a)
Let
\mathsf{L}
be a self-resolving subcategory in
\mathsf{K}
.
Denote by
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{E}
the full DG-subcategory consisting
of all the quasi-coherent CDG-modules whose underlying quasi-coherent
graded
B^{*}
-modules belong to
\mathsf{L}
, and endow
\mathbf{F}
with
the inherited exact DG-category structure.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

(b)
Let
\mathsf{M}
be a self-coresolving subcategory in
\mathsf{K}
.
Denote by
\mathbf{G}\subset\mathbf{E}
the full DG-subcategory consisting
of all the quasi-coherent CDG-modules whose underlying quasi-coherent
graded
B^{*}
-modules belong to
\mathsf{M}
, and endow
\mathbf{G}
with
the inherited exact DG-category structure.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Furthermore, assume that the full subcategories
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathsf{K}

are preserved by the infinite direct sums in
B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{G})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{G}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.
Assuming additionally that
\mathsf{M}
is a coresolving subcategory in

\mathsf{K}
, the latter functor is a triangulated equivalence.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
Corollary
6.22
.
∎

Let us point out once again that assertions about the contraderived
categories are absent in
Corollary
7.14
(a),
because the functors of infinite product of quasi-coherent sheaves
are not exact.
If one wants to consider contraderived categories of module objects
over a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra over a nonaffine scheme,
then one should work with contraherent cosheaves
[
38
]
instead
of quasi-coherent sheaves.

The first assertion of the following corollary is a generalization
of
[
18
, Corollary 2.3(h–k)]
.

Corollary 7.15
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category and
\Lambda\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}
be
an autoequivalence preserving and reflecting short exact sequences.
Assume that either
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, or
\Lambda
is involutive
and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Let
w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{E}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
be a potential (as in
Section
2.5
).
Let
\mathsf{H}
be a self-resolving subcategory in
\mathsf{E}
preserved by
the autoequivalences
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{-1}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{H},\Lambda,w))\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w))

induced by the inclusion of exact categories
\mathsf{H}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{E}

is fully faithful.

Furthermore, if the exact category
\mathsf{E}
has exact products and
the full subcategory
\mathsf{H}\subset\mathsf{E}
is closed under products,
then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{H},\Lambda,w))\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w))

induced by the inclusion of exact categories
\mathsf{H}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{E}

is fully faithful.
Assuming additionally that
\mathsf{H}
is a resolving subcategory in

\mathsf{E}
, the latter functor is a triangulated equivalence.

Proof.

Put
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w)
,
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{H},\Lambda,w)
,

\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda)
,
\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{P}(\mathsf{H},\Lambda)
,
as in Examples
4.42

and
6.1
(5), and apply
Theorems
7.9
(a)
(for the first two assertions of the corollary)
and
7.11
(a)
(for the third one).
∎

8.
Finite Homological Dimension Theorem

8.1.
Strongly generated thick subcategories

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category and
\mathsf{M}
,
\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{T}

be two classes of objects.
The following concept and notation goes back to
[
8
, 1.3.9]
.

One says that an object
X\in\mathsf{T}
is an
extension
of two
objects
M
and
N\in\mathsf{T}
, if there exists a distinguished
triangle
M\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow M[1]
in
\mathsf{T}
.
The class of all objects
X\in\mathsf{T}
for which there exists
a distinguished triangle
M\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow M[1]
with

M\in\mathsf{M}
and
N\in\mathsf{N}
is denoted by
\mathsf{M}*\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{T}
.
According to
[
8
, Lemme 1.3.10]
, the operation
*
on
the subclasses of objects in
\mathsf{T}
is associative.

In particular, the notation
\mathsf{M}^{*n}
for the class of objects

\mathsf{M}*\mathsf{M}*\dotsb*\mathsf{M}
(
n
factors) is unambiguous for any given
integer
n\geq 1
.
Assuming for simplicity that the class of objects
\mathsf{M}\subset\mathsf{T}

is closed under the shifts
[1]
,
[-1]
and finite direct sums, and
following
[
13
, Section 2.2]
, we will denote by

\langle\mathsf{M}\rangle_{n}\subset\mathsf{T}
the class of all direct summands
of objects from
\mathsf{M}^{*n}
.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a thick subcategory in a triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
,
and let
\mathsf{M}\subset\mathsf{X}
be a class of objects.
Following the terminology in
[
13
, Section 2.2]
, we will say
that the class
\mathsf{M}

strongly generates
the thick subcategory

\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{T}
if there exists a finite integer
n\geq 1
such that

\mathsf{X}=\langle\mathsf{M}\rangle_{n}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

(a)
Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with infinite
coproducts and
\mathsf{M}
,
\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{T}
be two classes of objects
closed under coproducts in
\mathsf{T}
.
Then the classes of objects
\mathsf{M}*\mathsf{N}
and
\langle\mathsf{M}\rangle_{n}

are also closed under coproducts in
\mathsf{T}
(for all
n\geq 1
).

(b)
Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with infinite
products and
\mathsf{M}
,
\mathsf{N}\subset\mathsf{T}
be two classes of objects
closed under products in
\mathsf{T}
.
Then the classes of objects
\mathsf{M}*\mathsf{N}
and
\langle\mathsf{M}\rangle_{n}

are also closed under products in
\mathsf{T}
(for all
n\geq 1
).

Proof.

It suffices to prove part (b) (part (a) is dual).
The assertion about the class
\mathsf{M}*\penalty\mathsf{N}
holds due to
the fact that the products of distinguished trianges are
distinguished triangles
[
31
, Proposition 1.2.1]
.
The assertion about the class
\langle\mathsf{M}\rangle_{n}
easily
follows, because products of direct summands are direct summands
of products.
∎

8.2.
Absolutely acyclic objects as totalizations of
finite exact complexes

The following proposition provides a converse assertion to
Lemma
5.9
.
We will use the notation
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\subset{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
from Sections
5.1

and
7.4
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category.
Then for any absolutely acyclic object
X\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

there exists a finite exact complex
Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in the exact
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that
X
is a direct summand of
the object
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
More precisely, if
n\geq 1
is an integer such that

X\in\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, then
there exists an exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y_{n+1}\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that

X
is a direct summand of
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

The proof of the proposition is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category and
0\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence in the exact category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, and
T=\Tot(U\to V\to W)\in\mathbf{E}
be its totalization.
Let
S\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow S[1]
be a distinguished triangle
in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, let
P\in\mathbf{E}
be
an object, and let
P\longrightarrow X
be a morphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Then there exists a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and a morphism
R[1]\longrightarrow S
in

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the following composition
C\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X

is equal to the composition
C\longrightarrow R[1]\longrightarrow S\longrightarrow X
in
the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, i. e., the pentagon on
the right-hand side of the diagram is commutative up to homotopy:

\textstyle{S[1]}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Here
C
is the cone of the closed morphism
R\longrightarrow Y
in
\mathbf{E}
,
and
C\longrightarrow P
and
C\longrightarrow R[1]
are the natural closed morphisms
of degree
0
.
So the triangle in the lower middle part of the diagram is
commutative in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, while the bottom line is the standard
distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

This is a generalization of
[
18
, Lemma 1.6.G]
.
Let us first briefly discuss why the complicated assertion of
the lemma has any chance to be true at all.
Specifically, let us explain why the desired commutative diagram
exists
in the absolute derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
.
Indeed, in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, the morphisms
S\longrightarrow X
and

C\longrightarrow P
are isomorphisms.
Choosing the object
Y\in\mathbf{E}
to be contractible, one makes
the morphism
C\longrightarrow R[1]
an isomorphism (in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

and even in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
) as well.
Now it is clear that the composition
C\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
factorizes
through the isomorphisms
C\longrightarrow R[1]
and
S\longrightarrow X
in
the absolute derived category.

To prove the lemma (i. e., construct the desired commutative pentagon
diagram in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
), we present a simplified
version of the argument from
[
18
]
.
The argument starts with choosing a closed morphism
P\longrightarrow X
of
degree
0
representing the given homotopy class.

Consider the composition
P\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T
and represent it
by a triple
(f,g,h)
with
f\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{1}(P,U)
,
g\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(P,V)
, and
h\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{-1}(P,W)
, as in
Section
7.3
.
Arguing similarly to the proof of
Proposition
7.10
, we view
h
as
a morphism
P\longrightarrow W[-1]
in the additive category
\mathbf{E}^{0}
and,
applying the functor
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{E}}
, produce a morphism

\Phi(P)\longrightarrow\Phi(W[-1])
in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Applying the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}
to the admissible epimorphism

V[-1]\longrightarrow W[-1]
in the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, we obtain
an admissible epimorphism
\Phi(V[-1])\longrightarrow\Phi(W[-1])
in
the exact category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
Let
K
be the related pullback object in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
;
so we have a commutative square diagram as in the proof of
Proposition
7.10
with
an admissible epimorphism
K\longrightarrow\Phi(P)
and a morphism

K\longrightarrow\Phi(V[-1])
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

Put
Y=\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}(K)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
By Lemma
5.7
(b), the morphism

Y=\Psi^{+}(K)\longrightarrow P
corresponding by adjunction to the admissible
epimorphism
K\longrightarrow\Phi(P)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is an admissible
epimorphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Let
R
be kernel of the admissible epimorphism
Y\longrightarrow P
; so
we have a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Put
C=\cone(R\rightarrowtail Y)\in\mathbf{E}
, and let
C\longrightarrow P
and

C\longrightarrow R[1]
be the natural closed morphisms of degree
0
.
We claim that the composition of closed morphisms

C\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T
is homotopic to zero in
\mathbf{E}
.

Indeed, one can continue to follow the proof of
Proposition
7.10
.
By Lemma
7.6
, it suffices to show that
the component
\tilde{k}^{\prime}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{E}}(C,W[-1])
of our morphism

C\longrightarrow T
can be lifted to an element of
\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(C,V[-1])
.
In the category
\mathbf{E}^{0}
, the object
C
is naturally isomorphic to
the direct sum
R[1]\oplus Y
, and the morphism
\tilde{k}^{\prime}
vanishes
on the component
R[1]
.
Hence it suffices to show that the related element
\tilde{k}\in\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(Y,W[-1])
can be lifted to an element of

\Hom_{\mathbf{E}}^{0}(Y,V[-1])
.

The element
\tilde{k}
is the
W
-component of the composition

Y=\Psi^{+}(K)\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T
, which is a closed morphism
in
\mathbf{E}
.
According to Lemma
7.8
,
we only need to check that the component
K\longrightarrow\Phi(W[-1])
of
the composition
K\longrightarrow\Phi\Psi^{+}(K)\longrightarrow\Phi(P)\longrightarrow\Phi(T)

can be lifted to a morphism
K\longrightarrow\Phi(V[-1])
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
But we are given such a lifting by the construction of
the object
K\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.

We have shown that the composition
C\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T

vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, and it follows that the composition

C\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
factorizes through the morphism
S\longrightarrow X

in the distringuished triangle
S\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow S[1]
.
We have constructed the diagram of undotted arrows

\textstyle{S[1]}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with the quadrangle in the right-hand side commutative up to homotopy.

It remains to deduce the dotted factorization through
R[1]

in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Here we simply notice that the object
Y=\Psi^{+}(K)
is contractible
in
\mathbf{E}
by the definition of the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{E}}
, hence

C\longrightarrow R[1]
is an isomorphism in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
(See
[
18
, proof of Lemma 1.6.G]
for a fancier argument not using
the observation that the functor
\Psi^{+}
produces contractible
objects.)
∎

Proof of Proposition
8.2
.

We follow
[
18
, first half of the proof of Theorem 1.6]
.
Let us prove the following more general claim: for any objects

X\in\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
and
P\in\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, and any
morphism
f\colon P\longrightarrow X
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, there exists an exact complex

0\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the composition
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
of the natural closed morphism
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow P
with
the morphism
f
vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Indeed, it suffices to consider the case when
X\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})^{*n}
,
as the passage to a direct summand is trivial.
So we have a sequence of objects
T_{1}
, …,
T_{n}\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

and distinguished triangles
X_{i-1}\longrightarrow X_{i}\longrightarrow T_{i}\longrightarrow X_{i-1}[1]
,
i=1
, …,
n
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that
X_{0}=0

and
X_{n}=X
.

Applying Lemma
8.3

to the distinguished triangle
X_{n-1}\longrightarrow X_{n}\longrightarrow T_{n}\longrightarrow X_{n-1}[1]
and the morphism
P\longrightarrow X_{n}
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, we obtain
a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and a morphism
R_{1}[1]\longrightarrow X_{n-1}
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

such that the composition
\cone(R_{1}\to Y_{0})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X_{n}

is equal to the composition
\cone(R_{1}\to Y_{0})\longrightarrow R_{1}[1]\longrightarrow X_{n-1}\longrightarrow X_{n}
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Applying Lemma
8.3

again to the morphism
R_{1}[1]\longrightarrow X_{n-1}
and the distinguished
triangle
X_{n-2}\longrightarrow X_{n-1}\longrightarrow T_{n-1}\longrightarrow X_{n-2}[1]
,
we construct a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R_{2}\longrightarrow Y_{1}\longrightarrow R_{1}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and a morphism
R_{2}[2]\longrightarrow X_{n-2}
in

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, etc.
Finally we produce a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow R_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow R_{n-1}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
and a morphism
R_{n}[n]\longrightarrow X_{0}=0
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Put
Y_{n}=R_{n}
.

Now we have a commutative diagram in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
as depicted on
Figure 1,

\textstyle{\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{1}\to Y_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{X=X_{n}}}
\textstyle{\cone(R_{1}\to Y_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{2}\to Y_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{1}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cone(R_{2}\to Y_{1})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{3}\to Y_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{2}[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{n-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{R_{n-1}[n-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cone(R_{n}\to Y_{n-1})[n-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{{X_{0}=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Figure 1.
The composition of morphisms vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

showing that the composition of morphisms

\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
vanishes in the homotopy
category, since it factorizes through a zero object.
Indeed, the pentagons on the diagram are commutative in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

by Lemma
8.3
, while
the triangles and the quadrangles are obviously commutative in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
already.

Having proved the more general claim, let us deduce the assertion of
proposition as it is stated.
Given an object
X\in\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
, we put
P=X
and
consider the identity morphism
f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
.
As we have shown, there exists an exact complex

0\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the natural closed morphism

\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\longrightarrow X
is homotopic to zero in
\mathbf{E}
.
It remains to consider the distinguished triangle

X[-1]\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0}\to X)\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX

in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
, and observe that the object

X[-1]
becomes a direct summand of the object
\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0}\to X)
in the triangulated category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
whenever
the morphism
\Tot(Y_{n}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\longrightarrow X
vanishes
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
∎

8.3.
Preliminaries on Yoneda extensions

Let us recall the basics of the Yoneda Ext construction in exact
categories (see, e. g.,
[
36
, Sections A.7–A.8]
).

Given two objects
X
and
Y
in an exact category
\mathsf{K}
, by
an
n
-extension of
X
by
Y
one means an exact complex

0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow A_{n}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow A_{1}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0

in
\mathsf{K}
.
This means that the complex
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
can be obtained by splicing (admissible) short exact
sequences in
\mathsf{K}
, which we will denote by
0\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{i-1}\longrightarrow 0
,
i=1
, …,
n
, with
Z^{A}_{n}=Y
and

Z^{A}_{0}=X
.

An
elementary equivalence
acting from an
n
-extension

A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to an
n
-extension
B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
is a morphism of
complexes
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
inducing the identity maps on
the homology objects
X
and
Y
.
The equivalence relation on the
n
-extensions of
X
by
Y
is,
by the definition, generated by the elementary equivalences.
When
n=1
, all elementary equivalences of
1
-extensions are
isomorphisms, hence all equivalent
1
-extensions are isomorphic;
but for
n>1
this is no longer the case.

The equivalence classes of
n
-extensions of
X
and
Y
form
the abelian group
\Ext_{\mathsf{K}}^{n}(X,Y)
.
In fact, it is straightforward to see from the definition of
the derived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{b}}(\mathsf{K})
(as presented in
[
29
]
)
that the set of equivalence classes of
n
-extensions of
X
by
Y

is naturally bijective to the group
\Ext_{\mathsf{K}}^{n}(X,Y)=\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{b}}(\mathsf{K})}(X,Y[n])
.

The class of all morphisms with exact cones in the homotopy category
of complexes in
\mathsf{K}
is localizing, i. e., it satisfies the left and
right Ore conditions.
This implies a similar property of the Yoneda
n
-extensions.
Namely, two
n
-extensions
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
are
equivalent if and only if there exists an
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X

by
Y
together with a fraction (“roof”) of elementary equivalences

A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longleftarrow C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
, and if and only if there exists
an
n
-extension
D_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
together with a fraction of
elementary equivalences
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow D_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longleftarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

The
zero
n
-extension
(corresponding to the zero element

0\in\Ext_{\mathsf{K}}^{n}(X,Y)
) is represented by the complex

0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
(for
n\geq 2
) or
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Y\oplus X\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
(for
n=1
).
Now, given an
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
, one easily
observes that an elementary equivalence acting from
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to
the zero
n
-extension exists if and only if the admissible
monomorphism
Y\longrightarrow C_{n}
is split.
Dually, an elementary equivalence acting to an
n
-extension

D_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
from the zero
n
-extension exists if and only if
the admissible epimorphism
D_{1}\longrightarrow X
is split.

One says that an
n
-extension
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
is

split
(or
trivial
) if it is equivalent to the zero

n
-extension.
We have proved the following lemma.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be an exact category,
X
,
Y\in\mathsf{K}
be two objects,
and
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be an
n
-extension of
X
by
Y
.
Then the
n
-extension
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is split if and only if there exists
an elementary equivalence of
n
-extensions
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

of
X
by
Y
such that the admissible monomorphism
Y\longrightarrow C_{n}
is
split, or equivalently, if and only if there exists an elementary
equivalence of
n
-extensions
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow D_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y

such that the admissible epimorphism
D_{1}\longrightarrow X
is split. ∎

We will say that an elementary equivalence
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of

n
-extensions of
X
by
Y
in
\mathsf{K}
is an
elementary
epiequivalence
if the induced morphisms of the objects of cocycles

Z^{C}_{i}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{i}
are admissible epimorphisms for all

1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Clearly, it follows that the morphisms
C_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
are also
admissible epimorphisms in this case for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
Conversely, if the morphisms
C_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
are admissible
epimorphisms for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
and the exact category
\mathsf{K}

is weakly idempotent-complete, then an elementary equivalence

C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is an elementary epiequivalence.

The next lemma can be used in conjunction with the previous one.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be an exact category,
X
,
Y\in\mathsf{K}
be two objects,
and
f\colon B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be an elementary equivalence of

n
-extensions of
X
by
Y
.
Then there exists an
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
together
with elementary epiequivalences of
n
-extensions
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

and
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

Notice that there is
no
claim of commutativity of
the triangle diagram of elementary equivalences of
n
-extensions

C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
Lemma
8.5
.
This triangle is essentially
never
commutative, and we do not
need it to be.
Rather, it will be important for us that there exists an elementary
equivalence of
n
-extensions acting from
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to the zero

n
-extension of
X
by
Y
whenever there is such an elementary
equivalence acting from
B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to the zero
n
-extension.

Proof.

This is
[
36
, first paragraph of the proof of Lemma 4.4]
.
The case
n=1
is trivial, so we assume
n>1
.
Put
C_{i}=B_{i}\oplus A_{i}\oplus A_{i+1}
for
1<i<n
,
C_{n}=B_{n}\oplus A_{n}
, and
C_{1}=B_{1}\oplus A_{2}
.
In the exact sequence
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow C_{n}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow C_{1}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
, the components of the differential
Y\longrightarrow C_{n}

are the differential
Y\longrightarrow B_{n}
and the zero morphism

Y\longrightarrow A_{n}
.
The only nonzero components of the differential
C_{i}\longrightarrow C_{i-1}

are the differential
B_{i}\longrightarrow B_{i-1}
and the identity map

A_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
.
The components of the differential
C_{1}\longrightarrow X
are the differential

B_{1}\longrightarrow X
and the zero morphism
A_{2}\longrightarrow X
.
So the exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
is
constructed as the direct sum of the exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
and the contractible two-term complexes

0\longrightarrow A_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}\longrightarrow 0
, where
1<i\leq n
.

The elementary equivalence
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow B_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is the direct summand
projection; so the only nonzero component of the morphism
C_{i}\longrightarrow B_{i}
is the identity morphism
B_{i}\longrightarrow B_{i}
.
The elementary equivalence
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is formed by
the following morphisms
C_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
.
The components of the morphism
C_{n}\longrightarrow A_{n}
are the morphism

f_{n}\colon B_{n}\longrightarrow A_{n}
and the identity morphism
A_{n}\longrightarrow A_{n}
.
For
1<i<n
, the components of the morphism
C_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
are
the morphism
f_{i}\colon B_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
, the identity map
A_{i}\longrightarrow A_{i}
,
and the differential
A_{i+1}\longrightarrow A_{i}
.
For
i=1
, the components of the morphism
C_{1}\longrightarrow A_{1}
are
the morphism
f_{1}\colon B_{1}\longrightarrow A_{1}
and the differential

A_{2}\longrightarrow A_{1}
.

To show that the elementary equivalence
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
is
an elementary epiequivalence, one can compute the objects of cocycles

Z_{i}^{C}\in\mathsf{K}
.
In fact, one has
Z_{i}^{C}=Z_{i}^{B}\oplus A_{i+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
.
The components of the induced morphism
Z_{i}^{C}\longrightarrow Z_{i}^{A}

are the induced morphism
Z_{i}^{B}\longrightarrow Z_{i}^{A}
and the admissible
epimorphism
A_{i+1}\longrightarrow Z_{i}^{A}
; so the morphism
Z_{i}^{C}\longrightarrow Z_{i}^{A}
is an admissible epimorphism by the dual version
of
[
14
, first assertion of Exercise 3.11(i)]
.
∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
(0\to W^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}\to Z^{\prime}\to 0)\longrightarrow(0\to W\to A\to Z\to 0)
be
a morphism of short exact sequences in an exact category
\mathsf{K}
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(a)
If the morphism
W^{\prime}\longrightarrow W
is an admissible epimorphism,
then the induced morphism to the pullback in the rightmost square

A^{\prime}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muA\sqcap_{Z}Z^{\prime}

is an admissible epimorphism.

(b)
If the morphism
Z^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
is an isomorphism, then
the leftmost square is (both a pushout and) a pullback,

W^{\prime}=W\sqcap_{A}A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Part (a): put
A^{\prime\prime}=A\sqcap_{Z}Z^{\prime}
, and consider the pullback of
the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow 0
by
the morphism
Z^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
.
Then we get a commutative triangle diagram of morphisms of short exact
sequences with identity morphisms on some of the leftmost and rightmost
terms:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Since both the short sequences in the upper half of the diagram are
exact, it follows that the upper leftmost square is a pushout (so

A^{\prime}\sqcup_{W^{\prime}}W=A^{\prime\prime}=A\sqcap_{Z}Z^{\prime}
).
Finally, since
W^{\prime}\longrightarrow W
is an admissible epimorphism by
assumption, we can conclude that
A^{\prime}\longrightarrow A^{\prime\prime}
is also an admissible
epimorphism with the same kernel (by the dual assertion
to
[
14
, Proposition 2.15]
).

Part (b) is
[
14
, Proposition 2.12(iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii)]
.
∎

8.4.
Graded split Yoneda Ext classes

Now we pass to the following setting.
Let
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{K}
be two exact categories and
\Phi\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}

be an exact functor.
The aim of this Section
8.4
is to
prove the following proposition, which will be useful in
Section
8.5
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Assume that an exact functor
\Phi\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}
satisfies
the following condition:

(
\spadesuit
)

For any two objects
B\in\mathsf{E}
and
K\in\mathsf{K}
, and
any admissible epimorphism
K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
in
\mathsf{K}
, there exist
an admissible epimorphism
C\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{E}
and a morphism

\Phi(C)\longrightarrow K
in
\mathsf{K}
making the triangle diagram

\Phi(C)\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
commutative in
\mathsf{K}
.

Let
X
,
Y\in\mathsf{E}
be two objects, and let
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be
an
n
-extension of
X
by
Y
in the exact category
\mathsf{E}
.
Then the
n
-extension
\Phi(A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
of the object
\Phi(X)
by
the object
\Phi(Y)
is split in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
if and
only if there exists an
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y
in
\mathsf{E}

together with an elementary equivalence of
n
-extensions
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathsf{E}
such that the functor
\Phi
takes
the admissible monomorphism
Y\longrightarrow C_{n}
in
\mathsf{E}
to a split
monomorphism in
\mathsf{K}
.

Condition (
\spadesuit
) plays an important role in the theory of
the Yoneda Ext functor in exact categories;
see
[
36
, Section 4.4]
and
[
39
, condition (i
′
) in
Section 0.1]
.
In the special case of a fully faithful functor
\Phi
, this
condition is also a part of the definition of a self-resolving
subcategory in Section
7.1
.

Proof.

An exact functor
\Phi
takes
n
-extensions in
\mathsf{E}
to

n
-extensions in
\mathsf{K}
, and it also takes elementary equivalences
of
n
-extensions in
\mathsf{E}
to elementary equivalences of

n
-extensions in
\mathsf{K}
.
This suffices to prove the “if” implication of the proposition.
The nontrivial part is the “only if”.

We follow
[
18
, second half of the proof of Theorem 1.6]
with
some details added (as the exposition in
[
18
]
is rather sketchy).
So let
A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
be an an
n
-extension of
X
by
Y
in
\mathsf{E}

such that the
n
-extension
\Phi(A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
in
\mathsf{K}
is split.
By Lemma
8.4
, there exists an elementary

n
-extension
K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
\Phi(X)
by
\Phi(Y)
in
\mathsf{K}
together with
an elementary equivalence of
n
-extensions
K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})

such that the admissible monomonorphism
\Phi(Y)\longrightarrow K_{n}
is split.
By Lemma
8.5
, we can assume that

K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
is an elementary epiequivalence, i. e.,
the induced morphisms
Z^{K}_{i}\longrightarrow\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})
are admissible
epimorphisms in
\mathsf{K}
for all
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

Applying condition (
\spadesuit
) to the admissible epimorphism

K_{1}\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{1})
in
\mathsf{K}
, we obtain an admissible epimorphism

C_{1}\longrightarrow A_{1}
in
\mathsf{E}
and a morphism
\Phi(C_{1})\longrightarrow K_{1}

in
\mathsf{K}
such that the triangle diagram

\Phi(C_{1})\longrightarrow K_{1}\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{1})
is commutative.
It follows that the induced morphism
Z^{C}_{1}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{1}
is
an admissible epimorphism, too (with the same kernel as
the morphism
C_{1}\longrightarrow A_{1}
).
We want to continue this procedure, producing objects
C_{i}\in\mathsf{E}

for all
2\leq i\leq n
forming an
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
of
X
by
Y

in
\mathsf{E}
, together with elementary equivalences of
n
-extensions

C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathsf{E}
and
\Phi(C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
in
\mathsf{K}

(forming a commutative diagram of elementary equivalences of

n
-extensions
\Phi(C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
in
\mathsf{K}
).

The next step of the construction can be formulated in the same
terms as in
[
18
]
.
In the commutative diagram with exact rows in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

the morphism to the pullback in the middle square,

is an admissible epimorphism, as one can see by viewing

Consider the commutative diagram in

\textstyle{\Phi(C_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

hence the morphism

is an admissible epimorphism.

) to the object

in
\mathsf{K}
,
we obtain an admissible epimorphism

\mathsf{E}

.

together
with commutativity of the two leftmost squares on the diagram

that always vanishes.

\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

we see that the morphism to the pullback in the rightmost square,

K_{i+1}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(A_{i+1})\sqcap_{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})}Z^{K}_{i},

is an admissible epimorphism.

Consider the commutative diagram in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})}

As above, we argue that any pullback of admissible epimorphism is
an admissible epimorphism, hence the morphism

K_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{K}_{i}}\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(A_{i+1})\sqcap_{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})}\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\,=\,(\Phi(A_{i+1})\sqcap_{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})}Z^{K}_{i})\sqcap_{Z^{K}_{i}}\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})

is an admissible epimorphism.

Applying condition (
\spadesuit
) to the object

A_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i}\in\mathsf{E}
and the admissible epimorphism

K_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{K}_{i}}\Phi(Z^{i}C_{i})\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i})
in
\mathsf{K}
, we obtain an admissible
epimorphism

(35)

C_{i+1}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muA_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i}

in
\mathsf{E}
and a morphism

(36)

\Phi(C_{i+1})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muK_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{K}_{i}}\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})

in
\mathsf{K}
such that the triangle diagram

(37)

\Phi(C_{i+1})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muK_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{K}_{i}}\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Phi(A_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i})

is commutative in
\mathsf{K}
.

The morphism
A_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i}\longrightarrow Z^{C}_{i}
is an admissible
epimorphism in
\mathsf{E}
, because the morphism
A_{i+1}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{i}
is.
Hence the composition
C_{i+1}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muA_{i+1}\sqcap_{Z^{A}_{i}}Z^{C}_{i}\longrightarrow Z^{C}_{i}
is an admissible epimorphism; denote its kernel
by
Z^{C}_{i+1}\in\mathsf{E}
.
Now the morphism (
35
) translates
into a commutative square, and passing to the kernels we obtain
a morphism of short exact sequences in
\mathsf{E}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{C}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{C}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{A}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{A}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

The morphism (
36
) translates
into a commutative square as well, and passing to the kernels we obtain
a morphism of short exact sequences in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(C_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Finally, commutativity of the triangle
diagram (
37
) translates into
commutativity of the triangle diagram of morphisms of short exact
sequences in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(C_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{i+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

We have managed to extend the commutative triangle diagram of
morphisms of exact complexes (
34
) one step
further to the left.
Proceeding in this way, we construct a diagram
like (
34
) for
i=n-1
, that is

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(C_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(C_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Now we are coming close to an end of the construction, and
a different procedure is needed for
i=n
.
The commutative diagram in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{K_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

with short exact sequences
0\longrightarrow\Phi(Y)\longrightarrow K_{n}\longrightarrow Z^{K}_{n-1}\longrightarrow 0
and
0\longrightarrow\Phi(Y)\longrightarrow\Phi(A_{n})\longrightarrow\Phi(Z^{A}_{n-1})\longrightarrow 0
implies that the square in
the right-hand side is a pullback.
The object
C_{n}\in\mathsf{E}
is constructed by taking a pullback of
the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow A_{n}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{n}\longrightarrow 0

by the morphism
Z^{C}_{n-1}\longrightarrow Z^{A}_{n-1}
, as on the diagram

\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{C}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{A}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Then functoriality of the pullbacks implies commutativity of
the diagram in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{\Phi(C_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{C}_{n-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{K}_{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(A_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(Z^{A}_{n-1})}

finishing the construction of the
n
-extension
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
and
the elementary equivalences of
n
-extensions
C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\longrightarrow A_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}

and
\Phi(C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
.

It remains to recall that the
n
-extension
K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
was chosen
at the beginning of this proof so that the admissible monomorphism

\Phi(Y)\longrightarrow K_{n}
were split.
In other words, there was an elementary equivalence acting from

K_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
to the zero
n
-extension of
\Phi(X)
by
\Phi(Y)
.
Hence there is also an elementary equivalence acting from
the
n
-extension
\Phi(C_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
to the zero
n
-extension.
This means that the admissible monomorphism
\Phi(Y)\longrightarrow\Phi(C_{n})

is also split, as desired.
∎

8.5.
Finite homological dimension theorem

The following proposition, formulated in the terminology and notation
of the paper
[
13
]
(see
Section
8.1
),
is the main result of Section
8
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair in which the exact category

\mathsf{K}
has finite homological dimension.
Then the full triangulated subcategory of absolutely acyclic
objects
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is strongly generated by
the totalizations of short exact sequences in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
More precisely, if the homological dimension of
\mathsf{K}
does not
exceed
n
, then
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
.

The next theorem, whose idea goes back to
[
18
, Theorem 1.6]
,
follows easily.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair in which the exact category

\mathsf{K}
has finite homological dimension.

(a)
Assume that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact
coproducts.
Then any coacyclic object in
\mathbf{E}
is absolutely acyclic,

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})={\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and therefore the coderived category
of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E}).

(b)
Assume that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact
products.
Then any contraacyclic object in
\mathbf{E}
is absolutely acyclic,

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})={\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and therefore the contraderived category
of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E}).

Proof.

One observes that, for any exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
with exact
coproducts, the class
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
of all totalizations of short
exact sequences in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is closed under coproducts
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Dually, for any exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
with exact products,
the class
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
of all totalizations of short exact sequences
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is closed under products in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Now, if the exact category
\mathsf{K}
has finite homological dimension
n
,
then
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
according to
Proposition
8.8
.
Hence, by Lemma
8.1
,
the thick subcategory of all absolutely acyclic objects
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})

is closed under coproducts in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
under the assumptions of
part (a), and it is closed under products in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

under the assumptions of part (b).
∎

Before proving
Proposition
8.8
,
let us collect a couple of lemmas.
The following lemma is a more precise version of
Lemma
5.9
.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be an exact DG-category, and let
