0\longrightarrow Y_{n+1}\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow 0
be
a finite exact complex with
n
middle terms in the exact category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Then the totalization
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
belongs to the class

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})^{*n}\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
5.9
.
∎

The next lemma establishes applicability of
Proposition
8.7
in our
context.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
and
\mathsf{K}
be exact categories, and let

\Phi\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}
be an additive functor.
Assume that the functor
\Phi
has an exact right adjoint functor

\Psi^{-}\colon\mathsf{K}\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}
.
Let
B\in\mathsf{E}
and
K\in\mathsf{K}
be two objects, and let
K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
be an admissible epimorphism in
\mathsf{K}
.
Then there exist an admissible epimorphism
C\longrightarrow B
in
\mathsf{E}

and a morphism
\Phi(C)\longrightarrow K
in
\mathsf{K}
making the triangle
diagram
\Phi(C)\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
commutative in
\mathsf{K}
.
In other words, condition
(
\spadesuit
)
is satisfied.

Proof.

This is a generalization of
[
18
, Lemma 1.6.H]
.
Applying the exact functor
\Psi^{-}
to the admissible epimorphism

K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
in
\mathsf{K}
, we obtain an admissible epimorphism

\Psi^{-}(K)\longrightarrow\Psi^{-}\Phi(B)
in
\mathsf{E}
.
Consider the adjunction morphism
B\longrightarrow\Psi^{-}\Phi(B)
in
\mathsf{E}
,
and take the pullback, producing a commutative square diagram in
\mathsf{E}

with an admissible epimorphism
C\longrightarrow B
:

\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Psi^{-}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Psi^{-}\Phi(B)}

Applying the functor
\Phi
, we obtain a commutative square in
the left-hand side of the diagram in
\mathsf{K}

\textstyle{\Phi(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi\Psi^{-}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi\Psi^{-}\Phi(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Phi(B)}

Here the horizontal arrows in the right-hand side are the adjunction
morphisms, the square in the right-hand side is commutative because
the adjunction counit is a natural transformation, and the composition
in the lower line is the identity morphism by the general property of
adjunctions.
It follows that the triangle
\Phi(C)\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow\Phi(B)
is
commutative in
\mathsf{K}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition
8.8
.

Notice first of all that if the exact category
\mathsf{K}
has either
enough projective or enough injective objects, then the assertions of
the proposition follow from the proof of
Theorem
5.6

(with Remark
5.12
).
In fact, one obtains a stronger result that any object of

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
is homotopy equivalent to the totalization of
an exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y_{n+1}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow 0
.
By Lemma
8.10
, it
follows that
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})={\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})^{*n}
in this case.

In the general case, we continue to follow
[
18
, proof of
Theorem 1.6]
and proceed within the context of the proof of
Proposition
8.2
.
Let
X\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
and
P\in\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
be two objects, and
let
f\colon P\longrightarrow X
be a morphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
As it was shown in the proof of
Proposition
8.2
, there exists
an exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{m}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
such that the composition

\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Our aim is to show that one can have
m<n
.
Assuming that
m\geq n
, we will explain how to shorten the complex

Y^{\prime}_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
so as to obtain an exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y_{m}\longrightarrow Y_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

with the same property that the composition

\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Applying the exact functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathsf{K}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}
, we
obtain an exact complex
0\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{m+1})\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{m})\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{0})\longrightarrow\Phi(P)\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{K}
.
This exact complex can be viewed as a Yoneda
(m+1)
-extension
representing some element of
\Ext_{\mathsf{K}}^{m+1}(\Phi(P),\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}))
.
By assumption, any such Ext group vanishes in
\mathsf{K}
; so the this

(m+1)
-extension splits in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.

Lemma
8.11
tells
that Proposition
8.7
is
applicable to the exact functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathsf{K}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\allowbreak\longrightarrow\mathsf{K}
.
Applying the proposition, we obtain a morphism of exact complexes
in
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
depicted in the lower half of the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}_{m-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with the property the exact complex in the middle row is obtained
by splicing short exact sequences, the leftmost one of which is
taken to a split short exact sequence by the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathsf{K}}
.

Let us express the latter property in notation with formulas.
The exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\longrightarrow Y_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
is obtained by splicing
a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\longrightarrow Y_{m}\longrightarrow 0
with an exact complex
0\longrightarrow Y_{m}\longrightarrow Y_{m-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
(depicted
in the upper line of the diagram).
The result of Proposition
8.7

allows us to choose the exact complex in the middle row of
the diagram in such a way that the admissible monomorphism

\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{m+1})\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime\prime}_{m})
is split, or in other words,
the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime}_{m+1})\longrightarrow\Phi(Y^{\prime\prime}_{m})\longrightarrow\Phi(Y_{m})\longrightarrow 0
is split in
\mathsf{K}
.

In view of Lemma
3.9
,
the latter property means that the short sequence
0\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\longrightarrow Y_{m}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is split exact in
the additive category
\mathbf{E}^{0}
.
Consequently, the totalization
\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\to Y_{m})
is
contractible, i. e., it represents a zero object in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
It follows that the morphism of totalizations induced by
the morphism of complexes in the upper half of the diagram,

\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Tot(Y_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})

is a homotopy equivalence, i. e., an isomorphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Since the composition

\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime}_{m}\to\dotsb\to Y^{\prime}_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muP\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX

vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
by the choice of the complex
Y^{\prime}_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
,
it follows that the composition

\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\\
\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime}_{m}\to\dotsb\to Y^{\prime}_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muP\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX

vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
as well.
Since the composition

\Tot(Y^{\prime}_{m+1}\to Y^{\prime\prime}_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\\
\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Tot(Y_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muP\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX

vanishes and the leftmost map is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
,
we can conclude that the composition

\Tot(Y_{m}\to Y_{m-1}\to\dotsb\to Y_{0})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muP\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muX

vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

We have managed to cut our original complex
Y^{\prime}_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
down to a shorter
complex
Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}
with the same property, as we wished.
By decreasing induction in
m
, we eventually arrive to an exact
complex
0\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which the composition

\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow P\longrightarrow X
vanishes in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Finally, given an absolutely acyclic object
X\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
,
we take
P=X
and
f=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, and find an exact complex

0\longrightarrow Y_{n}\longrightarrow Y_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow Y_{0}\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
for which the natural closed
morphism
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})\longrightarrow X
is homotopic to zero.
Then the argument spelled out at the very end of the proof of
Proposition
8.2
shows that the object
X

is a direct summand of the object
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\to X)
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

It remains to apply
Lemma
8.10
to
the effect that
\Tot(Y_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}\to X)\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})^{*n}
and
therefore
X\in\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
.
∎

8.6.
Examples

In the specific case of the exact DG-category of complexes in
an exact category, as per Example
4.39
,
a result stronger than the respective specialization of
Theorem
8.9
is long known.
In this case, the absolute derived category coincides not only with
the coderived and/or contraderived category, but also with
the conventional unbounded derived category;
see
[
34
, Remark 2.1]
.

Let us formulate here the particular cases of
Theorem
8.9
arising in
the context of Examples
4.40
–
4.42

and
6.1
.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring.
Let
\mathsf{K}\subset R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
be a full subcategory as in
the first paragraph of
Corollary
6.21
, and let

\mathbf{E}\subset\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
the related full DG-subcategory
endowed with the inherited exact DG-category structure.
Assume that the exact category
\mathsf{K}
has finite homological
dimension.

(a)
If the full subcategory
\mathsf{K}
is preserved by
infinite direct sums in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, then all coacyclic objects
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are absolutely acyclic, that is

\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and therefore the coderived
category of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E}).

(b)
If the full subcategory
\mathsf{K}
is preserved by
infinite products in
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
, then all contraacyclic objects
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are absolutely acyclic, that is

\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and therefore the contraderived
category of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E}).

Proof.

Similar to the proofs of
Corollaries
6.21

and
7.13
.
∎

The next corollary is a generalization of
[
18
, Theorem 1.6]
.

Corollary 8.13
.

Let
X
be a scheme and
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B^{*},d,h)
be a quasi-coherent
CDG-quasi-algebra over
X
.
Let
\mathsf{K}\subset B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
be a full subcategory as in
the first paragraph of
Corollary
6.22
,
and let
\mathbf{E}\subset\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
the related full DG-subcategory
endowed with the inherited exact DG-category structure.

Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{K}
is preserved by
infinite direct sums in
B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
and the exact category
\mathsf{K}

has finite homological dimension.
Then all coacyclic objects in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are absolutely acyclic,
that is
\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
, and therefore the coderived
category of
\mathbf{E}
coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E}).

Proof.

Similar to the proofs of
Corollaries
6.22

and
7.14
.
∎

The following corollary is a generalization
of
[
18
, Corollary 2.3(d,f)]
.

Corollary 8.14
.

Let
\mathsf{E}
be an exact category and
\Lambda\colon\mathsf{E}\longrightarrow\mathsf{E}
be
an autoequivalence preserving and reflecting short exact sequences.
Assume that either
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, or
\Lambda
is involutive
and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Let
w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{E}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
be a potential (as in
Section
2.5
).
Assume that the exact category
\mathsf{E}
has finite homological
dimension and exact coproducts.
Then all coacyclic objects in the DG-category of factorizations

\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w)
are absolutely acyclic, that is

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w))={\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w))
,
and therefore the coderived category of
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w)

coincides with the absolute derived category,

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w))=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{E},\Lambda,w)).

Proof.

Similar to the proofs of
Corollaries
6.23

and
7.15
.
∎

8.7.
Generalizations

The following result, whose idea goes back
to
[
38
, Proposition A.6.1]
, is a generalization of
Theorem
8.9
with
a flavor of Theorem
7.9
.

Theorem 8.15
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair and
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})\subset(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})

be an exact DG-subpair.
Assume that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact products,
the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is self-resolving in the exact category

\mathsf{K}
, and the exact category
\mathsf{L}
has finite homological dimension.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful.

Setting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{E}
and
\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{K}
, one obtains
Theorem
8.9
(b) as
a particular case of
Theorem
8.15
.

Proof.

When the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{F}
is closed under products in
\mathbf{E}
,
one can use Theorem
8.9
(b)
to the effect that
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})=\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})
, and
Theorem
7.9
(a) tells that
the functor
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
is fully faithful;
so the desired assertion follows.
The proof in the general case is a combination of the arguments
proving the two mentioned previous theorems.

According to Lemma
7.2
,
it suffices to show that all objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
are
approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
By Proposition
7.10
(a), all objects
of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
,
hence also via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
.
According to Lemma
7.5
,
the class of all objects approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
is a full triangulated subcategory in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
It remains to show that the class of all objects approachable
from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
is closed under infinite
products in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Let
F\longrightarrow\prod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

with
F\in\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
and all the objects
X_{\alpha}\in\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
.
Consider the components
F\longrightarrow X_{\alpha}
of the morphism

F\longrightarrow\prod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
.
Then for every
\alpha
there exists an object

Y_{\alpha}\in{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
such that the morphism
F\longrightarrow X_{\alpha}

factorizes through
Y_{\alpha}
.
It follows that the morphism
F\longrightarrow\prod_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}

factorizes through
\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

According to
Proposition
8.8
,
we have
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})=\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\rangle_{n}
, where

n
is the homological dimension of the exact category
\mathsf{L}
.
Furthermore,
\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})\rangle_{n}\subset\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
, and the class
\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}

is closed under products in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
by
Lemma
8.1
(b).
Thus
\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}\in\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}
;
and by the definition
\langle{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\rangle_{n}\subset{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
.

Following the proof of
Theorem
7.9
(a), all objects
of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via

{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Therefore, any morphism
F\longrightarrow\prod_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}
factorizes
through an object from
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
, and we are done.
∎

The next result, whose idea goes back
to
[
38
, Corollary A.6.2 and the subsequent paragraph]
,
is a common generalization of
Theorems
5.10

and
8.9
with a flavor of
Theorem
7.11
.

Theorem 8.16
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair and
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})\subset(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})

be a strict exact DG-subpair.
Assume that the exact DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has twists and
exact products, the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is resolving in the exact
category
\mathsf{K}
, and the exact category
\mathsf{L}
has finite homological
dimension.
Furthermore, assume that there is a resolving subcategory
\mathsf{M}\subset\mathsf{K}
preserved by the shift functors
[n]
,
n\in\Gamma

such that countable products of objects from
\mathsf{M}
in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
belong to
\mathsf{K}
and have finite
\mathsf{L}
-resolution
dimensions in
\mathsf{K}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is a triangulated equivalence.

Taking
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{proj}}
and
\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})_{\mathsf{proj}}
, one obtains
Theorem
5.10
(b) as
a particular case of
Theorem
8.16
.
Setting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{E}
and
\mathsf{M}=\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{K}
, one (almost) recovers
Theorem
8.9
(b) as
a particular case of
Theorem
8.16
.

Proof.

The triangulated functor
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
is
fully faithful by
Theorem
8.15
.
So it suffices to find for any object
E\in\mathbf{E}
an object

F\in\mathbf{F}
together with a morphism
F\longrightarrow E
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

with a cone belonging to
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{ctr}}(\mathbf{E})
.
This is provable similarly to the proof of
Theorem
5.10
(b).

Specifically, choose an object
M_{0}\in\mathsf{M}
together with
an admissible epimorphism
M_{0}\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{E}}^{K}(E)
in
\mathsf{K}
.
By Lemma
5.7
(b), the corresponding
morphism
\Psi^{+}(M_{0})\longrightarrow E
is an admissible epimorphism
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Similarly to the proof of
Lemma
5.8
(b), we have a natural
short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow M_{0}\longrightarrow\Phi\Psi^{+}(M_{0})\longrightarrow M_{0}[1]\longrightarrow 0
in the exact category
\mathsf{K}
.
Since the full subcategory
\mathsf{M}\subset\mathsf{K}
is closed under shifts
and extensions, it follows that
\Phi\Psi^{+}(M_{0})\in\mathsf{M}
.

Put
N_{0}=\Psi^{+}(M_{0})
.
Then we have a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow N_{0}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
\Phi(N_{0})\in\mathsf{M}
.
The same construction produces a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with

\Phi(N_{1})\in\mathsf{M}
, etc.
Proceeding in this way, we construct an exact complex

\dotsb\longrightarrow N_{2}\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{0}\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow 0
in

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
\Phi(N_{i})\in\mathsf{M}
.

Denote by
T=\Tot^{\sqcap}(N_{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}})
the product totalization of
the complex
\dotsb\longrightarrow N_{2}\longrightarrow N_{1}\longrightarrow N_{0}\longrightarrow 0

in the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
.
By Lemma
3.11
, the object
\Phi(T)

is isomorphic to the product
\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}\Phi(N_{i})[-i]

in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
.
By assumption, it follows that the object
\Phi(T)
has finite

\mathsf{L}
-resolution dimension in
\mathsf{K}
.
Denote this resolution dimension by
n
.
The cone of the natural closed morphism
T\longrightarrow E
in
\mathbf{E}

is contraacyclic by
Lemma
5.11
(b).

Choose an object
L_{0}\in\mathsf{L}
together with an admissible
epimorphism
L_{0}\longrightarrow\Phi(T)
in
\mathsf{K}
.
By Lemma
5.7
(b), the corresponding
morphism
\Psi^{+}(L_{0})\longrightarrow T
is an admissible epimorphism
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Furthermore, we have
F_{0}=\Psi^{+}(L_{0})\in\mathbf{F}
.
So we get a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow T_{1}\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
F_{0}\in\mathbf{F}
.
The same construction produces a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{2}\longrightarrow F_{1}\longrightarrow T_{1}\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

with
F_{1}\in\mathbf{F}
, etc.

Proceeding in this way and arguing similarly to the beginning of
the proof of Theorem
6.6
(a),
we construct an exact complex
0\longrightarrow D\longrightarrow F_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow 0
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
with
F_{i}\in\mathbf{F}
.
Then the complex
0\longrightarrow\Phi(D)\longrightarrow\Phi(F_{n-1})\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow\Phi(F_{0})\longrightarrow\Phi(T)\longrightarrow 0
is exact in
\mathsf{K}
with

\Phi(F_{i})\in\mathsf{L}
.
By Proposition
6.2
(a), it follows that

\Phi(D)\in\mathsf{L}
(we recall that
n
is the
\mathsf{L}
-resolution
dimension of
\Phi(T)
in
\mathsf{K}
).

By the assumption that
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})
is a strict exact DG-subpair
in
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
, we can conclude that
D\in\mathbf{F}
.
Put
F_{n}=D
, and denote by
F
the totalization of the finite
complex
F_{n}\longrightarrow F_{n-1}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow F_{0}
in
the DG-category
\mathbf{F}
.
Then the cone of the natural closed morphism
F\longrightarrow T
in
\mathbf{E}

is absolutely acyclic
by Lemma
5.9
.
We have
F\in\mathbf{F}
, and the cone of the composition of closed
morphisms
F\longrightarrow T\longrightarrow E
is contraacyclic in
\mathbf{E}
.
∎

9.
Compact Generation Theorem

9.1.
Grothendieck DG-categories

We recall from Section
4.5
that
an additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones is said to be

abelian
if both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are abelian.
In fact, according to
Proposition
4.34
, it suffices that
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
be abelian.
Any abelian DG-category carries the abelian exact DG-category
structure, which consists of the abelian exact structures on both
the abelian categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category.
We recall that an object
G\in\mathsf{C}
is said to be a
generator
of

\mathsf{C}
if for any two parallel morphisms
f^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime\prime}\colon C\longrightarrow D
in

\mathsf{C}
the equation
f^{\prime}g=f^{\prime\prime}g
for all morphisms
g\colon G\longrightarrow C
in

\mathsf{C}
implies
f^{\prime}=f^{\prime\prime}
.
A generator
G
is called a
strong generator
if for any
morphism
f\colon C\longrightarrow D
in
\mathsf{C}
bijectivity of the map of sets

\Hom(G,f)\colon\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(G,C)\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(G,D)
implies that

f
is an isomorphism (see
[
1
, Section 0.6]
for an equivalent
definition).
Similarly one defines a multi-object generator (a
generating
set of objects
) of a category, as in
[
1
]
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.

(a)
Let
A\in\mathbf{A}
be an object which is a generator of
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Then the object
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is a generator of
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Besides, the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
also takes strong generators to
strong generators.

(b)
Let
X\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
be an object which is a generator of
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Then the object
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}(X)\in\mathbf{A}
is a generator of the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Besides, the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
also takes strong generators
to strong generators.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
, the functors
\Phi
and

\Psi^{+}
have faithful right adjoint functors (
\Psi^{-}
and
\Phi
,
respectively).
A functor left adjoint to a faithful functor takes generators to
generators, as one can see immediately from the definitions.
Furthermore, the functors
\Psi^{-}
and
\Phi
are conservative by
Lemma
3.12
.
A functor left adjoint to a faithful conservative functor takes
strong generators to strong generators.
∎

The definition of the
coproduct
of an (infinite) family
of objects in a DG-category was given in
Section
1.3
.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.
Then an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
is the coproduct of a family of objects

A_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{A}
if and only if the object
A\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is
the coproduct of the family of objects
A_{\alpha}\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
So the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has coproducts if and only if
the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
does.

Proof.

The “only if” assertion follows directly from the fact that
the functor assigning to a complex of abelian groups its group of
degree
0
cocycles preserves products.
This implication, mentioned in Section
1.3
,
does not require the assumptions of
\mathbf{A}
having shifts or cones.

To prove the “if”, assume that an object
A
is the coproduct of
objects
A_{\alpha}
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Let
\iota_{\alpha}\colon A_{\alpha}\longrightarrow A
be the structure morphisms of

A
as the coproduct of
A_{\alpha}
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
; so
\iota_{\alpha}\in\Hom^{0}_{\mathbf{A}}(A_{\alpha},A)
are closed morphisms of degree
0
in

\mathbf{A}
, that is
d(\iota_{\alpha})=0
.
Then, for every object
B\in\mathbf{A}
, the morphisms
\iota_{\alpha}

induce a morphism of complexes of abelian groups

(38)

\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A,B)\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\prod\nolimits_{\alpha}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(A_{\alpha},B).

We need to prove that (
38
) is
an isomorphism of complexes of abelian groups.
For this purpose, it suffices to show that the same map is
an isomorphism of the underlying graded abelian groups of
the complexes in question.
Replacing
B
with its shifts
B[n]
,
n\in\Gamma
, it remains
to show that (
38
) is an isomorphism
of the underling abelian groups in the cohomological degree
0
.

The latter fact is established by the observation that the functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
, being a left adjoint, preserves coproducts, so

\Phi(A)
is the coproduct of
\Phi(A_{\alpha})
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
,
together with Lemma
3.9
.
∎

We refer to
[
1
, Theorem 1.5]
or
[
2
]

for a discussion of
directed
and
filtered
colimits.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an abelian DG-category with coproducts.
Then the coproduct functors are exact in the abelian category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
if and only if they are exact in the abelian category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
The directed colimit functors are exact in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
if and only
if they are exact in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

Proof.

The assertion about coproducts is a particular case of
Lemma
5.1
(a).
The assertion about directed colimits is provable similarly: it follows
from the observation that the functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
,
being left adjoints, preserve all colimits, together with the fact that
these functors are exact and faithful (so they take nonzero objects
to nonzero objects).
∎

In an abelian category, any generator (or generating set) is strong,
and existence of coproducts implies existence of colimits.
A
Grothendieck category
is an abelian category with a generator,
infinite coproducts, and exact directed/filtered colimits.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an abelian DG-category.

(a)
If the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is Grothendieck,
then so is the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
The DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has coproducts in this case.

(b)
If the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has coproducts and
the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is Grothendieck, then
the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is Grothendieck.

Proof.

Combine the results of Lemmas
9.1
,

9.2
,
and
9.3
.
∎

We will say that an abelian DG-category is
Grothendieck
if
the abelian categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are
Grothendieck.

Examples 9.5
.

(1) Let
\mathsf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
Then the abelian DG-category
\mathbf{C}(\mathsf{A})
of (unbounded) complexes
in
\mathsf{A}
, as in Example
4.39
, is
a Grothendieck DG-category.

(2) Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring.
Then the abelian DG-category
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
of left CDG-modules
over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, as in Example
4.40
,
is a Grothendieck DG-category.
In fact,
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
is even a locally finitely presentable
abelian DG-category in the sense of the definition in
the next Section
9.2
.

(3) Let
X
be a scheme and
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B^{*},d,h)
be a quasi-coherent
CDG-quasi-algebra over
X
.
Then the abelian DG-category
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent left
CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
, as in
Example
4.41
, is
a Grothendieck DG-category.

(4) Let
\mathsf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category and

\Lambda\colon\mathsf{A}\longrightarrow\mathsf{A}
be an autoequivalence.
Assume that either
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}
, or
\Lambda
is involutive
and
\Gamma=\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Let
w\colon\mathrm{Id}_{\mathsf{A}}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{2}
be a potential.
Then the abelian DG-category of factorizations
\mathbf{F}(\mathsf{A},\Lambda,w)
,
as in Example
4.42
,
is a Grothendieck DG-category.

9.2.
Finitely generated and finitely presentable objects

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category with directed colimits.
An object
P\in\mathsf{C}
is said to be
finitely
presentable

[
1
, Definition 1.1]
if the functor
\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(P,{-})

from
\mathsf{C}
to the category of sets preserves directed colimits.
The object
P
is said to be
finitely
generated
(
[
1
, Definitions 1.13(1) and 1.67]
for

\lambda=\aleph_{0}
) if the functor
\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(P,{-})
preserves
the colimits of directed diagrams of monomorphisms.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones
such that both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
have directed colimits.
Then both the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

preserve finite generatedness and finite presentability of objects.

Proof.

Any functor left adjoint to a functor preserving directed colimits
takes finitely generated objects to finitely generated objects and
finitely presentable objects to finitely presentable objects
(notice that any right adjoint functor takes monomorphisms to
monomorphisms).
Furthermore, both the right adjoint functors
\Psi^{-}_{\mathbf{A}}
and

\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
preserve directed colimits, since they, in turn, have
right adjoints.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones
such that both the additive categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
have directed colimits.
Then both the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

reflect
finite generatedness and finite presentability
of objects.
In other words, an object
A\in\mathbf{A}
is finitely generated
(respectively, finitely presentable) in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
whenever
the object
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
is finitely generated (resp., finitely
presentable) in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
An object
X\in\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
is finitely generated (resp., finitely
presentable) in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
whenever the object

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}(X)
is finitely generated (resp., finitely presentable)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathbf{A}
be an object such that the object
\Phi(A)
is finitely
presentable in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Then, by Lemma
9.6
,
the object
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
is finitely presentable
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
According to Lemma
3.8
, we have

\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)\simeq\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
.
By Lemma
4.2
, we have a natural kernel-cokernel
pair of morphisms
A[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)\longrightarrow A
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
;
hence the object
A
is the cokernel of a natural morphism
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
It remains to observe that the class of finitely presentable objects
is preserved by cokernels.
All the other assertions are provable similarly.
∎

A category
\mathsf{C}
is said to be
locally finitely presentable
if
it has all colimits and a strong generating set consisting of finitely
presentable objects
[
1
, Definition 1.9 and Theorem 1.11]
.
The class (or full subcategory) of all finitely presentable objects
in
\mathsf{C}
is denoted by
\mathsf{C}_{\mathsf{fp}}\subset\mathsf{C}
.
Classical references on locally finitely presentable (and more
generally, finitely accessible) additive categories include
the papers
[
28
,
15
,
26
]
; see also the discussion
in
[
53
, Section 8.1]
.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts and cones.

(a)
If the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is locally
finitely presentable, then so is the additive category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

(b)
If the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
has all
colimits and the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is locally
finitely presentable, then the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

is locally finitely presentable as well.

Proof.

Let us show that the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
has colimits
whenever the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
does.
Indeed, the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
has coproducts in this case by
Lemma
9.2
, hence so does
the DG-category
\mathbf{A}^{\natural}
and the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
4.16
the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
has cokernels whenever the additive
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
does.
Therefore, all colimits exist in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.

Now we can assume that both the categories
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
have colimits.
Lemma
9.1
has an obvious version for
single generators replaced with generating sets, which can be applied
together with Lemma
9.6

to prove that the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
has a set of finitely
presentable generators if and only if the category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
does.
∎

An additive DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with shifts and cones is said to be

locally finitely presentable
if both the additive categories

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are locally finitely presentable.
Similarly one can define the notion of a
locally

\lambda
-presentable DG-category
for a regular cardinal
\lambda

(based on the notion of a locally
\lambda
-presentable
category
[
1
, Definition 1.17 and Theorem 1.20]
).
We refer to
[
53
, Section 6.2]
for a detailed discussion of
locally presentable DG-categories.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
Then an object of
\mathsf{A}
is finitely generated if and only if it
cannot be produced as the union of an infinite ascending chain of its
subobjects.
Equivalently, an object
A\in\mathsf{A}
is finitely generated if and only if,
whenever
A
is the sum of a family of its subobjects
(A_{\alpha})
,
there exists a finite subfamily in
(A_{\alpha})
whose sum is also equal
to
A
.
The full subcategory
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{fg}}\subset\mathsf{A}
of all finitely generated
objects in
\mathsf{A}
is closed under quotients and extensions.
All finitely generated objects are quotients of finite direct sums of
copies of the generator of the category; so up to isomorphism, there is
only a set of objects in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathsf{fg}}
.

We will only define
local finite generatedness
in the context
of
abelian
categories, referring to
[
1
, Section 1.E]
for
the general case and taking into account the known fact that any
locally finitely generated abelian category is Grothendieck (see,
e. g.,
[
49
, Corollary 9.6]
for a more general result).
A Grothendieck abelian category is said to be
locally finitely
generated
if it has a generating set consisting of finitely generated
objects.
Any object in a locally finitely generated abelian category is
the sum of its finitely generated subobjects.

Proposition 9.9
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian DG-category.
The the abelian category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is locally finitely generated
if and only if the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is locally
finitely generated.

Proof.

Follows from Lemmas
9.1

and
9.6
.
∎

A Grothendieck abelian DG-category is said to be
locally
finitely generated
if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Proposition
9.9
.

9.3.
Exactly embedded full abelian DG-subcategories

Let
\mathsf{A}
be an abelian category and
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathsf{A}
be a full
subcategory.

Assume that
\mathsf{B}
is an abelian category.
We will say that
\mathsf{B}
is an
exactly embedded full abelian
subcategory
in
\mathsf{A}
if the inclusion
\mathsf{B}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{A}
is
an exact functor between abelian categories.
Equivalently, this means that
\mathsf{B}
inherits an exact category
structure from the abelian exact category structure of
\mathsf{A}
and
the inherited exact category structure on
\mathsf{B}
is the abelian
exact category structure.

A full subcategory
\mathsf{B}
in an abelian category
\mathsf{A}
is an exactly
embedded full abelian subcategory if and only if it is closed under
finite direct sums, kernels, and cokernels.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an abelian DG-category, and
\mathbf{B}\subset\mathbf{A}
be
a full additive DG-subcategory closed under shifts and cones.
Assume that
\mathbf{B}
is an abelian DG-category.

We will say that
\mathbf{B}
is an
exactly embedded full abelian
DG-subcategory
in
\mathbf{A}
if both the fully faithful inclusions

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B})\rightarrowtail\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{B}^{\natural})\rightarrowtail\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
are exact functors between abelian
categories.
Equivalently, this means that the full DG-subcategory
\mathbf{B}
inherits
an exact DG-category structure from the abelian exact DG-category
structure of
\mathbf{A}
and the inherited exact DG-category structure on

\mathbf{B}
is the abelian exact DG-category structure.

A more detailed discussion of exactly embedded full abelian
DG-subcategories in abelian DG-categories can be found
in
[
53
, Section 3.3]
; see, in particular,

[
53
, Proposition 3.11]
.

9.4.
Locally Noetherian DG-categories

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian category.
An object
N\in\mathsf{A}
is said to be
Noetherian
if it has no
infinite ascending chain of subobjects.
Equivalently, an object in
\mathsf{A}
is Noetherian if and only if all
its subobjects are finitely generated.
The class of all Noetherian objects in
\mathsf{A}
is closed under
subobjects, quotients, and extensions; so the full subcategory of
Noetherian objects is an exactly embedded full abelian subcategory
in
\mathsf{A}
.

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian DG-category.
Then both the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

preserve
and
reflect
Noetherianity of objects.
The class of all Noetherian objects in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is preserved
by shifts and twists (hence also by cones).

Proof.

Any faithful exact functor reflects Noetherianity of objects,
because it takes an infinite ascending chain of subobjects to
an infinite ascending chain of subobjects.
Now let
A\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
be a Noetherian object.
By Lemma
4.2
, we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow A[-1]\longrightarrow\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0
in the abelian
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, implying that
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
is also a Noetherian
object in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
According to Lemma
3.8
, we have
\Xi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)\simeq\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
.
Since the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
reflects Noetherianity, we can conclude
that
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(A)
is a Noetherian object in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
The preservation of Noetherianity by the functor
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}
is
provable similarly.

Concerning the second assertion of the lemma, the preservation by
shifts is obvious, and the preservation by cones can be deduced
from the result obtained in the proof of
Lemma
4.2
.
More generally, the preservation of Noetherianity by twists follows
from the facts that the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
preserves and reflects
Noetherianity and takes twists to isomorphisms (see
Lemma
3.11
).
∎

A Grothendieck abelian category
\mathsf{A}
is said to be
locally
Noetherian
if it has a generating set of Noetherian objects.
Equivalently, this means that any object of
\mathsf{A}
is the sum of its
Noetherian subobjects.
An object of a locally Noetherian category is finitely generated
if and only if it is finitely presentable and if and only if it is
Noetherian.

Proposition 9.11
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a Grothendieck abelian DG-category.
Then the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is locally Noetherian if and only if
the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is locally Noetherian.

Proof.

Follows from Lemmas
9.1

and
9.10
.
∎

We will say that a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is
locally Noetherian

if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Proposition
9.11
.
Assuming this is the case, consider the full DG-subcategory

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}\subset\mathbf{A}
whose objects are all the finitely generated
(equivalently, Noetherian) objects in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, that is

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}})=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})_{\mathsf{fg}}
.
It follows from Lemma
9.10

that the objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}})^{\natural})
are all the finitely
generated (equivalently, Noetherian) objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

(so the notation
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}^{\natural}
is unambigous).
The full DG-subcategory of Noetherian objects
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}
is
an exactly embedded full abelian DG-subcategory in
\mathbf{A}
.

Examples 9.12
.

(1) Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring and
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
be
the DG-category of left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Consider the DG-ring
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*},\partial)

from Section
3.1
; so
\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}^{*}
is
the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
with the sign of the grading changed.

According to Example
3.17
, the abelian
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is equivalent to
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.
On the other hand, it was mentioned in the proof of
Proposition
3.1
that the abelian category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is obviously equivalent to
R^{*}[\delta]{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
.

The category
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is locally Noetherian if and only if
the graded ring
R^{*}
is graded left Noetherian.
Similarly, the category
R^{*}[\delta]{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is locally Noetherian
if and only if the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
is graded left
Noetherian.
Hence Proposition
9.11
provides
a fancy proof of the assertion that the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]

is graded left Noetherian if and only if the graded ring
R^{*}
is.
These equivalent conditions characterize CDG-rings
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

for which the DG-category
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
is locally Noetherian.

Given a regular cardinal
\lambda
, one can similarly show that
every homogeneous left ideal in
R^{*}
has
<\lambda
generators
if and only if every homogeneous left ideal in
R^{*}[\delta]

has
<\lambda
generators.

(2) Let
X
be a quasi-compact quasi-separated scheme and
B^{*}

be a quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra over
X
.
Then the Grothendieck abelian category
B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
of quasi-coherent
graded left modules over
B^{*}
is locally Noetherian if and only if,
for every affine open subscheme
U\subset X
, the graded ring

B^{*}(U)
is graded left Noetherian.
If this is the case, the quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
B^{*}

is said to be
graded left Noetherian
.
Furthermore, a quasi-coherent graded left
B^{*}
-module
M^{*}
is
Noetherian if and only if, for every affine open subscheme

U\subset X
, the graded
B^{*}(U)
-module
M^{*}(U)
is Noetherian.

Let
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(B^{*},d,h)
be a quasi-coherent CDG-quasi-algebra
over
X
and
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
be the DG-category of quasi-coherent
left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
According to Example
3.18
, the abelian
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is equivalent to
B^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--qcoh}}}
.
Thus the Grothendieck DG-category
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--qcoh}}}
is locally Noetherian
if and only if the quasi-coherent graded quasi-algebra
B^{*}
is graded
left Noetherian.

9.5.
Locally coherent DG-categories

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a locally finitely generated abelian category.
A finitely generated object
A\in\mathsf{A}
is called
coherent
if,
for any morphism
f\colon B\longrightarrow A
to
A
from a finitely generated
object
B\in\mathsf{A}
, the kernel of
f
is also finitely generated.
The class of all coherent objects in
\mathsf{A}
is closed under kernels,
cokernels, and extensions.

Lemma 9.13
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a locally finitely generated abelian DG-category.
Then both the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

preserve
and
reflect
coherence of objects.
The class of all coherent objects in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is preserved
by shifts and twists (hence also by cones).

Proof.

Let us show that the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
reflects coherence.
Let
A\in\mathbf{A}
be an object for which the object
\Phi(A)
is
coherent in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Let
B\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
be a finitely generated object and

f\colon B\longrightarrow A
be a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Put
K=\ker(f)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
By Lemma
9.6
,
the object
\Phi(B)
is finitely generated in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Hence the kernel of the morphism
\Phi(f)\colon\Phi(B)\longrightarrow\Phi(A)

is finitely generated in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
as well.
Since the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
is exact, we have
\ker\Phi(f)=\Phi(K)
, so the object
\Phi(K)
is finitely generated.
Using Lemma
9.7
,
we can conclude that the object
K\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is finitely
generated.
Thus the object
A\in\mathbf{A}
is coherent.

The rest of the proof is similar to that of the proof of
Lemma
9.10
and based on
the fact that the class of all coherent objects is closed under
extensions.
For an alternative exposition, see
[
53
, Lemmas 8.5–8.6]
.
∎

A locally finitely generated abelian category is said to be

locally coherent
if it has a generating set consisting of
coherent objects
[
54
, Section 2]
.
Any locally coherent category is locally finitely presentable.
An object of a locally coherent category is coherent if and only if
it is finitely presentable.
We refer to
[
51
, Section 13]
and
[
53
, Section 8.2]
for
a further discussion.

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a locally finitely generated abelian DG-category.
Then the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is locally coherent if and only if
the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is locally coherent.

Proof.

Follows from Lemmas
9.1

and
9.13
.
∎

We will say that a DG-category
\mathbf{A}
is
locally coherent

if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Proposition
9.14
.
If this is the case, consider the full DG-subcategory

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}}\subset\mathbf{A}
whose objects are all the finitely presentable
(equivalently, coherent) objects in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, that is

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}})=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})_{\mathsf{fp}}
.
It follows from Lemma
9.13

that the objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}((\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}})^{\natural})
are all the finitely
presentable (equivalently, coherent) objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})

(so the notation
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}}^{\natural}
is unambigous).
The full DG-subcategory of coherent objects
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fp}}
is
an exactly embedded full abelian DG-subcategory in
\mathbf{A}
.

Example 9.15
.

Let
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}=(R^{*},d,h)
be a CDG-ring and
\mathbf{A}=\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
be
the DG-category of left CDG-modules over
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}
.
Continuing the discussion in
Example
9.12
(1), we observe that
the category of graded modules
R^{*}{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is locally coherent if
and only if the graded ring
R^{*}
is graded left coherent (i. e.,
all finitely generated homogeneous left ideals in
R^{*}
are finitely
presented as left
R^{*}
-modules).
Similarly, the category
R^{*}[\delta]{\operatorname{\mathsf{--mod}}}
is locally coherent
if and only if the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]
is graded left
coherent.
Thus Proposition
9.14
provides
a fancy proof of the assertion that the graded ring
R^{*}[\delta]

is graded left coherent if and only if the graded ring
R^{*}
is.
These equivalent conditions characterize CDG-rings
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}

for which the DG-category
\boldsymbol{R}^{\text{\smaller$\scriptstyle\blackdiamond$}}{\operatorname{\mathbf{--mod}}}
is locally coherent.
(See also
[
53
, Corollary 8.9]
.)

9.6.
Small objects

Let
\mathsf{A}
be an additive category with infinite coproducts,
and let
(A_{\upsilon}\in\mathsf{A})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
be a family of
objects in
\mathsf{A}
indexed by some set
\Upsilon
.
Then, for any subset
\Xi\subset\Upsilon
, the coproduct

\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}A_{\xi}
can be viewed as a split subobject
of the coproduct
\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
.
In other words, there is a natural split monomorphism

\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}A_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
in
\mathsf{A}
, whose cokernel
is the natural split epimorphism
\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi}A_{\zeta}
.

For any object
S\in\mathsf{A}
, there is a natural map of abelian groups

(39)

\bigoplus\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Hom_{\mathsf{A}}(S,A_{\upsilon})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Hom_{\mathsf{A}}(S,\coprod\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}).

Using the fact that the morphism
\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}A_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
is a split monomorphism
for any finite subset
\Xi\subset\Upsilon
, one can immediately see
that the map (
39
) is injective
for all objects
S
and
A_{\upsilon}\in\mathsf{A}
.

We will say that an object
S\in\mathsf{A}
is
small
if, for any
family of objects
(A_{\upsilon}\in\mathsf{A})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
,
the map (
39
) is an isomorphism.
Equivalently, an object
S\in\mathsf{A}
is small if and only if, for
every family of objects
(A_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
and
every morphism
f\colon S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}

in
\mathsf{A}
, there exists a finite subset
\Xi\subset\Upsilon
such that
the morphism
f
factorizes through the split monomorphism

\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}A_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
; or equivalently, the composition of
f
with the split
epimorphism
\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi}A_{\zeta}
vanishes.

Clearly, any finitely generated object in an additive category
with directed colimits is small.
Small modules are otherwise known as “dually slender”; and small
objects were called “weakly finitely generated” in
[
49
]
;
we refer to
[
49
, Remark 9.4]
for a further discussion with
references.
Small objects in triangulated categories are known as
compact
.

Lemma 9.16
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be an additive category with coproducts.

(a)
If
S\longrightarrow T
is an epimorphism in
\mathsf{A}
and
S\in\mathsf{A}

is a small object, then
T
is also a small object in
\mathsf{A}
.

(b)
If
S\overset{j}{\longrightarrow}T\overset{k}{\longrightarrow}R\longrightarrow 0
is
a composable pair of morphisms in
\mathsf{A}
such that
k=\coker j
,
and both
S
and
R
are small objects, then
T
is also a small
object in
\mathsf{A}
.

Proof.

Part (a) is the particular case of part (b) for
R=0
.
To prove part (b), consider a family of objects

(A_{\upsilon}\in\mathsf{A})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
and a morphism

f\colon T\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
in
\mathsf{A}
.
Consider the composition
S\overset{j}{\longrightarrow}T\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
.
Since the object
S\in\mathsf{A}
is small, there exists a finite
subset
\Xi^{\prime}\subset\Upsilon
such that the composition
S\overset{j}{\longrightarrow}T\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi^{\prime}}A_{\zeta}
vanishes.
Since the morphism
k
is a cokernel of
j
, it follows that
the morphism
T\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi^{\prime}}A_{\zeta}

factorizes through the epimorphism
k
.

So we obtain a morphism
g\colon R\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi^{\prime}}A_{\zeta}
.
Since the object
R\in\mathsf{A}
is small, there exists a finite subset

\Xi^{\prime\prime}\subset\Upsilon\setminus\Xi^{\prime}
such that the composition

R\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi^{\prime}}A_{\zeta}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus(\Xi^{\prime}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime})}A_{\zeta}

vanishes.
Now one easily concludes that the composition

T\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus(\Xi^{\prime}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime})}A_{\zeta}
vanishes,
and it remains to say that the union
\Xi^{\prime}\cup\Xi^{\prime\prime}
of two finite
subsets
\Xi^{\prime}
and
\Xi^{\prime\prime}
in
\Upsilon
is finite.
∎

Lemma 9.17
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts, cones, and
infinite coproducts.
Then both the additive functors
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
and
\Psi^{+}_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})\longrightarrow\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

preserve
and
reflect
smallness of objects.
The class of all small object in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
is preserved
by shifts and twists (hence also by cones).

Proof.

Let us show that the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
reflects smallness.
Let
S\in\mathbf{A}
be an object for which the object
\Phi(S)
is small
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
Let
(A_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
be a family of objects
in
\mathbf{A}
and
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
be
a morphism in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
The functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
, being a left adjoint, preserves coproducts;
so we have the induced morphism
\Phi(f)\colon\Phi(S)\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Phi(A_{\upsilon})
in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
.
By assumption, there exists a finite subset
\Xi\subset\Upsilon

for which the composition
\Phi(S)\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Phi(A_{\upsilon})\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi}\Phi(A_{\zeta})
is zero.
This composition can be obtained by applying the functor
\Phi

to the composition
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\zeta\in\Upsilon\setminus\Xi}A_{\zeta}
of morphisms
in the category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Since the functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
is faithful, it follows that the latter
composition vanishes in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, as desired.

The rest of the proof is similar to that of the proofs of
Lemmas
9.10

and
9.13
, and uses
Lemmas
3.8
,
4.2
,
and
9.16
(b).
∎

Lemma 9.18
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an additive DG-category with shifts, cones, and
infinite coproducts, and let
S\in\mathbf{A}
be an object which is small
in the additive category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.
Then the object
S
is also small (compact) in the triangulated
category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Let
(A_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
be a family of objects
in
\mathbf{A}
.
Then there is a natural map of complexes of abelian groups

(40)

\bigoplus\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(S,A_{\upsilon})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Hom_{\mathbf{A}}^{\text{\smaller\smaller$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}(S,\coprod\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}).

induced by the natural closed morphisms
A_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
of degree
0
in
\mathbf{A}
.
We would like to show that (
40
)
is an isomorphism of complexes of abelian groups.

For this purpose, it suffices to check that the underlying map
of (
40
) is an isomorphism of
graded abelian groups.
Replacing all
A_{\upsilon}
with
A_{\upsilon}[n]
for a given

n\in\Gamma
, the question is reduced to showing
that (
40
) is an isomorphism on
the grading components of degree zero.

It remains to interpret the map induced
by (
40
) on the grading components
of degree zero as the map

(41)

\bigoplus\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}(\Phi(S),\Phi(A_{\upsilon}))\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\Hom_{\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})}(\Phi(S),\coprod\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}\Phi(A_{\upsilon}))

using Lemma
3.9
and
the fact that the functor
\Phi
preserves coproducts.
Then the map (
41
) is
an isomorphism of abelian groups, since the object
\Phi(S)\in\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
is small by
Lemma
9.17
.
Thus the map (
40
) is an isomorphism
on the grading components of degree zero, as desired.

We have shown that the map (
40
)
is an isomorphism of complexes of abelian groups.
The passage to the degree
0
cohomology groups of the complexes
in (
40
) proves that
S
is a small
object in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
.

Alternatively, one can simply say that if every morphism

S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
in the category

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
factorizes through
\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}A_{\xi}
for some
finite subset
\Xi\subset\Upsilon
, then the same holds for every
morphism
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}A_{\upsilon}
in
the category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
, because any morphism in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

can be lifted to a morphism between the same objects
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})

and the coproducts in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
and
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})
agree.
∎

9.7.
Full-and-faithfulness and compactness theorem

We start with two lemmas, which are formulated in terms
of the notion of approachability in triangulated categories
(see Section
7.2
).
The following one should be compared with
Lemma
7.4
.

Lemma 9.19
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a (triangulated) category with infinite coproducts
and
\mathsf{S}
,
\mathsf{Y}\subset\mathsf{T}
be two full subcategories.
Assume that the full subcategory
\mathsf{S}
consists of (some)
compact objects in
\mathsf{T}
, while the full subcategory
\mathsf{Y}

is closed under finite direct sums in
\mathsf{T}
.
Then the full subcategory of all objects approachable from
\mathsf{S}

via
\mathsf{Y}
is closed under coproducts in
\mathsf{T}
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{\upsilon})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}
be a family of objects
in
\mathsf{T}
such that, for every
\upsilon\in\Upsilon
, the object

X_{\upsilon}
is approachable from
\mathsf{S}
via
\mathsf{Y}
.
Let
S\in\mathsf{S}
be an object and
f\colon S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}X_{\upsilon}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{T}
.
Then, since the object
S
is compact in
\mathsf{T}
, the morphism
f

factorizes as
S\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\xi\in\Xi}X_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}X_{\upsilon}
, where
\Xi
is
a finite subset of
\Upsilon
.
Since every component
S\longrightarrow X_{\xi}
,
\xi\in\Xi
factorizes
through an object of
\mathsf{Y}
and the full subcategory
\mathsf{Y}
is closed
under finite direct sums in
\mathsf{T}
, we can conclude that
the morphism
f
factorizes through an object of
\mathsf{Y}
.
∎

Lemma 9.20
.

Let
\mathsf{T}
be a triangulated category with infinite coproducts and

\mathsf{S}
,
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{T}
be (strictly) full triangulated subcategories
such that the full subcategory
\mathsf{X}
is closed under coproducts
in
\mathsf{T}
.
Let
\mathsf{Y}\subset\mathsf{S}\cap\mathsf{X}
be a full triangulated subcategory in
the intersection.
Assume that all the objects of
\mathsf{S}
are compact in
\mathsf{T}
and
all the objects of
\mathsf{X}
are approachable from
\mathsf{S}
via
\mathsf{Y}
.
Then the triangulated functor between the Verdier quotient categories

\mathsf{S}/\mathsf{Y}\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}

induced by the inclusion of the triangulated categories
\mathsf{S}\rightarrowtail\mathsf{T}
is fully faithful, and all the objects in its
image are compact in
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
.

Proof.

The full-and-faithfulness assertion is a particular case
of Lemma
7.2
.
Before proving compactness, we need to explain why coproducts
exist in the quotient category
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
.
Indeed, since the triangulated category
\mathsf{T}
has coproducts and
the full subcategory
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathsf{T}
is closed under coproducts,
it follows that the quotient category
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
also has coproducts
and the Verdier quotient functor
\mathsf{T}\longrightarrow\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
preserves
coproducts by
[
11
, Lemma 1.5]
or
[
31
, Lemma 3.2.10]
.

To establish the compactness, we have to recall some details of
the proof of Lemma
7.2
.
Consider a family of objects
(T_{\upsilon}\in\mathsf{T})_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}

and an object
S\in\mathsf{S}
.
By the construction of the triangulated Verdier quotient category,
any morphism
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
in
the quotient category
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
is represented by a fraction
(or “roof”)
S\longleftarrow D\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
of two morphisms
D\longrightarrow S
and
D\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
in
\mathsf{T}
, where a cone
X

of the morphism
D\longrightarrow S
belongs to
\mathsf{X}
.
So we have a distinguished triangle
D\longrightarrow S\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow D[1]

in
\mathsf{T}
with
S\in\mathsf{S}
and
X\in\mathsf{X}
.
By the approachability assumption of the lemma, the morphism

S\longrightarrow X
factorizes as
S\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow X
, where
Y\in\mathsf{Y}
.
It is important for our argument that
Y\in\mathsf{S}\cap\mathsf{X}
.

Put
G=\cone(S\to Y)[-1]
, so we have a distinguished triangle

G\longrightarrow S\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow G[1]
in
\mathsf{S}\subset\mathsf{T}
.
Then the pair of morphisms
\mathrm{id}_{S}\colon S\longrightarrow S
and
Y\longrightarrow X

can be completed to a morphism of distinguished triangles in
\mathsf{T}
;
so we get a morphism
G\longrightarrow D
making the triangle diagram

G\longrightarrow D\longrightarrow S
commutative.

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D[1]}

By the octahedron axiom, since both the morphisms
D\longrightarrow S
and

G\longrightarrow S
have cones belonging to
\mathsf{X}
, a cone of the morphism

G\longrightarrow D
also belongs to
\mathsf{X}
.

Now our morphism
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}

in
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
can be represented by the fraction

S\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5muD\mskip 1.5mu\leftarrow\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\mskip 1.5muG\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5muD\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\coprod\nolimits_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}

of morphisms in
\mathsf{T}
with an object
G\in\mathsf{S}
.
Consider the composition
G\longrightarrow D\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
in
\mathsf{T}
.
Since the object
G\in\mathsf{S}
is compact in
\mathsf{T}
, it follows that there
exists a finite subset of indices
\Xi\subset\Upsilon
such that
the morphism
G\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}

factorizes as
G\longrightarrow\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}T_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
in
\mathsf{T}
.
Thus the original morphism
S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
in the quotient category
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{X}
factorizes as

S\longrightarrow\coprod_{\xi\in\Xi}T_{\xi}\longrightarrow\coprod_{\upsilon\in\Upsilon}T_{\upsilon}
.
∎

The following theorem is a generalization
of
[
35
, Theorem 3.11.1]
and
[
41
, Corollary 2.6(a)]
.

Theorem 9.21
.

Let
(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})
be an exact DG-pair and
(\mathbf{F},\mathsf{L})\subset(\mathbf{E},\mathsf{K})

be an exact DG-subpair.
Assume that the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
has exact coproducts,
the full subcategory
\mathsf{K}\subset\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E}^{\natural})
is closed under
coproducts, the full subcategory
\mathsf{L}
is self-resolving in
\mathsf{K}
,
and all the objects of
\mathsf{L}
are small in
\mathsf{K}
.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

induced by the inclusion of exact DG-categories
\mathbf{F}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{E}
is fully faithful, and all the objects in its essential image
are compact in
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
.

Proof.

First of all, let us explain once again why coproducts exist in
the coderived category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
(so the compactness assertion
makes sense).
Indeed, coproducts exist in the homotopy category
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})

(induced by the coproducts in the DG-category
\mathbf{E}
), and
the full subcategory of coacyclic objects
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})\subset\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
is closed under coproducts by the definition.
By
[
11
, Lemma 1.5]
or
[
31
, Lemma 3.2.10]
,
it follows that coproducts exist in the quotient category

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})=\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})/{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
, and the Verdier quotient
functor
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
preserves coproducts.

Furthermore, following the proof of the “
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}
reflects
smallness” assertion in
Lemma
9.17
, one can see
that the assumption that all the objects of
\mathsf{L}
are small in
\mathsf{K}

implies that all the objects of
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
are small in
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Hence, by Lemma
9.18
,
all objects of
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
are compact in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.

Let us show that all the objects of the full subcategory
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})

are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
The argument follows the proof of
Theorem
7.9
with suitable
variations.
By Proposition
7.10
(a), all
the objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
are approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})

via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
, hence also via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
.
By Lemma
7.5
, the class of all
objects approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
is
a full triangulated subcategory in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
Finally, according to
Lemma
9.19
, the class
of all objects approachable from
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
via
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
is
closed under coproducts in
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
.
We can conclude that all the objects of
{\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
belong to
this class.

Thus Lemma
9.20
is applicable
to
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{E})
,
\mathsf{S}=\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{F})
,
\mathsf{X}={\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{E})
,
and
\mathsf{Y}={\mathsf{Ac}}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{F})
, providing both the full-and-faithfulness
and compactness assertions of the theorem.
∎

9.8.
Compact generation: Noetherian case

Let
\mathbf{A}
be an abelian DG-category.
Recall the notation
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{inj}}\subset\mathbf{A}
for the full DG-subcategory
formed by all the graded-injective objects, i. e., such objects

J\in\mathbf{A}
that the object
\Phi_{\mathbf{A}}(J)
is injective in the abelian
category
\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})
(see
Section
5.2
).

In this section we consider a locally Noetherian (Grothendieck
abelian) DG-category
\mathbf{A}
.
The following proposition is a particular case of
Theorem
5.10
(a).

Proposition 9.22
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a locally Noetherian DG-category.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{inj}})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})

induced by the inclusion of exact/abelian DG-categories
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{inj}}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{A}
is an equivalence of triangulated categories.

Proof.

It suffices to check that the assumptions of
Theorem
5.10
(a)
are satisfied for the DG-category
\mathbf{A}
with its abelian exact
DG-category structure.
Any Grothendieck abelian category has exact coproducts, hence
any Grothendieck abelian DG-category also has exact coproducts
(cf. Lemma
9.2
).
Moreover, any Grothendieck abelian category has enough injective
objects.
Furthermore, any abelian DG-category has twists by
Proposition
4.35
.
Finally, coproducts of injective objects are injective in any
locally Noetherian Grothendieck category; so condition (
*
)
is trivially satisfied for locally Noetherian categories.
∎

The following theorem, provable by an argument due to Arinkin,
is our generalization of
[
35
, Theorem 3.11.2]

and
[
18
, Proposition 1.5(d)]
.

Theorem 9.23
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a locally Noetherian DG-category and

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}\subset\mathbf{A}
be its full DG-subcategory of finitely
generated (Noetherian) objects.
Then the triangulated functor

\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{abs}}(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}})\mskip 1.5mu\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mskip 1.5mu\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})

induced by the inclusion of abelian DG-categories

\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{A}
is fully faithful, and (representatives of
the isomorphism classes of all) the objects in its image form a set of
compact generators of the triangulated category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

To prove the full-and-faithfulness of the triangulated functor
in question and compactness of all the objects in its essential image,
we apply
Theorem
9.21
.
Consider the exact DG-pair
(\mathbf{A},\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural}))
and its exact
DG-subpair
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}},\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural}_{\mathbf{fg}}))
(recall that

\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural}_{\mathbf{fg}})=\mathsf{Z}^{0}(\mathbf{A}^{\natural})_{\mathsf{fg}}
, as per the discussion in
Section
9.4
).
Recall further that all finitely generated objects are small, and
observe that the full subcategory of Noetherian objects is
self-resolvable in any locally Noetherian category.

In order to prove that the Noetherian objects generate the coderived
category
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})
, we use the result of
Proposition
9.22
, telling that
any object of
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})
can be represented by a graded-injective
object of
\mathbf{A}
.
Furthermore, the semiorthogonality assertion of
Theorem
5.5
(a) implies
that the Verdier quotient functor
\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})\longrightarrow\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})

induces an isomorphism of the Hom groups
\Hom_{\mathsf{H}^{0}(\mathbf{A})}(A,J)\simeq\Hom_{\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{co}}(\mathbf{A})}(A,J)
for any objects
A\in\mathbf{A}
and

J\in\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{inj}}
.
It remains to use the following lemma, going back to Arinkin, in
order to finish the proof of the theorem.
∎

Lemma 9.24
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a locally Noetherian DG-category and
J\in\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{inj}}

be a graded-injective object.
Assume that all closed morphisms
E\longrightarrow J
from Noetherian objects

E\in\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{fg}}
to
J
are homotopic to zero in
\mathbf{A}
.
Then the object
J
is contractible.

Proof.

Consider the following poset
P
.
The elements of
P
are pairs
(B,t_{B})
, where
B\subset J
is
a subobject of the object
J
